<meta>This is the title page of a 1551 edition. It introduces the work of Giovanni Braccesco, who interprets the writings of the famous alchemist Geber.</meta>

# THE EXPOSITION
## OF GEBER THE PHILO-
### SOPHER BY MASTER GIO-
*vanni Braccesco of Orzinuovi,* <note>Orzinuovi (historically Iorci novi) is a town in Lombardy, Italy.</note>
**IN WHICH ARE DECLARED**
*many most noble secrets of nature.* <note>In the 16th century, "secrets of nature" typically referred to alchemical processes and the hidden properties of minerals and plants.</note>

<image-desc>Small decorative woodcut ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

**WITH PRIVILEGE FROM THE SUPREME**
**Pontiff Paul III and the Most Illustrious Venetian Senate, for ten years.** <note>A "privilege" was an early form of copyright protection, granting the printer exclusive rights to publish the work for a set period.</note>

->ETERNAL<-

<image-desc>Large woodcut printer's device of Gabriel Giolito de' Ferrari. A phoenix or eagle with spread wings stands upon a heart-shaped globe or vessel which is inscribed with the letters 'G', 'G', and 'F'. The globe rests upon a small anchor. A ribbon banner entwined around the bird's wings and the globe bears the motto "Always the same" <note>original Latin: "SEM-PER E-ADEM"</note>. To the left of the bird, the words "FROM MY DEATH" <note>original Italian: "DE LA MIA MORTE"</note> are printed vertically; to the right, the words "I LIVE, I LIVE" <note>original Italian: "V I V A V I V A"</note> are printed vertically.</image-desc>

**IN VENICE BY GABRIEL**
**GIOLITO DE' FERRARI**
**AND BROTHERS,**
**1 5 5 1.**

<summary>This is the title page of Giovanni Braccesco’s commentary on the works of Geber, a seminal figure in alchemy, printed in Venice in 1551. It highlights the book's legal protections (privileges) and features the elaborate printer's mark of the Giolito press.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1551 edition of Giovanni Braccesco’s "Exposition of Geber." It begins the formal dedication to the author's patron, Count Bartholomeo Martinengo.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS
## LORD, THE LORD COUN-
### te Bartholomeo Martinengo,
### of Villa Chiara. <note>Villachiara is a town in the province of Brescia, Italy; the Martinengo family was a prominent noble house in that region.</note>

---

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'L' depicting a man in 16th-century clothing standing in a landscape and using a large pair of compasses to measure the ground.</image-desc>

*The science of minerals* <term>science of minerals: In the 16th century, this specifically referred to metallurgy and the alchemical study of metals and their transformations.</term>
*by the ancient philoso-*
*phers out of reverence cal-*
*led secret philoso-* <note>original Italian: "secreta philosophia"</note>
*phy, Most Illustrious Lo-*
*rd Count, has today reached such a point,*
*through the ignorant greed of men,*
*that, having fled from human minds,*
*it has left us nothing of itself but dark-*
*ness and vanity; but provident nature, which*
*never ceases in her care for preservation,*
*creates in various ages (whenever*
*she sees the need) some pure intel-*
*lect, by whom the precious treasures of the*
*noble sciences, already lost either by the en-*
*vy of time or the wrath of higher causes,* <note>The "wrath of higher causes" likely refers to astrological influences or divine will, which were believed to affect the rise and fall of civilizations and their knowledge.</note>

<summary>This page is the opening of the dedication to Count Bartholomeo Martinengo. Braccesco laments that alchemy (the "science of minerals") has been corrupted by human greed and lost to time, but suggests that nature periodically provides brilliant minds to recover this lost wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Bartholomeo Martinengo, Villachiara, alchemy, science of minerals, secret philosophy, greed, nature, recovery of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Count Bartholomeo Martinengo from the previous page, where Braccesco discussed nature's role in recovering lost scientific knowledge.</meta>

...might be found again and come to light, so that the world, which had become naked and poor through the loss of them, may be adorned and enlightened by them. I—not as one who convinces himself that he is such a person <note>Referring to the "pure intellects" mentioned on the previous page whom nature sends to recover lost knowledge.</note>, but as a loving professor of this divine science <term>divine science: A common Renaissance term for alchemy, suggesting it is a sacred pursuit that reveals the hidden workings of God's creation.</term>—it has seemed to me a thing worthy of my labors and a pious duty toward those who walk in darkness following vain shadows, to show them that little bit of light which, by the grace of the most generous God, has been infused into me. 

Nor do I believe that I can be reproached in any way for this loving thought of mine, unless my critics also have such presumptuous audacity as to tear apart <note>In this context, to "tear apart" means to harshly criticize or devalue the authority of these figures.</note> Hermes, Plato, Aristotle <note>The author invokes these classical names to provide ancient, "respectable" pedigree to alchemical study.</note>, and many other great philosophers who have written about this most noble part of philosophy—and better and more learnedly than all of them, Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), an Islamic polymath whose writings formed the foundation of medieval and Renaissance alchemy.</note>, from whose most obscure writings, with...

<sig>A ii</sig> <note>This is a signature mark used by 16th-century printers to keep the folded sheets of the book in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>Braccesco humbles himself as a student of alchemy ("divine science") rather than a master, stating his intent to help those lost in "shadows" by interpreting the difficult works of Geber and other classical philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>divine science, alchemy, Hermes, Plato, Aristotle, Geber, Jabir ibn Hayyan, philosophy, illumination</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Count Bartholomeo Martinengo, following the previous page's discussion of the ancient foundations of alchemy and the works of Geber.</meta>

...through very long study and continuous labors, I have discovered that little bit of light which, under the protection <note>original: "ombra" (shadow), a common metaphor for the shelter or patronage of a high-ranking noble.</note> of Your Most Illustrious Lordship, today arises from within the darkness of this most hidden science <term>most hidden science: Alchemy, referred to as "hidden" or "occult" because its secrets were traditionally kept from the uninitiated through symbols, allegory, and coded language.</term>. The reasons that have moved me to dedicate these writings of mine (whatever their worth may be) to Your Most Illustrious Lordship are so many and of such a nature that they await a more worthy place and occasion to be recounted in full. For now, let it suffice that Your Most Illustrious Lordship is such that I know myself to be your most devoted servant; and I pray that you deign to accept with a joyful countenance this small testimony of my faithful service.

->Giovanni Braccesco.<-

<summary>Giovanni Braccesco concludes his dedication to his patron, presenting his alchemical research as a small "light" emerged from long study and requesting the Count's favorable reception of the work.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Braccesco, alchemy, patronage, dedication, occult science, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

# THE INDEX OF THE
## EXPOSITION OF GEBER.

Geber has spoken most profoundly. 8
With a cheap price <note>original: "prezzo uile"; alchemists often claimed the "First Matter" was a common, inexpensive substance found everywhere, though overlooked by the ignorant.</note> one arrives at the end of this work. 8
The principles of nature are also the principles of this mastery <term>mastery: from the Latin "magisterium," referring to the successful completion of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone</term>. 8
He who does not have a soul subtly investigating the natural principles and foundations of nature will not find the root of this most precious science. 9
The natural principles of metals are of three kinds, namely: most remote, remote (or intermediate minerals), and immediate. 9
All metals are generated from vitriol <note>original: "uitreolo"; a term for metallic sulfates, often considered a key starting material in the "Great Work."</note> and sulfur. 9
Common quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> and sulfur are not the principles of metals. 10
"First matter" <term>materia prima: the original, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</term> in this art means matter close to the nature of metals. 10
Vitriol is close to the nature of metals, because it is sulfurous, and has within itself the mineral virtue to transform into metal. 10
The mineral virtue of generating metals is in washed sulfur, without which metals could never be generated. 10
Only those things which have the nature of salts or alums are soluble. 11
From dissolved vitriol, a double smoke is released, and these two smokes are named by the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"; referring to the ancient alchemical authorities such as Hermes Trismegistus or Aristotle.</note> as sulfur and quicksilver. 11
The sulfurs within metals take on the proximate virtue of turning into metal. 11
From imperfect metals, various vitriols necessary to us are extracted. 10. 11
Prepared sulfurs are an excellent thing for converting their quicksilver into gold or silver. 12
If that vaporous exaltation should fall within a certain red earth, it would be converted into common quicksilver. 12
If that double smoke, penetrating through the yew <note>original: "tasso"; while usually meaning the yew tree or a badger, in this technical context it may refer to a specific vessel or a porous material used in distillation.</note>, finds that washed sulfur, it dissolves it and joins with it in the smallest parts, and by successive de-

...by cooking in the mine it is converted into metal. 12 13
All metals are generated from one and the same matter, and the diversity among them proceeds from the diversity of the mineral site, from various accidents <term>accidents: in alchemical philosophy, these are non-essential external qualities—like impurities or color—that can be changed without altering the inner essence</term>, from the diversity of that washed sulfur, and from different methods of digestion <term>digestion: a process of slow, consistent heating used to mature or "ripen" a substance</term>. 13
All the accidents that have occurred to the radical matter can be removed. 13
If we wish to make the Elixir, which is a metal more than perfect, it is necessary to make it with nature’s own seed. 13
The elements of the Philosopher’s Stone <term>Philosopher's Stone: the legendary substance capable of perfecting base metals into gold and grant long life</term> have acquired a nearby nature for being converted into metal, a quality which does not belong to the elements of other things. 13
Common vitriol is a remote principle to the art. 14
The principles of the art are found neither in vegetables, nor in animals, nor in anything descending from them, because they are foreign to the nature of metals. 14
Common sulfur and common quicksilver <note>original: "ar. ui."; abbreviation for "argento vivo" or mercury</note> are not the principles of nature, and therefore they cannot be the principles of the art. 14
Sulfur alone cannot generate metals, nor the Elixir. 15
He who knows how to prepare sulfur in such a way that it joins with metals will know one of the greatest secrets of nature, and a short way to perfection. 15
The Elixir can be made in two ways, namely by calcination <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> and solution, and without calcination and distillation. 15
Quicksilver joined with its own sulfurs can be coagulated and fixed. 15 16 17
Quicksilver cannot be fixed with herbs alone. 15
Our quicksilver is a salt which resembles true lime <note>original: "calce"; likely referring to a caustic or powdery mineral residue</note>. 17
The quicksilver of metals is converted into salt by calcination. 17
In metallic bodies there are two sulfurous qualities. 17
Our arsenic partakes of sulfur and quicksilver and is called Hermaphrodite <note>original: "Hermafrodito"; a term used for arsenic because it shares properties of both metals and non-metals</note>; nonetheless, on its own it cannot generate metals or the Elixir. 18
Tutty <note>original: "tutia"; a zinc-based byproduct of smelting</note> is the white smoke of our Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal tin</term>, therefore it yellows our copper. 19
Sulfur and quicksilver are fixed...

Sulfur with its salt and vinegar is calcined <term>calcined: reduced to a powder through heat</term> and washed. 21

Our <term>Jupiter: alchemical code for Tin</term> and <term>Saturn: alchemical code for Lead</term> are a salt and are together before distillation. 21.31

The vessels for <term>putrefaction: the stage where the substance is allowed to decay or decompose to be reborn</term> and for the preparation of sulfur are similar, that is, flat on the bottom. 22

The Philosopher’s stone has within itself all natural preparation, and all that it needs for its perfection. 25

The Elixir, <term>potable gold: gold refined into a liquid form for medicinal use</term>, the <term>quintessence: the purest essence or "fifth element"</term> and gems are made from one and the same matter. 25

The opinion of some that the Elixir should be made from common gold. 26

The Elixir cannot be made from common gold because its sulfur has reached the final permanent <term>tincture: the power of a substance to impart color or change the nature of another</term>, and because it has no tincture except as much as it needs, and because it is complete and determined, and because it cannot be properly reduced to its <term>prime matter: the original, formless substance from which all things are made</term>. 27.28

The <term>radical moisture: the fundamental, vital liquid essential to the life and structure of a substance</term> of other metals is as virtuous, incorruptible, and incombustible as gold. 29

In the Philosopher’s stone, the <term>Sun: gold</term> and the <term>Moon: silver</term> <note>original: "Sole & Luna"</note> are present in virtue and power. 29

Regarding the perfect bodies, Geber...

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the index (Tavola), providing further symbolic interpretations of classical mythology as a veil for alchemical processes. It connects figures like Argus, Orpheus, and Jove to specific chemical transformations such as distillation, fixation, and the "peacock's tail" color change.</meta>

# TABLE

By the eyes of Argus turned into a peacock’s tail, they have signified our sulfur, which changes from color to color. <note>In alchemy, the "Peacock's Tail" (cauda pavonis) is a stage where the matter in the flask displays a shimmering array of colors, signaling that the transformation is progressing correctly.</note> 35

Under the fable of Orpheus, they have hidden the sweetness of the quintessence and of potable gold. <note>Potable gold (aurum potabile) was a legendary medicinal oil of gold believed to grant longevity.</note> 36

According to the opinion of Empedocles, under the fable of Pyrrha and Deucalion, they have hidden the matter and the practice of this art. <note>In the myth, Pyrrha and Deucalion repopulated the earth by throwing stones behind them; alchemists saw this as a metaphor for transforming "stones" into a new "life" or substance.</note> 42

The fixation of the Elixir they have hidden by saying that the Gorgon turned all those who looked at her into stone. <note>Fixation is the process of making a volatile substance stable and solid, much like Medusa’s gaze turning living things to stone.</note> 43

They have hidden distillation by saying that Jove <note>original: "Gioue"; Jove is another name for Jupiter.</note> turned himself into an eagle and carried Ganymede into Heaven. 43

In the fable of Daedalus and Icarus, they have hidden putrefaction and distillation. <note>The flight of Daedalus and the fall of Icarus represent the rising of vapors (distillation) and their subsequent condensation or "death" (putrefaction).</note> 43. 44

The distillation of the gold of the Philosophers they have hidden by saying that once the first golden branch was plucked, another similar golden one grew. 44

This distillation they have hidden by saying that Jove cut off the masculine parts of his father Saturn. 44

<column-break/>

The mercurial water is the chariot of Phaethon. <note>Phaethon's wild ride in the sun-chariot represents the difficulty of controlling the volatile "mercurial" energy during heating.</note> 44

By armed Minerva is signified this distilled water in which is the most subtle part of that sulfur called <term>iron: traditionally associated with the planet Mars</term>. 45

By Vulcan, who follows Minerva, is signified the sulfur which follows this water and its salt in putrefaction. 45

By the thick cloud with which Jove surrounded Io, is signified the film which appears in the coagulation of the Elixir. 45

The black films which appear in the calcination of sulfur are the black sails with which Theseus returned to Athens. <note>This refers to the "Nigredo" or blackening stage, the first major phase of the alchemical work representing death and decomposition.</note> 45

Under the name of the flood and the generation of animals, they have described the distillation and the generation of the sulfurs. 45

By Mars they have signified our sulfur, and by Juno, the element of air, and sometimes the element of earth. 46

By Latona confined on the Island of Delos is signified our Copper which, placed... <note>Latona, the mother of Apollo and Diana, often represents the raw material that gives birth to the "solar" and "lunar" principles of the work.</note>

<summary>This page functions as a mythological-alchemical key, explaining how Greco-Roman myths (such as Medusa, Argus, and Theseus) are actually coded instructions for chemical stages like distillation and fixation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical or thematic index (Tavola) of an alchemical treatise. -># TABLE<-

<note>This entry continues from the previous page's discussion of a specific alchemical process or myth.</note> ...in the flask generates the **Sun** and the **Moon**. <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Sun and Moon represent Gold and Silver, or the masculine and feminine principles (Sulfur and Mercury) that must be joined.</note> 47

They have hidden the preparation of **sulfur**, saying that **Vulcan**, because he was ugly, was cast onto the **Island of Lemnos**. <note>Vulcan was the god of fire and smithing; his "ugliness" and fall represent the harsh, transformative power of fire in treating mineral sulfur.</note> 47

**Atalanta**—that is, our swiftest and lightest water—is stopped and coagulated by means of sulfurs. <note>In myth, the swift hunter Atalanta was slowed down by golden apples; here, she represents volatile Mercury being "fixed" or solidified by Sulfur.</note> 47

The **sulfurs** are the balls with which **Theseus** gummed up the mouth of the **Minotaur** in the labyrinth—that is, our mercurial water in the flask. <note>The labyrinth represents the complex internal structure of the alchemical vessel (the "boccia").</note> 47

By the **Phoenix**, which is always renewed, they have meant the **multiplication** of the **Elixir**. <note>Multiplication is the stage where the power and quantity of the alchemical medicine are increased exponentially.</note> 54

In the name and the fable of **Demogorgon**, they have hidden the matter and the practice of this art. <note>Demogorgon was viewed by Renaissance mythographers as the primordial creator god; alchemists used this to represent the "First Matter" of the Great Work.</note> 71. 72

The **chaos** is our **Saturn**. <note>original: "Satur."; Saturn usually refers to Lead, but "our Saturn" often refers to the raw, unrefined starting material or "Chaos."</note> 72

The **Elixir** cannot be made from **silver**. 48

The **Elixir** is made from **imperfect metals**. <note>Imperfect metals include lead, tin, copper, and iron, which the alchemist seeks to "perfect" into gold.</note> 49

The **imperfect metals** are intermediate minerals. 50

The opinion of some that the **Elixir** may be made from **common lead**. 51

The opinion of some that the **Elixir** may be made from **common tin**. 51

<column-break/>

The **Elixir** cannot be made from common **Lead** nor common **tin**, because they are unclean at their root. 52

The first order is **preparation**; the second, **fixation**; and the third, the **multiplication** of the **Elixir**. <note>Fixation is the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to fire.</note> 53

From the burnt dregs, our **mercury** is extracted, with which the multiplication is performed. 54

Common **Copper** and **Iron**, at their root, are pure and clean. 55

The **Elixir** must not be made from common **Copper**. 56

The ore of our **Copper** is the salt called **Saturn**. <note>original: "Satur."; this likely refers to "Salt of Saturn" or Lead Acetate, a chemical reagent.</note> 56

The opinion that the **Elixir** cannot be made from common **iron**. 57

Through the pronouncements of **Geber** and other philosophers, it is proven that the greatest secret and the **elixir** are extracted from common **iron**. <note>Geber refers to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous figure in Arabic alchemy, whose Latinized works were foundational to European practice.</note> 57. 58

Through the arguments of **Geber** and other philosophers, it is proven that the **elixir** is made from common **iron**. 59. and following.

The vessel for the **distillation** of **mercury** must be like a flat table or have very little concavity. 61

The **Elixir** is made from common iron, because it has more **fixed sulfur** <note>Fixed sulfur refers to the non-volatile component of a metal that resists being driven off by heat.</note> than other metals, and because its **spirits** are more powerful, and because its "earths" <note>Impurities or solid residues.</note> are more easily separated, and it is more easily reduced to the **prime matter**, and because its prepared **mercury** better protects its **sulfur** from burning, and because it is incomplete and is a "semi-mineral" in which the extremes are present in power, and because it is the proper mine of the **philosophers' mercury**. <note>original: "ar. ui" (argento vivo). Mercury was often called "living silver."</note> 59 and following.

The ancients, through various enigmas and metaphors, have indicated that the **Elixir** is extracted from **iron**. 64, 65

Iron is called "man" because it possesses a soul, a body, and a spirit. 65

Iron is generated in the earth specifically through the power of the **North Star**. <note>Alchemists believed celestial bodies influenced the growth of specific metals within the earth; the North Star (Stella Tramontana) was linked to the magnetic and steadfast nature of iron.</note> 65

Iron is that stone which is seen at a cheap price compared to other metals. 65

Iron is called "vegetable." <note>In alchemy, "vegetable" refers to the ability of a substance to grow, multiply, or possess a life-force, rather than referring to a plant.</note> 65

The **Elixir** is made from a most vile stone. 65, 66

The most vile stone of the philosophers is vegetable, animal, and mineral. 66

Concerning the separation of the **elements**. 66

<column-break/>

The **slag** <note>original: "loppa"</note>, or rather the scum and **calx** <note>A powder produced by heating a metal; an oxide.</note> of iron, which is thrown into the streets by blacksmiths, is the "most vile stone" of the philosophers. 67

The **radical moisture** <note>The essential, life-sustaining fluid within matter that was believed to be the source of its permanence and resistance to fire.</note> of metals does not burn in the fire. 67

Through the teachings of **Geber**, it is proven that we must sublimate the **spirits** from the **calces** <note>Plural of calx.</note> of iron. <note>Geber refers to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the legendary 8th-century Islamic alchemist whose works formed the foundation of European alchemy.</note> 67, 68

The **calx** of iron which, through the intensity of the fire, is converted into a state of **glass** colored almost green, or dark sky-blue, or dark sapphire, is the most powerful stone of the philosophers.

That **glass** is the **radical moisture** of iron. 68, 69

Through the teachings of the philosophers, it is proven that the "vile stone" of the philosophers does not burn in the fire. 69, 70

Iron is cleansed of much "earth" <note>Solid impurities.</note> when it is reduced to the state of **glass**. 70

The ancients created **metallic glass**. 70

**Glass** is the "greatest vile stone," famously discovered. 70

# THE TABLE OF THE WOOD OF LIFE.

Adam knew the things which were able to prolong human life; he used them for himself and afterwards taught them to some others. 75

Preservative medicines must be very durable. 75

Wishing to preserve youth in the young and to restore the <term>radical moisture</term><note>The "radical moisture" (humido radicale) was believed to be the innate life-fluid of a person; aging was viewed as the gradual drying out of this moisture.</note> and natural heat in the old, we must choose the most incorruptible and most virtuous substance that exists under the lunar globe. 75

Elemental things are composed not only of elements, but of <term>quintessence</term><note>The "fifth essence," a pure substance distinct from the four physical elements (earth, water, air, fire), believed to hold the life-force of a material.</note>, which for a long time preserves things in their being. 76

By using this quintessence, man could reach nearly the age of the ancient fathers. <note>A reference to the long-lived Patriarchs of the Old Testament, such as Methuselah.</note> 76

This is the best medicine that exists in the whole world. 77

This is that medicine with which <term>Asclepius</term><note>The Greek god of medicine and healing.</note> raised those who were nearly dead. 77

<column-break/>

This medicine could not be made from vegetable things, nor from animals. 77

This medicine cannot be made from semi-minerals. <note>original: "mezi minerali." These were substances like salts, vitriols, or sulfur that were mineral in nature but not fully formed metals.</note> 78

Fire does not corrupt the radical moisture of metals. 78

The heavens have infused more and more wonderful virtues into the <term>prime matter</term><note>The "prima materia"—the original, undifferentiated substance from which all metals grow in the earth.</note> of metals than in any other thing, because they have worked more upon it. 79

Metals reduced to their first simplicity and sufficiently <term>digested</term><note>An alchemical term for the process of heating a substance at a steady, gentle temperature to mature or purify it.</note>, almost immediately restore human bodies to true health. 79

This medicine cannot be made from precious stones. 80

Our medicine extracted from metals, sufficiently <term>decocted</term><note>Boiled or refined through heat to extract its essence.</note>, acquires wonderful smoothness and sweetness. 81

This medicine has the power to heal all curable infirmities. 82

# DIALOGUE OF
### MASTER GIOVANNI BRACCESCO.
->from Orzinuovi, in explanation of<-
->Geber the Philosopher.<-

->DEMOGORGON AND GEBER.<-

<margin>
Dem.

Geb.

Dem.
</margin>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring two figures in a landscape; one appears to be seated or reclining while the other stands or moves toward them.</image-desc>MAY GOD save you, most wise grandson of the great Muhammad. <note>original: "Maumetho." In Renaissance alchemical tradition, Geber was often erroneously described as a descendant of the Prophet Muhammad to emphasize his status as a source of Eastern wisdom.</note>
*You are welcome; what cause has induced you to come to this place?*
The natural appetite, which greatly troubles me with the desire to understand the deep secrets of nature, has forced me for twenty-five years to study your works with all diligence; but because you have spoken most profoundly, for all this time I have not been able to penetrate the true sense, although many times I persuaded myself that I possessed such a secret. For this reason, therefore, I have searched various parts of the world, hoping to find someone so wise who could give me knowledge of this, your so divine and most secret part of philosophy. And although I have found almost infinite men who rashly affirm that they certainly know your intention, nonethele- <note>The text ends mid-word with "nientediman-", meaning "nevertheless."</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a philosophical dialogue where the character Demogorgon approaches the legendary alchemist Geber. Demogorgon explains his twenty-five-year struggle to understand Geber's cryptic writings on the secrets of nature.</summary>

<meta>The text follows from the previous page where Demogorgon was explaining that although many claim to understand Geber’s writings, they often fail to grasp their true meaning. The catchword from the previous page, "nientediman-," is completed here as "nientedimanco" (nevertheless).</meta>

# THE EXPLANATION

—theless, after much *labor and expense of theirs*, they have complained about you. But because I have always revered and loved you, I have always defended your honor against your detractors. And because you are the <term>Master of Masters</term> <note>A title of supreme respect in the alchemical tradition, emphasizing Geber's status as the ultimate authority on the craft.</note>, I greatly desired to see your reverend presence and make myself subject to it, if you will deign to accept me into the number of your disciples. For this reason, I have departed from Italy, not caring about the very great dangers which occurred to me during the long journey, and have come here to Persia into your sight, to learn from you this most profound <term>science</term> <note>In the 16th century, 'science' (scientia) referred to a structured body of knowledge, here specifically the 'art' of alchemy.</note>, if it please you to teach it to me.

<margin>Geb.</margin> Because your petition is honest and this desire of yours proceeds from a generous spirit—and also because of the love you bear for me—I am content to accept you into the company of my other disciples; and it would be pleasing to me to understand the reason why those ignorant ones complain about me.

<margin>Dem.</margin> Many times I have contended with some malicious ones who said that you have deceived them, and that in the preface of your <term>Summa</term> <note>Likely the *Summa perfectionis magisterii* (The Sum of Perfection), the most influential alchemical text in medieval Europe, attributed to Geber.</note> you said: "By God, whoever has operated according to this book will rejoice to have found the end of this art." They say they have operated according to what your book teaches, and nevertheless they have never done anything good; on the contrary, they have lost their time, their wealth, and—which weighs on them more—their honor, and cannot converse among other men— <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "huo-," meaning "men" (huomini).</note>

<summary>In this opening dialogue, Demogorgon arrives in Persia to seek tutelage under the master Geber, defending Geber's reputation against those who claim his alchemical writings are deceptive and lead to financial ruin.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Demogorgon, Persia, alchemical education, Summa perfectionis, defense of alchemy, discipleship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Geber and Demogorgone (often representing Democritus). Demogorgone is relaying the criticisms of frustrated alchemists who find Geber’s writings intentionally obscure. Geber responds by defending the "sacred" nature of alchemical knowledge, asserting it is reserved for those chosen by God.</meta>

### OF GEBER.

...men, because they are <note>original: "mostrati a dito"; literally "pointed at with a finger," meaning to be singled out for public shame or mockery.</note> singled out for mockery. For this reason, therefore, they blaspheme and curse you as a false and deceitful man, who delights in <note>original: "far beccare il ceruello"; an idiom meaning to drive someone to distraction or to confuse their mind entirely.</note> confusing the minds of every person, of whatever rank and dignity they may be.

**Geb.** Do you not know that there was never a man so divine and wise that the wicked and malicious did not find cause for <term>scandal</term> <note>In the 16th-century sense, "scandal" (scandalo) refers to a stumbling block or an offense against one's beliefs or expectations.</note>?

**Dem.** *What will you say of many very wise men, of almost irreproachable life, who have been caught in common errors?*

**Geb.** I have written in the fifth chapter of the first book that this <term>Art</term> <note>Alchemy was often called "the Art" (l'Arte), implying both a technical craft and a divine inspiration.</note> is kept in the power of God, who gives it to whom He wills, and takes it from whom He pleases. And at the end of the <term>Summa</term> <note>The *Summa perfectionis magisterii* (The Sum of Perfection), the most influential alchemical text of the Middle Ages, attributed to Geber.</note> I have said that I have written this doctrine in a way of speaking that only God can understand—or he into whom God pours His grace, or I who wrote it.

**Dem.** *In that case, then, it was not necessary to write or publish such science if there is no one who can understand it.*

**Geb.** At the end of the fourth book, I said that I have written this divine science in such a manner that only the <term>craftsman</term> <note>The 'artificer' (artefice), referring to the practical operator in the laboratory who has sufficient experience.</note> and the <term>Sons of Doctrine</term> <note>A traditional term for initiated students or those deemed spiritually and intellectually worthy of alchemical secrets.</note> can understand it. To those, I have spoken clearly; but to the others, I have told them to flee from this science, because it is an enemy and contrary to them, and it leads them into misery and poverty...

<summary>In this dialogue, Geber defends the intentional obscurity of his alchemical writings, arguing that the "divine science" is a gift from God intended only for a worthy elite of "Sons of Doctrine." He dismisses the complaints of failed alchemists, suggesting their failure is a result of their own unworthiness rather than his deception.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Demogorgone, Summa perfectionis, Sons of Doctrine, alchemical secrecy, divine grace, pedagogical style, elitism in science</keywords>

-># THE EXPOSITION<-

...it would. They blame themselves and their own presumption, because I have warned them many times not to take the literal word of this <term>science</term> <note>Alchemy was frequently referred to as "the Science" or "the Sacred Science."</note>, but to penetrate deeply to the marrow of the allegorical sense, and to grasp my intention, which I have artfully veiled from the wicked; and where it appears that I have spoken most clearly, there I have spoken most obscurely. And if they have become poor, they have not put faith in my words, for in the first book at the fifth chapter, I said that it was not necessary for them to consume their goods or spend their money, because one reaches the end of this work with a <note>original: "prezzo uile"; literally a "vile" or cheap price. This refers to the alchemical belief that the *materia prima* (prime matter) is a common, inexpensive substance found everywhere.</note> cheap price, especially when the craftsman has true knowledge of the principles of this art.

**Dem.** Reading your books, I have understood that the principles of nature are also the principles of the art. For in the first book at the ninth chapter you said that, having spoken of the principles which are of nature’s intention, you would also speak of those insofar as they are principles of this our <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterio"; referring to the *Magisterium*, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. And particularly speaking of the natural principles of metals in the first book, at the twelfth chapter, you said: "The natural principles in the work of nature are the <note>original: "spirito fetente"; literally "fetid spirit," a common term for sulfur due to its smell.</note> fetid spirit and the <note>original: "acqua uiua"; "living water," usually identifying mercury or a specialized solvent.</note> living water, which we grant is named <note>original: "acqua secca"; "dry water," an alchemical paradox for mercury, which is a liquid that does not wet the hands.</note> dry water." You have written these same words when speaking of the principles of the art in the *Book of the Investigation* at the end, saying: "Let us conclude our stone..."

<summary>Geber explains that his works are written in allegory to protect them from the unworthy and that the true Work requires little money. Demogorgone quotes Geber's definitions of natural principles, specifically sulfur and mercury, as the foundation of the Art.</summary>

# OF GEBER.
B
<meta>catchword: Of ear-</meta>

...is nothing other than a <term>stinking spirit</term> <note>original: "spirito fetente"; in alchemical theory, this usually refers to sulfur because of its pungent odor and its role as a "spiritual" or volatile principle.</note> and living water <note>original: "acqua uiua"; a common name for quicksilver or mercury.</note>, which we have also called <term>dry water</term> <note>original: "acqua secca"; an alchemical paradox used to describe mercury, which is a liquid that does not wet surfaces like normal water.</note>. If, therefore, the natural principles of metals are also the principles of this art, give me knowledge of the natural principles, so that in this way I may have knowledge of the principles of this <term>mastery</term> <note>The "Magisterium" or the process of achieving the Great Work.</note>.

<margin>Geb.</margin> This is required by the order of instruction; therefore, in the preface of the first book I said: He who does not know the natural principles will be remote and far from our art, because he has no true root upon which to found his intention. And in the 3rd chapter of the aforementioned book, I also said that he who does not have great natural genius, and a soul subtly investigating the natural principles and foundations of nature, will not find the root of this most precious science. You must therefore know that the natural principles of metals are of three kinds: some are most remote, some are remote (or mineral intermediaries), and some are immediate.

<margin>Dem.</margin> Declare to me in order all these principles, so that I may entirely grasp the whole natural order of the generation of metals, and so that through artifice I may in some way imitate nature.

<margin>Geb.</margin> The most remote principles and roots of metals, although (according to the opinion of <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note>) they are the four elements, nevertheless, because naming is done according to the predominant part, I say that earth mixed with water is the principal root and foundation of all mineral things. <term>Aristotle</term> in the 4th book of the <term>Meteorology</term> <note>original: "methaura"; referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*, which provided the scientific framework for mineral formation in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says: Of earth...

<vocab>Geber, Demogorgone, natural principles, metals, dry water, Hermes, four elements, Aristotle, Meteorology, minerals</vocab>

<summary>Geber explains to Demogorgone that a deep understanding of natural philosophy and the "roots" of metals—specifically the mixture of earth and water—is the only true foundation for alchemical success. He begins to categorize these principles into remote, intermediary, and immediate causes, citing authorities like Hermes and Aristotle.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, natural principles, four elements, sulfur-mercury theory, dry water, Aristotle's Meteorology, Hermes Trismegistus, alchemy, mineral generation</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Geber and Demogorgon. The previous page discussed the "most remote" principles of metals; this page identifies those principles as earth and water, citing classical and medieval authorities. The text begins mid-word ("ra") completing the word "terra" (earth) from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

# THE EXPLANATION

...[earth] and water are metals, and all things which are liquefied by fire. The same is also confirmed in the 3rd book of the <term>Metaphysics</term> <note>original: "methau."; referring to Aristotle’s *Metaphysics*, a foundational text for medieval science.</note>, and by <term>Avicenna</term> <note>A Persian physician and philosopher (Ibn Sina) whose works on chemistry and medicine were highly influential.</note>, and <term>Albertus Magnus</term> <note>A 13th-century scholar and saint known for his study of minerals.</note> in his writing on minerals, and <term>St. Thomas Aquinas</term> <note>A famous theologian who also studied the nature of physical substances under Albertus Magnus.</note> at the end of the 3rd book of the Metaphysics.

**Dem.** Tell me of the earth mixed with water: how are they transmuted and converted into metal?

**Geb.** *The sun and all the stars and planets, through the light received from the sun, continually heat the earth—below and above, inside and out—by their continuous circular motion and the reverberation of their rays, and through their most subtle and powerful penetrative virtue. And because everything burned and <term>decocted</term> <note>original: "decotta"; a process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or change its state.</note> finally <term>corrupts</term> and transmutes—and the corruption of one substance is the <term>generation</term> of another—therefore the earth mixed with water, decocted and transmuted over a long time, converts into another earthy nature, just as we see with wood and stones that are burned, which convert into ashes and lime.*

**Dem.** Into what kind of earth is the earth thus transmuted converted?

**Geb.** *It converts into a sort of earth which holds within itself some substance of salt or <term>alum</term> <note>A naturally occurring mineral salt often used in dyeing and medicine.</note>; just as we see through experience that ashes and lime, sweat, urine, spit, and sea water decocted by the sun, contain within themselves certain salts. And for this reason, I said at the beginning of the <term>Testament</term> <note>original: "testamẽto"; likely referring to the *Testamentum*, a famous alchemical text attributed to Geber.</note> that from every <term>combusted</term> <note>original: "cõbufta"; burned or reduced to ash by fire.</note> thing, salt can be made.*

**Dem.** What is the common name for this salt or alum which is hidden in that decocted earth?

**Geb.** *Commonly it is called <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "uitreolo"; a metallic sulfate, often green or blue, used as a primary ingredient in alchemical work.</note>.*

**Dem.** Oh! So from vitriol are metals generated?

**Geb.** *They are all generated from vitriol and <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: "solpho"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of matter.</note>.*

<summary>In this dialogue, Geber explains that metals are formed from earth and water through the constant heating and "decoction" provided by the sun and stars. He identifies vitriol and sulfur as the specific substances that emerge from this process to generate metals.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Geber, vitriol, sulfur, Aristotle, Avicenna, Albertus Magnus, St. Thomas Aquinas, transmutation, generation and corruption</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the legendary alchemist Geber and Demogorgon. Having previously established that earth and water are the fundamental elements of metals, Geber now defends this theory against the common alchemical belief that metals are made solely of sulfur and mercury.</meta>

->### ON GEBER<-

**Dem.** How shall I be certain of this?

**Geb.** Everything resolves into that which it is composed of; if you know how to resolve metals into their **prime matter** <note>The fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</note>, you will see that they resolve into **vitriol** <note>A historical name for sulfate minerals, often valued by alchemists for their corrosive and transformative properties.</note>, which I have called **dry water**. For this reason, I said in the *Book of Investigation* <note>original: "li. della inuesti."; referring to Geber's *Liber investigationis magisterii*, a key text in the alchemical corpus.</note> in the chapter on vitriol: "It is certain that from imperfect metals various vitriols necessary to us are extracted." I confirmed the same in the third book, chapter 7.

**Dem.** With your permission, it seems to me that in your words there is—

**Geb.** How so, a contradiction?

**Dem.** You have said in many places that **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "ar. uiuo" for *argento vivo* (mercury). The "Sulfur-Mercury theory" was the standard medieval explanation for the composition of metals.</note> are the natural principles of metals, and now you speak of vitriol. How does this work?

**Geb.** Common quicksilver and sulfur are not the principles of metals, as I have sufficiently proven in the first book, chapter 12. And if you understand my speech well, I have said that those are not natural principles in their own nature, but something else which follows from the alteration of their substances in the root of nature, toward the earthy substance.

**Dem.** I do not understand you.

**Geb.** Have I not told you that the root and foundation of all mineral things is **earth mixed with water**?

**Dem.** Continue.

**Geb.** Therefore, the alteration of these into the earthy substance does not produce the substance and essence of common sulfur and quicksilver, but rather the root of those things—that is, in the earth mixed with water—which are <term>decocted</term> <note>original: "dequocono"; a process of heating or boiling to change the nature of a substance.</note> and transmuted by mineral and celestial heat, as I have said.

**Dem.** Tell me now of the remote principles and mineral mediums...

->B ii<-

<meta>Catchword: Dem.</meta>

<summary>Geber explains that while sulfur and mercury are commonly cited as the components of metals, they are actually secondary products derived from a more fundamental mixture of earth and water. He uses the resolution of metals into vitriol (dry water) as proof of this underlying composition.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Demogorgon, prime matter, vitriol, dry water, sulfur, quicksilver, mercury, decoction, earth and water, mineral heat</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Geber and Demogorgon regarding the true nature of metals. Having previously argued that vitriol—which he calls "dry water"—is the true "prime matter" of metals, Geber now explains the specific mineral conditions and "powers" required for metals to form within the earth.</meta>

->### THE EXPOSITION<-

**Geb.** The mineral medium <note>A substance that acts as an intermediary or environment.</note> from which all metals are generated, as well as quicksilver <note>original: "ar.ui.", abbreviation for *argento vivo*.</note>, marcasite <note>A brittle mineral, often iron pyrite, which resembles gold or silver in luster.</note>, antimony, and many other minerals: it is primarily that hidden vitriol in that scorched earth. And when it is said that metals must be reduced to their **prime matter**—that is, the state closest to the metallic nature—it refers to the nature of vitriol and sulfur.

**Dem.** Are metals generated much more from that than from any other alum or salt?

**Geb.** Because that is more sulfurous and is closer to the nature of metals; and from the heavens, it possesses a more immediate mineral **virtue** <note>In alchemy, a "virtue" is an inherent power or celestial influence that gives a substance its specific properties.</note> to be converted into metal, by means of sulfur, the mineral site, and the sufficient color.

**Dem.** What is the mineral site?

**Geb.** It is the stone in which resides the mineral virtue that hardens and **fixes** <note>To "fix" a substance is to make it stable and resistant to fire, preventing it from evaporating.</note> metals.

**Dem.** What is this mineral virtue?

**Geb.** It is a celestial power for turning things into metal, hidden in a certain sulfur that has been washed and is as bright as silver <note>original: "arg.", abbreviation for *argento*.</note>.

**Dem.** I do not know this "washed sulfur."

**Geb.** Have you ever seen in the sand, or in the stones that are dug from underground, some very thin scales which shine like silver?

**Dem.** I believed those were the substance of silver.

**Geb.** That is a sulfur washed by nature, decocted <note>Refined, purified, or "cooked" by the natural heat of the earth.</note> and partially fixed. It shares in the nature, the brilliance, and the metallic fusion. And without this, metals could never be generated. And in every place where a good quantity of this is found, there metals could be generated...

<summary>Geber explains that metals are formed from a specific type of "washed sulfur" and vitriol influenced by celestial powers. He identifies the shimmering scales found in earth and stone not as silver itself, but as the essential, refined sulfur necessary for metallic growth.</summary>

->### OF GEBER<-
->B iii<-

metals <note>original: "talli". Completing the sentence from the previous page: "...metals (as Albert says in On Minerals)."</note> (*as Albertus Magnus says in his work On Minerals.*)

**Geb.** Vitriol is generated in the earth.

**Dem.** How then does one come to find that washed sulfur <note>Referring to the "silver-bright" sulfur mentioned on the previous page.</note> within the stone?

**Geb.** Mineral mountains are not made of stone at the bottom as they are on the surface; therefore, those who mine metals, when they find earth and alum—that is, vitriol—they mine no further, because there is the end of the vein <note>original: "minera", referring to the mineral deposit or the "mother" of the ore.</note> (*as Pliny affirms in Book 33, although sometimes other veins may be found nearby*). And because (*as I have said in the 2nd book, chapter 15*) we can prove through the works of nature that only those things which have the nature of salts and similar substances are soluble. Therefore, the aforementioned vitriol, by the power of subterranean heat, is dissolved, and from that solution <margin>+</margin> a double smoke <note>This refers to the "two vapors" (one moist, one dry) that Aristotle and later alchemists believed combined to create minerals and metals.</note> is released (*as I have said in Book 1, chapter 12*). For heat is always a mixing force, and most strongly unites and binds together the subtle earthy part and the humid watery part when they are digested together; and thus the celestial heat, drawing to itself the subtle watery humidity, also pulls the subtle earthy part with it. All of this is confirmed by Albertus Magnus in the 3rd book of his *Meteorology* <note>original: "methau.", an abbreviation for *Meteora* (Meteorology), the standard text for discussing the formation of vapors in the earth.</note> saying: "The enclosing humid vapor and the dry vapor enclosed within its belly are elevated together." And this happens, as Galen and Avicenna say, by the power of the heat that lifts both vapors, because heat always mixes and causes the essence of one to be moved into the essence of the other. These two smokes or vapors are named by the philosophers as sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "arg. ui.", abbreviation for *argento vivo* (mercury).</note>, because that earthy vapor...

<summary>Geber explains that vitriol acts as the foundation of metallic veins in the earth. Under the influence of subterranean and celestial heat, vitriol dissolves and releases two vapors (sulfur and quicksilver), which are the fundamental building blocks of all metals.</summary>

*...subtle, oily, and somewhat digested <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the slow heating of a substance to mature or purify it, similar to how food is processed in the stomach.</note> is the essential matter of sulfur, although I have named it arsenic; and that watery moisture, viscous and mixed with the subtle earthy part, is the proximate matter of quicksilver <term>quicksilver|Mercury (*argentum vivum*). Alchemists believed metals were born from the marriage of sulfur (the masculine, hot principle) and mercury (the feminine, cold principle).</term> (as Albertus Magnus says in the 3rd book of On Minerals, chapter 4).*

Regarding these two sulfurs, Avicenna <note>A Persian polymath (c. 980–1037) whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational to European alchemy.</note>, speaking of minerals under the name of atraments <term>atraments|From the Latin *atramentum* (black ink); a term used for dark vitriols or metallic salts used to make pigments and dyes.</term>, said: "Atraments are composed of salt, of sulfur, and of stones, etc." Just as common vitriols are used as a dye for cloths, so by analogy, sulfurs are the "light" and the dye of metals; for this reason, they are called atraments and vitriols. Because of this, I said above that various vitriols necessary to us were extracted from imperfect metals. That sulfur which is generated in stones is composed of sulfur and stones, but that which is released from vitriol is composed of salt and sulfur. Within these is the "mineral virtue" <note>The inherent power or "DNA" of a mineral that directs its growth into a specific metal.</note> of certain liquefiable bodies which are generated from those sulfurs. The sulfur generated in stones does not dissolve because it does not have the nature of salt; however, the saltiness does dissolve along with the sulfur that is enclosed deep within that salt, and afterward, it thickens through decoction <note>The process of boiling or heating a substance to extract its essence or change its state.</note>. Those metallic sulfurs have already taken on the mineral virtue required to generate metals within certain metallic bodies, and

# ON GEBER
B iiii

*...especially in one in which they have acquired the iron-like virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to the inherent power, quality, or essential property of a substance.</note>, and the copper-like virtue, although they also acquire the virtue of gold and of silver, and sometimes they are named gold or silver; And that which is generated within the stone in its metal has acquired the iron-like virtue, and before the washing <note>A process of purification or "lavation" to remove impurities from a mineral.</note> it is red or saffron-colored, and has the properties of iron, and is called iron; but that which is enclosed in the depths of the vitriol <term>vitriol|A glassy mineral (metallic sulfate). Alchemists used different colors of vitriol—blue for copper, green for iron—as primary materials for their experiments.</term> or salt before the preparation is green like common vitriol, and in the metal it has acquired the copper-like virtue, and the properties of copper, therefore it is named, for example, Venus <note>In alchemical shorthand, Venus is the traditional name and symbol for copper.</note>, and green copper <note>Verdigris.</note>, And these two sulfurs can be extracted from metals by artifice. And because that sulfur called iron, in its preparation, becomes white like silver and is deprived of combustible oiliness <note>original: "ontuosità adustina." This refers to the impure, fatty substance alchemists believed caused metals to blacken or burn away in fire.</note>, therefore Avicenna says that it is an excellent thing for converting his quicksilver into silver; but because that green sulfur in its preparation becomes pure and clear with redness, and in it is the non-burning fiery virtue, therefore it is the best thing that the alchemists can find for making gold, and that is named gold,*

**Dem.** Now tell me how that double smoke comes to the stone,

**Geb.** *Those two vaporous exhalations drawn by the celestial heat <note>The heat of the sun or stars, believed to draw vapors out of the earth.</note>, if they find a terrestrial place that is porous or open through which they can exhale, they are pulled into the air, and there they are converted into comets, or falling stars, and apparent circles <note>Haloes or parhelia (sun dogs).</note> around the sun and moon, and into that celestial arch <note>The rainbow.</note>, or into winds, thunders, lightning, clouds, mists, hail, snow, frost, dew, and other similar things.*

# OF GEBER.

...d, it finds the dissolvable substance, of the substance of the earth, that is, it finds **washed sulfur**: which is the fatness of the earth, and it dissolves it, and joins with it uniformly, until they become one natural substance. And this mixture, through successive **decoction** <note>A process of heating or "cooking" a substance to refine or change it, often used to describe how the earth's internal heat matures minerals into metals.</note> in the **mine**, thickens and hardens and becomes **metal**. <margin>Dem.</margin> If all metals are generated from vitriol and sulfur, transformed by long decoction into another substance of sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "arg. ui."; an abbreviation for *argentum vivum*, the Latin name for mercury.</note>, from where does such great diversity among them proceed?

<margin>Dem.</margin> Can those supervening accidents be removed?

And I tell you further, that the undigested parts can be digested.

<margin>Dem.</margin> This suffices me regarding the **natural principles**. Now give me knowledge of the principles of the Art, and in what way the principles of nature are also principles of the Art, and how Art can imitate Nature.

<margin>Geb.</margin> In the first book, in chapter 5, I said: It is expedient for the artisan to know the principles of this Art, and the roots...

For this reason, Saint Thomas <note>Refers to Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential theologian who also wrote on the nature of matter.</note> at the end of the third book of the Metaphysics says: "That alchemists, through the aforementioned natural principles—namely sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "arg. ui."; abbreviation for *argentum vivum*, or mercury.</note>—perform the true generation of metals." Since nature, in the generation of metals, possesses the specific and determined seed mentioned above—which has a nature and power close to being converted into a metallic nature—it follows that if we wish to make the **Elixir**, which is a more-than-perfect metal, it is necessary to do this by means of nature's own seed. Without this, only God, the creator of nature, could generate such a thing, just as He might make bread from stones, or a woman from a man’s rib.*

For those of the stone of the philosophers have been altered and transmuted from their first nature, and have acquired a nature and property close to being converted into metal, which is a quality that does not belong to the elements of other things.*

# OF GEBER.

<margin>Dem.</margin> They took, therefore, common vitriol <note>original: "uitreolo cōe"; a sulfate mineral often used as a starting material in alchemical experiments.</note>, which is the proper seed of metals, and by distillation they extracted from it that double smoke <note>Refers to the alchemical theory that metals are formed from two "exhalations"—one dry and sulfurous, the other moist and mercurial.</note>, and they cooked it so much with tempered fire that it would be converted into the substances of sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "ar. ui."; an abbreviation for *argentum vivum*, or mercury.</note>. And in this way they imitated nature.

<margin>Geb.</margin> *That is a principle remote from art <note>In this context, "art" refers to the skilled practice of alchemy as a human endeavor.</note>, and I have written in the first book at chapter 8 that we cannot imitate nature in its primary beginnings. And in the third book at chapter 2, at the end, I have said that in sulfur, arsenic, and quicksilver <note>original: "arg. ui."</note> we cannot imitate nature—that is, we cannot generate those in the manner that nature generates them, nor in that way can we lead them to perfection; because those being very subtle fumes, nature with tempered heat leads them to perfection over a thousand years; and if we, with vehement heat, wished to shorten the time, every thing would resolve into smoke.*

<margin>Dem.</margin> You make me wonder.

<margin>Geb.</margin> *For what reason?*

<margin>Dem.</margin> You have said that to make the **Elixir** we must take the seed of nature, and you do not want me to take common vitriol, which is the seed of nature. Is there perhaps another seed, or another vitriol closer to the art, through which craftsmanship might imitate nature and make the Elixir?

<margin>Geb.</margin> *Without a doubt;*

<margin>Dem.</margin> Where is it found?

<margin>Geb.</margin> *Where nature has placed it;*

<margin>Dem.</margin> This I knew; but tell me where she has placed it; is it found perhaps in vegetable things?

<margin>Geb.</margin> *I have said in the first book at chapter 11 that some have placed this art in all vegetable things, and they suggest possibilities, but not for those [vegetables], because they would fail in their labor sooner than it would be possible to finish the work;*

<margin>Dem.</margin> Is it found in animals?

# THE EXPLANATION
<meta>catchword: For.</meta>

<margin>Geb.</margin> Arnold <note>Refers to Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> in the book of the *Perfect Mastery* <note>original: "pfetto magisterio"; refers to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* or a similar work attributed to Arnold regarding the completion of the alchemical work.</note>, speaking of vegetables and animals, says: "Since these things are totally alien to the nature of metals, it is impossible to generate metals from them." And if some philosophers have placed this art in vegetable or animal things, or in anything derived from them, they have spoken by way of metaphor; because, since those are not the principles of nature <note>i.e., the fundamental building blocks of the physical world.</note>, they cannot be the principles of art. In this, many people make an error; because the **Elixir** <note>The medicinal agent used to "heal" base metals into gold.</note> being of a metallic nature, it must be generated from the seed of metals; and having to unite with them, it must be similar to them in substance, because only similar things unite firmly together.

<margin>Dem.</margin> They took, then, the sulfur and the common quicksilver <note>original: "ar. ui."; an abbreviation for *argentum vivum*.</note> generated by nature.

<margin>Geb.</margin> I have said above that these are not the principles of nature, therefore they cannot be the principles of art; and beyond this, human skill <note>original: "arteficio"; refers to the alchemist's practical work.</note> could never fix those together into a metallic substance—whether golden or silver—because it would be necessary for us to unite them together proportionally, and to know the method of proportion and the balance of the thickening heat <note>original: "calore inspissante"; the specific intensity of fire required to turn a liquid or vapor into a solid substance.</note>, which is unknown to us, as I have said in the first book at chapter 8.

<margin>Dem.</margin> I will take only the sulfur; since in the first book at chapter 13 you said: "He who knows, in the preparation, how to mix and reconcile <note>original: "amicare"; literally "to make a friend of," suggesting the harmonious joining of substances.</note> that with the bodies, will know one of the greatest secrets of nature, and a way of perfection, since many paths lead to one effect and one intent." ### OF GEBER

Most high God, He illuminates every body, because He is light and **tincture** <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a substance capable of infusing another body with its color and properties; it is often used as a synonym for the Elixir itself.</note>. <margin>Geb.</margin> *Although our **sulfur** <note>The active, "combustible" principle in alchemy, distinct from common brimstone.</note> is the principal seed and principle of metals and of the Elixir, nevertheless, just as a father, by means of his seed, does not generate children within himself but in another—namely, in the menstrual blood—so sulfur does not generate metals or the Elixir within itself, but in its own **quicksilver** <note>original: "ar. ui."; abbreviation for *argentum vivum*.</note>, or in other metals. Note, therefore, that the Elixir can be made in two ways: by distillation, solution, and coagulation of the matter; and without distillation, solution, and coagulation, as **Saint Thomas** <note>Refers to Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274). In a "shortening" chapter of mine <note>original: "cap. accortatorio"; a concise instructional summary or "shortcut" text.</note>, I have said that this abbreviated work is performed in twenty days, and that to do this work we need two stones—that is, two metals. # OF GEBER

I have said that the cause of the perfection of bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid metals, such as gold, silver, or lead.</note>—or of **quicksilver** <note>original: "arg. ui."; abbreviation for *argentum vivum*.</note>—is the middling substance of **sulfur** and **arsenic**; because of its earthiness and good substance, it is not hindered from entering the bodies, which is achieved perfectly through good fusion. Furthermore, due to its subtlety, the impression it makes is not easily removed by "flight" <note>Evaporation or sublimation caused by heat.</note>. However, the middling substance of these is not the cause of the perfection of bodies or of quicksilver unless it is better **fixed** <note>A "fixed" substance is one that has been rendered stable and will not evaporate when exposed to the fire.</note>. For if it is not fixed by the ultimate **fixation**, even though the impression it leaves on the bodies is not easily removed, it nevertheless does not remain stable perpetually. Regarding this sulfur and arsenic, speaking under the names of **Mars** and **Venus** <note>Traditional alchemical names for iron and copper, used here as symbolic "deck-names" for specific chemical states.</note> in the eighth chapter of the third book, I have said: "The fixation of these two substances approaches a firm fixation, but it is not yet firm and perpetual." <margin>Demogorgon</margin> It seems to me that in the first book, in the tenth chapter, you reproach the work of certain people who fixed the **spirits** <note>Volatile substances, such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic, that can "fly" from the fire.</note> within the bodies.

<margin>Geber</margin> To this, it is enough for me to say what I have written in the 2nd book, chapter 17, when speaking of the medicine which coagulates <note>To "coagulate" in alchemy means to turn a liquid or volatile substance into a solid, stable form.</note> **quicksilver** <note>original: "arg. uiuo"; an abbreviation of *argentum vivum*, the Latin name for mercury.</note>, and mixes with it in its depths, even into its smallest parts. For this substance will coagulate the quicksilver and convert it into a **solar nature** <note>Gold.</note> or a **lunar nature** <note>Silver.</note>.

Note, therefore, that for the reasons stated above, our quicksilver is not common quicksilver; rather, it is a salt, the likeness of which is the likeness of true common **lime** <note>In alchemy, "calce" or calx refers to the powdery residue left after a metal has been roasted or "calcined."</note>. This is because the quicksilver of metals, through **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a metal until it turns into a fine powder or oxide.</term> and **reduction** <term>reduction: the chemical process of returning a calcined powder back into its metallic form.</term>, is converted into salt, as is manifest throughout the entire *Testament*.

And this salt has all the properties which I have mentioned many times regarding our quicksilver—properties which hardly apply to common quicksilver. These include having great viscosity, great dryness, and a fetid earthiness; it is "dry water" <note>original: "acqua secca"; a paradoxical alchemical term for mercury, which is a liquid that does not wet the hands.</note> and similar things which we shall speak of another time.

Speaking of this our quicksilver in the 2nd book, chapter 16, I said that it has parts of sulfur naturally mixed within it—some more, and some less—which cannot be removed by any artificial means. ...means of binding those together; **Sulfur** <note>original: "solpho"</note> is a tincture of **redness** <term>redness: "rubedine" or Rubedo, the final stage of the alchemical Great Work, symbolizing perfection and solar heat.</term> easily, but of **whiteness** <term>whiteness: "albedine" or Albedo, the stage of purification and washing that follows the darkness of putrefaction.</term> with difficulty. <margin>prepared sulfur <note>original: "sulfur preparatum"</note></margin>

**Dem.** <note>Referring to Democritus, the ancient philosopher who, in alchemical tradition, often appears as a dialogue partner</note> If this sulfur, arsenic, and quicksilver <term>quicksilver: "arg. uiuo" (argentum vivum), or liquid mercury</term> are fixed <term>fixed: "fissi," referring to substances that have been made resistant to evaporation by fire</term>, they do not melt and do not penetrate the bodies because they have become earth; and if they are not fixed, being spirits, they flee from the fire. Therefore, how can the Elixir be made from them?

**Geb.** <note>Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, considered the father of chemistry</note> This is the reasoning of some who are little practiced in this art. And in Chapter 10 of the first book, I said that they do not do everything that can be known regarding this, and therefore they do not find the entire operation of it; by the force of their reasoning, therefore, they commit the fallacy of the consequent <note>original: "uitio del consequente," a logical error in which the practitioner draws a false conclusion because they lack a full understanding of the chemical transformation</note> due to their insufficiency.

<summary>In this dialogue, Geber explains how the "citrine" or yellow stage of the Great Work is achieved by balancing red and white proportions. He corrects Democritus’s misconception that chemical "spirits" must be either totally fixed or totally volatile, suggesting a more complex middle state is required for the Elixir.</summary>

**Dem.** *I do not understand this logical way of speaking;*

In this context, Geber argues that while earthy things are fixed, it does not mean all fixed things are earthy</term> is committed when one argues from the superior to the inferior affirmatively; therefore, it is not valid to say: "It is fixed, therefore it is earthy." This mode of reasoning is insufficient because <term>spirits: "spiriti," volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that can be vaporized by heat</term> can also be fixed without being converted into an earthy nature, and they establish themselves and penetrate bodies. Regarding <term>sulfur: "sulpho"</term>, this is evident in Book 1, Chapters 10 and 13; in Book 2, Chapters 12 and 18; and in Book 3, Chapters 1 and 6. This is a rapid consumption of the primary mercurial form, as it happens in six hours; but once the earthiness is separated by <term>washing: "lavatione," the purification of a substance by rinsing away impurities</term>, it is united with the other prepared spirits and placed in its vessel over a slow fire. Its vapors successively rise and descend many times <note>This refers to "reflux" or "circulation," where vapors condense and drip back down to be reheated</note>, and thus through a slow and long confirmation and fixation, it reaches <term>metallic fusion: "fusione metallica," the state where a substance can melt like a metal without turning to ash</term> and is not converted into earth. As for the preparation and fixation of other things into an earthy nature: metallic bodies are fixed through their <term>calcination: "calcinatione," the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>, as I have stated in Book 2, Chapter 18. <margin>fixation by calcination <note>original Latin: "fixatio calcinando"</note></margin> Through its calcination, the common <term>imperfect body: "corpo imperfetto," base metals like lead or tin, which were thought to "die" or oxidize easily in fire</term> is fixed when it is calcined without the mixture of any other substance, but by itself with its own <term>combustible sulfur: "solpho adustiuo," the internal flammable principle that alchemists believed caused metals to burn or corrode</term>.

Therefore, when your metal has remained in the fire so long that it can no longer be reduced to the body it was before, and can no longer be diminished nor change from color to color, then the metal is fixed—that is, converted into a certain earthy nature which does not flee from the fire. This state does not allow for metallic melting, but only for <term>vitrification: "uitrificatoria," the process of turning into a glass-like substance</term>; and then all the spirits existing in that <term>calx: "calce," the powdery residue left after a metal is burnt</term> are fixed. 
But because they cannot be prepared unless they are first separated, made volatile, and only afterward made fixed, let us see how they are fixed by conversion to an earthy nature; for in this way, you will gain a good part of the knowledge of their preparation. <margin>fixation through conversion <note>original Latin: "fixatio p conuersionem"</note></margin>
And since they are not all fixed together or in the same manner by this fixation, let us speak of them distinctly. Having spoken sufficiently here of Mercury; let us now discuss arsenic and sulfur.
Above I have said that through calcination, the mercury of metals is converted into salt. And because that salt has many earthy parts...

The first is useful, indeed necessary, as is sulfur; the second is useful as mortified quicksilver, that is, being moderately prepared. However, common quicksilver is not useful because, as <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and alchemist</note> says in *On Minerals* <note>original: "ne mine," referring to "De Mineralibus"</note>: if it is not mortified, it does not mix well with other things. Let us therefore take this last substance of our quicksilver, because by using it we are excused from taking common quicksilver and from the labors of its mortification. *...used, and the sulfur from it should be sublimated without ignition <note>direct fire</note>—that is, in dung <note>horse manure was used as a source of gentle, steady heat for digestion</note>—and after the putrefaction, in the [water] bath, always removing the sulfur, which very often sublimates into those films. The bottom of the aludel for sublimation—that is, for putrefaction—should be flat and not concave, so that the body of our marcasite, spread evenly and thinly over the bottom, may be able to rise and sublimate into the vinegar, evenly and abundantly in all its parts of salt and sulfur.*

*After the separation of the sulfur called <term>Mars: "Marte," the alchemical name for iron</term>, the salt must successively be distilled in the ignition vessel until all its sulfur called <term>arsenic: "arsenico"</term> has come out. And note that when all the sulfur called Mars has sublimated into many films, you will see its color—that is, the final films which sublimate afterward will change to a very white color; and after the separation of the vinegar, the unctuosity <note>original: "ontuosita," referring to an oily or greasy substance</note> separated from the sulfur will be burned away.*

And because in this way the sulfur becomes more perfect, more fixed <term>fixed: resistant to change by fire; non-volatile</term>, and more shining, it is therefore said that Antaeus always rises up stronger. ...water, and there by decoction <term>decoction: the process of boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or change its properties</term> it becomes black and dies; and this can also be understood as putrefaction <note>the breakdown or decomposition of the substance</note>, as you will understand when I speak of the elevation of bodies <term>elevation of bodies: the alchemical process of sublimation, where a solid is purified by turning it into vapor and back into a solid</term>.
