Therefore, when we wish to sublimate orpiment—that is, arsenic—or sulfur, we remove one oily quality from them by washing them in urine, lye, vinegar, and goat's milk. These washes are sharp and remove the oiliness from it. By those "sharp washes," one understands vinegar with salt. Avicenna <note>original: "Auic."; the Latinized name of Ibn Sina, a Persian polymath whose medical and chemical writings were highly influential</note> in the *Epistle to Arsen the Philosopher* says: Let us strive to separate from sulfur and arsenic everything within them which blackens silver. But as sulfur is the best of all minerals, we have found the way to it, which is that we decoct it with a slow fire so that the fiery power <note>original: "uirtù ignea"</note> within it does not burn up, but rather is drawn out; and so that the fire does not burn the substance of the sulfur and the fiery power, but only the oiliness. Sulfur is said to be the best of the other spirits because it is more fixed <term>fixed: a substance that is stable under heat and does not easily evaporate</term> and is a tincture of redness <note>original: "rubedine"; the quality of being red, often associated with the final stages of the alchemical Great Work</note>. By the silver which is blackened by the oiliness of sulfur, one understands the whiteness <note>original: "albedine"</note> hidden under the redness of the sulfur. Rhazes <note>original: "Rasi."; Al-Razi, a renowned Persian physician and alchemist</note> in the *Book of Divisions* <note>original: "lib. della Diui." or *Liber Divisionum*</note>, speaking of this preparation, says: Take the oil with the sponge which rises up, until nothing black rises anymore. And above, a round cloud will rise; leave it until it is finished and its sublimation is confirmed. After, take a cloud <note>original catchword: "nu="; likely "nuvola" (cloud)</note>...

### OF GEBER.

...cloud <note>original: "gola"; the completion of the word "nugola" from the previous page's catchword</note>, and afterward add boiling water when it decreases; the water then takes another cloud, and another, and continues in this way until the clouds are consumed, because those are the oil of sulfur. **Dem.** Leave these reasonings for a moment; you have not yet clearly told me from where, and from what matter, we must extract these spirits and natural principles that are close to our art.

**Geb.** Would it not be a vain study for you to think that these natural principles—digested, coagulated, and almost fixed, close to the art, and converted into the substance of sulfur and quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury; the original text uses "arg. ui." for *argentum vivum*</term>—could be found in any other thing upon the earth than in metals?

**Dem.** Oh, certainly I wanted to say that, but one thing has held me back<insert>.</insert>

**Geb.** You should have told me your opinion, and I would have drawn you out of doubt.

**Dem.** I have heard it said that the Elixir <note>The "Elixir" or Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical work</note> is mineral, vegetable, and animal; how then can it be made from metals, since those are dead substances which cannot generate their like?

**Geb.** The Elixir is called "vegetable" because in its multiplication <term>multiplication: the stage of the alchemical process where the power and quantity of the medicine is increased</term> it grows in virtue and quantity. It is called "animal" because when cast upon other metals, it makes them similar to itself <note>This refers to the Elixir's "life-giving" power to transform base metals into gold</note>. It is named "mineral" because it is generated from metals, and also because generating gold and silver is the property of mineral things. Or, the Elixir and our metallic stone are called vegetable because they are gener—

# THE EXPLANATION

...[gene]rated from salt which is called vegetable, because it is generated by the power of heat, which causes everything to grow <note>original: *uegetare*; to vegetate or sprout</note>, or else because said salt was first green like herbs and vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate mineral, often green in its "iron" form, vital to alchemical processes</term>; for this reason I have named it "living water." They are called "animal" because into their composition enters the soul—that is, a tincture which is named arsenic and *es* <term>*es*: Latin for copper or bronze; in alchemy, it refers to the "body" of the metal</term>, of which *es* it is written in the *Trumpet of the Philosophers* <note>original: *tuba de philosophi*; a well-known alchemical treatise</note>: "Our *es* is like man, who has a soul, a body, and a spirit." The soul is the red tincture, the body is the sulfurous substance and is nearly fixed <note>stable or non-volatile</note>, and the spirit is the volatile nature before it is fixed. They are called "mineral" because they are metallic, or because they contain within them that sulfur in which the metallic virtue consists. Aristotle <note>referred to as "Aristo."</note>, in his letter to Alexander, says: "Take the vegetable, animal, and mineral stone." We see that artists make certain medicines from metals, some more and some less perfect, by which they transform and hold certain metals in the color of gold or silver. And because in every category where these degrees of perfection are found, it is necessary to arrive at one which holds the supreme and most perfect degree of perfection in that category—as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> affirms in the 10th book of the *Metaphysics*—it is therefore necessary that a most perfect metallic medicine be found, which can transform imperfect metals into true gold or true silver. This is because things which have affinity in their substance can, through their corruption <note>in the Aristotelian sense of "change" or "decay" allowing for new generation</note>, be transformed one into the other, as we see with the elements. And because all metals (as I have said above) are generated from one and the same substance, and among...

# OF GEBER.

...that I have said, that of whatever thing the white or red **Elixir** <note>a potent alchemical medicine or transformative substance</note> is made, nonetheless within it there is nothing other than **sulfur** <term>sulfur: in alchemy, the active, masculine, and "combustible" principle of matter</term> and **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury; the passive, feminine, and fluid principle of matter</term>, of which one without the other does nothing, nor can they even exist. If, therefore, naturally one is together with the other, it is manifest that the Elixir is not extracted from many metals, but from one alone: for they are all composed of sulfur and quicksilver. Nonetheless, it is said that the Elixir is extracted from many things, because it is composed of sulfur, **arsenic** <note>in this context, likely referring to a volatile mineral principle rather than just the poison</note>, and quicksilver, with which—and with the diverse colors appearing during their preparation—many things have some similarity. It is true that in the **lesser work** <note>original: "opera breue"; a shorter or simpler alchemical process</note>, there are several paths, and various metals may be involved; but in the **Great Work** <term>Great Work: the *Magnum Opus*, the ultimate alchemical process for creating the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone</term>, only one has the power and potential close enough to be converted into the Elixir. And therefore, in the second book, chapter two, I have said that if you **sublimate** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</term> the spirits from the **calx** <term>calx: the powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been burnt or oxidized</term> of any metal, you will sublimate well; otherwise, the sublimation will be extremely difficult and involve such long labor as to lead to desperation.

**Dem.** Therefore, that metal can be nothing other than **gold**.

**Geb.** Why gold?

**Dem.** Because it is more virtuous, more perfect, more incorruptible and incombustible than the others, for its parts were extremely subtle, homogeneous, and pure; and together they have formed such a union that the earthy part is protected from burning by its moisture, and the moisture is so held by the earthy part that it cannot fly away in smoke through evaporation. Being, then, so incorruptible, and thus through **distillation** <term>distillation: a process of purification where a substance is heated to a vapor and then condensed back into a liquid</term> reduced to the ultimate purity and subtlety;

# THE EXPOSITION

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<margin>
one
reason
</margin>
[A] thing moves toward a form, or perfection, if first it does not have in itself some imperfect part of that form toward which it moves; we know that the **seed** <term>seed: (Latin: *sperma*) in alchemy, the embryonic or potential state of a substance before it has reached its final metallic form</term> would not move to acquire the form of man, if it did not first have man within it in potential; for if that were a complete man, it would not be a principle of motion, but rather of rest, because *Aristotle* <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC), the Greek philosopher whose theories on matter and "privation" provided the logical framework for alchemical change</note> says that when the species exists in the matter, motion ceases, because motion is nothing if not for the **privation** <term>privation: the absence of a quality or form that a substance naturally seeks to possess; the "hunger" of matter for a higher state</term> of the species, which is in the matter. Since therefore the imperfect, insofar as it is imperfect, naturally desires to be perfect, the privation leaves in it the natural desire to be perfect: And this desire is the principle of transmutations, as *Roger Bacon* <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), an English Franciscan friar and philosopher famous for his early work on the scientific method and alchemy</note> says; since gold is perfect in its metallic species, it therefore does not have within itself this privation and desire for the metallic form and species.

**Democritus.** I concede that common gold is perfect in its kind, but compared to the **Elixir** <note>original: "lo Elixir"; the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary goal of alchemy capable of perfecting all things</note>, it is imperfect, because artifice in this surpasses the work of nature.

**Geber.** I say that gold is a more perfect metal than the Elixir, which—although it excellently retains the metallic substance within itself—is not however a metal, because it is fragile, and is composed of **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances (like mercury or sulfur) that can be vaporized; Geber argues the Elixir is too "spiritual" to be classified as a solid metal</term> alone, and its **tincture** <term>tincture: the concentrated transformative power or "dye" of the Stone</term> exceeds the common metallic tincture.

**Democritus.** If to make the Elixir a metal is necessary, I want to take the spirits of gold, because they are pure and clean and fixed; and the others are unclean and volatile.

**Geber.** This is a sign that they are not those of which I have spoken in all my books. Because I have said that the

### OF GEBER.
D iiii
<meta>catchword: to chapter 15. Therefore gold duly cannot be calci=</meta>

Our **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: (Latin: *argentum vivum*) mercury, viewed as the "mother" or liquid principle of all metals</term>, **sulfur**, and **arsenic**, before preparation, are impure, combustible, and volatile; and in many places I have taught the way to remove from them many earthly, watery, and oily or combustible sulfurous qualities, which are not found in gold. **Albert** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a German Dominican friar and philosopher who wrote *De Mineralibus* (On Minerals), a key text for medieval alchemy</note> also in the third book of *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine."</note>, in chapter 6, says that a metal is never generated from another metal so long as it remains in its first metallic substance. And therefore in chapter 9 of the aforementioned book, he says that alchemy proceeds in this way: by corrupting one thing and removing it from its own **species** <term>species: the essential identity or "form" of a substance; in alchemical theory, one must destroy the "species" of lead before it can become the "species" of gold</term>, and with the help of those things that are within the metallic matter, inducing the species of another metal. Therefore, of all alchemical operations, the best is that which proceeds by those same principles through which nature proceeds—such as the purification of sulfur through **decoction** <term>decoction: the process of boiling or heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence</term> and **sublimation** <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then returned to a solid state, used here to purify chemicals</term>, and the purification of quicksilver. 

But those who use "white" metals to make things white, and "yellow" metals to make things yellow <note>original: "citrinano," referring to the color of citrine or gold</note>, while the original species of the metal remains, are undoubtedly deceivers; they do not create true gold or true silver. The **Philosopher** <note>Usually a reference to Aristotle, whose theories on the "First Matter" were the foundation of alchemical science</note> also says that no true transmutation of metals occurs unless they are reduced to their **first matter** <term>first matter: the formless, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made; to change lead to gold, one must first strip it down to this state</term>; that is, to the nature of **salt** or **vitriol** <term>vitriol: a group of sulfate minerals (like copper sulfate) often used as powerful solvents or catalysts in the lab</term>. This cannot be done without **calcination** <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder; alchemists saw this as a "death" or "purification by fire"</term>, as I have stated throughout the *Testamentum*. 

And because gold cannot be duly calcined (as I said in Book 3, Chapter 9), because true calcination is achieved without mixing in any outside substance, but only through its own combustible sulfur—as I mentioned in Book 1, Chapter 13 and Book 3, Chapter 6—and since this combustible sulfur is not present in gold (as I said in Book 4, Chapter 15), it follows that gold cannot be duly calci=

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical necessity of reducing metals to their 'first matter' to achieve true transmutation. Citing Albertus Magnus and Aristotle, the author argues that gold is unique and resistant to calcination because it lacks the 'combustible sulfur' found in lesser, impure minerals.</summary>

-cine. And this reason I have written in the 3rd book at chapter 6, saying: *That body to which the three causes of corruption written in the aforementioned place apply, is most of all corruptible; and in that to which not all [causes] apply, the speed of corruption is lessened according to the lessening of those causes.* And because in gold there is none of those causes of corruption, therefore it duly cannot be corrupted. And for this reason, in the 3rd book at chapter 4, I said that gold has a fixed substance and is without **burning sulfurousness** <term>burning sulfurousness: (Latin: *sulphureità comburente*) the volatile, impure sulfur that causes metals to tarnish or burn away in fire</term>; this is manifest through every operation performed on it with fire, because it neither diminishes nor catches fire. Geber argues that since gold is already perfect, trying to "fix" it through calcination is a pointless expense.</note>.

**Dem.** **Arnaldus** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> in the book of the *Perfect Magistery* <note>original: "perfetto magisterio"</note> says that the reduction of metal to the **first matter** <term>first matter: (Latin: *materia prima*) the primal, formless substance from which all metals originate</term> is easy; therefore, the transformation is easy.

**Geb.** Arnaldus does not mean common gold, because that can hardly and only with the greatest difficulty be destroyed due to its strong composition, as I said in the 1st book at chapter 8, at the end. In the 2nd book also, at chapter 7, I go about investigating from which metals we should **sublimate** <term>sublimate: to vaporize and re-condense a substance to purify it</term>—that is, extract—our **quicksilver** <gloss>mercury</gloss>. And although I do not openly exclude gold and silver, nonetheless I conclude by saying: therefore, the sublimation of quicksilver is better with those—that is, from those metals—with which it does not agree <note>original: "non conuiene." In alchemical terms, mercury "agrees" with gold by forming an amalgam; it is harder to separate mercury from gold than from metals it does not "agree" with.</note>; but it is certain that it agrees less with all the other metals than with gold, as I said in the 3rd book at chapters 2 and 4. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Democritus (Dem.) and Geber (Geb.) regarding the composition of the Elixir. It follows the previous discussion on the corruption of metals and why gold is unique, moving into the debate over whether the Elixir must be made from actual gold or "viler" materials.</meta>

->### OF GEBER.<-

it is better to extract it from others than from gold. *How can the **Gold Elixir** <term>Gold Elixir: the "medicine" or catalyst capable of transmuting base metals into gold or perfecting the human body</term> be made, according to the opinion of all the philosophers, if it is made from a most vile thing?* **Morienus** <note>Morienus is a legendary figure in alchemical literature, often cited as a master who taught the secrets of the stone to the King Khalid.</note> says: *I command you that in the weight of materials for this work, you spend nothing at all in any way, and especially not in the work of gold.*

**Dem.** *What will you answer to my reasons?*

**Geb.** I say that the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture: (Latin: *humidum radicale*) the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid believed to be the core of a substance’s permanence</term> of the other metals is virtuous, incorruptible, and incombustible, as much as gold. **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a famous scholar and theologian who wrote extensively on the nature of minerals.</note> *in the 3rd [book] on Minerals, at chapter 2, says*: We see all metallic things, even in great fires, retain their radical moisture. The same I have confirmed at the end of the 2nd book. And if modern physicians use it in their medicines, they produce no benefit at all, because if fire cannot corrupt it, much less can the stomach digest it. And if the ancient physicians, philosophers, and poets have praised gold, they did not mean common gold, but the **Philosophers' Gold** <term>Philosophers' Gold: a purified, non-vulgar state of matter used in the alchemical process, rather than common coins or jewelry</term>; because common gold cannot be duly distilled and dissolved by itself, for it cannot be duly converted into **salt** <term>salt: in an alchemical context, the fixed, crystalline residue after a substance has been processed</term>, as I have said above. To that reason, in which you say that it is reasonable that the golden Elixir be made of gold, I say that in the **Short Work** <term>Short Work: (*opus breve*) a faster, simplified alchemical process</term>, in which it is not necessary to dissolve or congeal the gold, gold is better than any other metal; but because in the **Greater Work** <term>Greater Work: (*opus magnum*) the full, arduous process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term>, the metal must be reduced to the **primary matter** <term>primary matter: (*materia prima*) the original, formless substance from which all things are created</term>, in which there is no difference, as I have said above, therefore it cannot be said that gold is better than the others; I do not say, however, that all are equally good in this work, because only one in the mine

<summary>Geber argues that the Elixir is not made from common gold but from a "primary matter" shared by all metals. He explains that while gold is pure, its indigestibility makes it useless for common medicine, and the "Philosophers' Gold" is a distinct, spiritualized substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical defense of using "vile" or base materials to create the Elixir, expanding on the previous discussion where Geber argued that the "radical moisture" of all metals is essentially the same. Here, the text explains how the alchemical "stone" contains the potential for gold and silver without being common gold or silver itself.</meta>

-># THE EXPLANATION<-
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

[its nature] has acquired something, and certain qualities through which its substance is better and closer to **our art** than all other metals. Regarding that other reason, I say that the **Philosopher** <note>Likely referring to Aristotle, the primary authority on natural philosophy in the Middle Ages.</note> refers to the proper and **univocal generation** <term>univocal generation: the biological process where a parent produces offspring of the same species (e.g., a human begets a human)</term> of animals; but in the generation which is brought about through the **corruption** <note>In alchemy, "corruption" is the necessary breakdown or "death" of a substance's form so that a new, higher form can be "generated."</note> of the immediate generator, it is enough that the generator be similar to the generated in virtue and power, as I have said before regarding human seed. 

In this way, I say that our **vile stone** <note>The "vile stone" is the starting material for the Great Work, often described as common, cheap, or overlooked by the ignorant.</note> from which the **Elixir**, **potable gold**, and the **quintessence** <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," a purified substance beyond the four elements believed to have extraordinary healing and transformative powers</term> are made, is gold and silver in virtue and proximate power. And regarding this, **Arnaldo** <note>Arnaldus de Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> speaks in his letter to the King of Naples, saying: *It is to be known that in the said composition, or stone, there are the **Sun** and the **Moon** <term>Sun and Moon: alchemical symbols for Gold and Silver, respectively</term> in virtue and power; for if these things were not there, neither Sun nor Moon could be made from it.* 

Nonetheless, it is not common Sun as Sun, nor common Moon as Moon, because the Sun and Moon existing in said composition are better than they are in their common nature. This is because the Sun and Moon of the said composite are **alive**, that is, **vegetable** <note>In an alchemical context, "vegetable" means the substance has the power to grow, multiply, or transform, unlike "dead" common metals.</note>, and the other common ones are dead because they are finished. Although the philosophers have named this stone "Sun" and "Moon," they did so because in that stone they exist potentially, but not visibly. And when the philosophers say that gold is the **tincture of redness** and the **ferment of redness**, I say they mean the **Philosophers' Gold**, which is **sulfur** <note>This refers to alchemical Sulfur, the active, masculine principle of heat and color, not necessarily the chemical element sulfur.</note>. Through its heat and fervor, it cooks, digests, and tints its **quicksilver** <note>original: "arg. ui." (argentum vivum)</note>. Similarly, the **Golden Elixir** tints and transforms our **mercury** during the **multiplication** <term>multiplication: the stage of the alchemical process where the power and quantity of the Elixir are increased</term>, and therefore it is the truest ferment. And

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical "stone" contains the living potential of gold and silver (Sun and Moon), which allows it to act as a ferment to transform other metals. It distinguishes between "dead" common metals and the "living" alchemical principles that can grow and multiply.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on the nature of alchemical materials. While the previous page cited Arnaldus de Villanova regarding the "vile stone" having the potential for gold, here the speaker (Geber) clarifies his specific definitions of "perfect bodies" and "sulfur," shifting from general theory to the practical refinement of "spirits" and the "Philosophers' Gold."</meta>

# OF GEBER.

If I said that the **perfect bodies** <note>In alchemy, "perfect bodies" refers to gold and silver, the metals that have reached full maturity in the earth.</note> can be improved, I did not mean common gold, or common silver, but the **elixir**, which can be improved and refined in the **multiplication** <term>multiplication: the process of increasing the power and quantity of the alchemical medicine or elixir</term> by adding **mercury** <note>original: "ar. ui." or *argento vivo*</note> alone. By "perfect bodies" I also meant the **prepared sulfurs**, which on account of their redness are sometimes called gold, and on account of their whiteness are called silver. 

And these must be prepared with their salt and vinegar, and afterwards they must be refined and dissolved with **sal ammoniac** <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy as a powerful flux and to help "volatilize" or move fixed substances</term>, that is, with the water of its salt; therefore in the 2nd book, at chapter 15, I said that the reason for the invention of the **subtle and sharp water** <note>Likely a reference to mineral acids like nitric acid or aqua regia, which could dissolve metals.</note> was the refinement of those **spirits**, which before preparation have neither fusion nor **ingress** <term>ingress: the ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate the "pores" of a metal to transform it</term>, and from which great utility was lost—namely, from the fixed spirits and those which are of their nature. **Arsenic** is of the nature of sulfur, as I said in the 1st book, at chapter 14. In the 2nd book, at chapter 12, I said the reason for the invention of pure water was the **imbibition** <note>The process of soaking or saturating a dry substance with liquid to soften or nourish it.</note> of the spirits and clean medicines, that is, prepared sulfurs.

**Dem.** <note>Short for Demogorgon or a similar student character in a dialogue.</note> You would do me a great favor if you would explain to me what the **gold of the philosophers** is, and their **potable gold**.

**Geb.** It is a most subtle **radical sulfur** <term>radical sulfur: the essential, underlying oily substance that gives a metal its properties</term> and pure, which I named above as arsenic. In the *Mirror of Alchemy* it is written: "Our sulfur is good and pure gold." **Rhazes** <note>Rhazes (c. 854–925) was a Persian physician and one of the most cited authorities in medieval alchemy.</note>, in the *Light of Lights*, says: "The oil which takes on the red color is sulfur, and that is also **bronze** <note>original: "es" from the Latin *aes*</note>, and it is compared to the sun and to gold." **Senior the Philosopher** <note>Senior Zadith (Ibn Umail), a 10th-century alchemist whose symbolic texts were highly influential.</note> says: "The gold from this stone is philosophers' gold, and it is the **tincture**, which is the **soul**, with which the spirit ascends." The aforementioned also says: "the divine spiritual water, in which is the soul, by which it is carried, which they name gold, etc." He also says: "**Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note> names the wa-"

<summary>Geber clarifies that his instructions for "improving" metals refer to refining the Elixir and prepared sulfurs rather than common gold. He defines "Philosophers' Gold" as a pure, radical sulfur or "tincture" that acts as the soul of the work, citing authorities like Rhazes and Senior Zadith.</summary>

Of this soul called gold, Hermes, in his *Secret of Secrets* <note>original: "secreto de segretissimi," referring to the *Secretum Secretorum*, a famous medieval grimoire attributed to Hermes</note>, said: *The wind has carried it in its belly.*

**Dem.** I do not understand what thing this **wind and belly** might be.

**Geb.** **Albert** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a scholar and bishop who wrote extensively on minerals and alchemy.</note>, in the first book of *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine." By the "wind," therefore, is signified that white smoke which carries within itself that red smoke called the soul, gold, **copper** <note>original Latin: "es uenere" (aes venere)</note>, **coin** <note>original: "nummo"</note>, arsenic, glass, vitriol, fire, oil, sulfur, green water, **green lion** <term>green lion: a common alchemical symbol for a powerful solvent (often vitriol or acidic mercury) that "devours" gold</term>, verdigris, wine, human blood, dragon's blood, orpiment, <insert>permanent water,</insert> and in many other ways. **Senior** <note>Senior Zadith (Zadith ben Hamuel), a 10th-century Arab alchemist whose work was highly influential in the West.</note> says: This divine water is named by the name of every humidity—of vinegars, and wines, and milks, and fats, and bloods, and sperm—and by the names of all the tinctures and flowers; and they understand by these the soul of the stone, which...

The same happens to sulfur: and when it is converted into whiteness <term>albedine: the 'albedo' or whitening stage, which follows the blackening stage in the alchemical Great Work</term>, and through long decoction <term>decottione: a process of long-continued heating or "cooking" a substance in a liquid</term> it becomes red, then the silver is converted into gold. In the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba de philosophi," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, appearing as a record of a meeting of various thinkers.</note> it is written: "Know that no tincture <note>In alchemy, a 'tincture' is a substance capable of infusing color and transmutative properties into another body.</note> is made except from our bronze <term>es: from the Latin 'aes,' meaning bronze or copper, but here referring to the secret base metal of the philosophers</term>; however, if the aforementioned bronze is not converted into white until it becomes a tincture, you will achieve nothing."

**Dem.** *Truly, I remain very much obliged to you, for you have saved me from great expense; and to tell you my error, for a long time I, along with many others, have been of the opinion that the Elixir and potable gold <term>oro potabile: "drinkable gold," a legendary alchemical medicine thought to grant longevity or cure all ills</term> were made from common gold.*

**Geb.** *You are like children, who like things that are beautiful in appearance; but these things and the quintessence <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence" or ether, the purest element that remains after the four common elements are balanced</term> are made from a stone that is ugly on the surface, but most beautiful in its hidden nature.*

**Dem.** Since through your kindness you have explained to me what the gold of the philosophers and potable gold are, I pray you further explain to me what the quintessence is: because I judge that it is better to know how to preserve the

### OF GEBER ###

*...drained into the depth of the bodies, because the volatile part overcomes the fixed part; Arnald <note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note> in the Rosary <note>original: "Rosario," referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a seminal alchemical treatise.</note> says that he who does not know how to convert gold into silver does not yet know how to convert silver into gold.*

**Dem.** *I would like to know how to convert silver into gold, but I do not care to know how to convert gold into silver.*

**Geb.** *The Senior <note>original: "Il Seniore," referring to the 10th-century Arabic alchemist Zadith ben Hamuel, known in Latin as Senior Zadith.</note> says: when the philosophers said, "whiten the gold" <note>original: "dealbate l'oro"</note>, it is not the gold of the common people <term>gold of the common people: "oro del vulgo," referring to ordinary metallic gold as opposed to the "philosophical" gold used in alchemy</term>. Therefore, when this red gold of the philosophers becomes white, it then converts into silver, and is named silver. The same happens to sulfur; and when it is converted into whiteness <note>original: "albedine," referring to the *albedo* or whitening stage of the Great Work</note>, and through long decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</term> it becomes red, then the silver is converted into gold. In the Assembly of the Philosophers <note>original: "Turba de philosophi," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, presented as a debate among ancient sages</note> it is written: Know that no tincture <term>tincture: a potent substance or "medicine" used to transform base metals into gold or silver</term> is made, except from our bronze <note>original: "es," from the Latin *aes*, meaning copper or bronze</note>; however, if the aforementioned bronze is not converted into white, until a tincture is made, you will achieve nothing.*

**Dem.** *Truly, I remain much obliged to you, for you have saved me much expense; and to tell you my error, for a long time, along with many others, I have been of the opinion that the Elixir and potable gold <term>potable gold: "oro potabile," a legendary liquid preparation of gold believed to cure all diseases and grant longevity</term> were made from common gold.*

**Geb.** *You are like children, who like things that are beautiful in appearance; but these things and the quintessence <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated form of a substance</term> are made from a stone that is ugly on the outside, but most beautiful in its hidden nature <note>This refers to the "Philosopher's Stone," which alchemists often claimed was found in common or "vile" matter that others discard.</note>.*

**Dem.** *Since by your kindness you have explained to me what the gold of the philosophers and potable gold are, I pray you further declare to me what the quintessence is: because I judge it to be better to know how to preserve the*

<meta>Catchword: health (sanità)</meta>

<summary>Geber explains to Democritus that the "gold" and "silver" of the philosophers are not common metals, but states achieved through chemical processes like whitening and decoction. He clarifies that the Elixir and Quintessence are derived from a seemingly "ugly" base material rather than expensive common gold.</summary>
<keywords>Arnald of Villanova, Rosarium Philosophorum, Senior Zadith, albedo, sulfur, Turba Philosophorum, tincture, bronze (aes), potable gold, quintessence, materia prima</keywords>

->### THE EXPLANATION ###<-

*...health, than to abound in temporal goods.*

**Geb.** You speak the truth; therefore I tell you that, according to the opinion of Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian humanist and philosopher who sought to reconcile Platonism with Christianity.</note> in the book *On the Threefold Life* <note>original: "libro delle tre uite," referring to *De vita libri tres*, a famous medical and philosophical treatise.</note>, the quintessence <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence" or element, believed to be the pure, celestial substance that makes up the heavens, distinct from the four earthly elements</term> is a spirit of the world soul <note>original: "anima del mondo," or *Anima Mundi*, the spirit believed to animate all nature.</note>, diffused throughout the corporeal and elemental parts.

**Dem.** This explanation is too obscure.

**Geb.** I have told you many times that in a single distillation, two vapors <note>original: "fumi," literally "smokes."</note> distill from our matter: namely, one red and the other white, and both are called quintessences. That red one, besides the names mentioned above, is called the soul of wine <term>soul of wine: "anima di uino," often used by alchemists to describe the concentrated spirit or "mercury" extracted from wine or other substances.</term> John of Rupescissa <note>Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366), a French Franciscan friar and alchemist famous for his work on the quintessence as a remedy for human ailments.</note> says: "We also call the soul of wine the quintessence, but our wine is the gold of the philosophers." The white vapor, which in distillation carries this red vapor in its belly <note>original: "uentre," also meaning womb or interior.</note>, is properly the true quintessence of which our philosophers speak. Arnald <note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311).</note> says: "Once the elements are separated, there remains a certain quintessence, which is called the spirit of the stone." This is not an element, nor does it have elemental qualities in act <term>in act: "in atto," a philosophical term meaning a quality is currently manifesting its physical effects.</term>, but rather in virtue <term>in virtue: "in uirtù," meaning it possesses the inherent power or potential to act, even if the physical sensation (like heat) is not present.</term>; because in act it is not hot and dry like fire, for it cools and heals hot illnesses. It is not hot and moist like air, because air easily becomes corrupt—as we see during times of plague, and in the generation of flies and spiders, which are created through the corruption of the air—but the quintessence of metals would last more than ten thousand years without corrupting, provided that, being placed in glass, it could not evaporate. Therefore Hermes, Plato, and Socrates, and many other philosophers who had knowledge of this medicine, said that it possessed perpetuity, and that...

<summary>Geber defines the quintessence as the "spirit of the world soul" and a non-corruptible substance capable of healing, citing authorities like Marsilio Ficino and John of Rupescissa. He distinguishes it from the four elements by its longevity and its ability to act "in virtue" rather than through physical heat or moisture.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, quintessence, world soul, distillation, soul of wine, John of Rupescissa, Arnald of Villanova, corruption, immortality, spirit of the stone</keywords>

->### ON GEBER ###<-

...were never meant to die, but because God had ordained otherwise, it happened to them as it does to all other men. <note>The text likely refers to the Biblical patriarchs or the first humans who, in alchemical and theological tradition, were thought to have potentially immortal bodies before the Fall or divine decree.</note>

<margin>Dem.</margin> *Then we shall use this medicine in vain.*

And among all the remedies, the best is <term>this quintessence</term> <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," a purified spirit thought to be distinct from the four earthly elements and capable of perfecting both metals and the human body.</term> prepared, as it is written in the tree of life <note>original: "legno della uita," likely referring to the alchemical "Arbor Vitae" or symbolic source of immortality.</note>, which is not moist and cold like water, because before coagulation it burns, and by the philosophers it is named burning water <note>original: "acqua ardente," an early term for distilled alcohol or spirits, which surprised medieval observers because it looked like water but was flammable.</note>, which is a property that does not belong to elemental water: And that it is not dry and cold like the earth, this is manifest because it is very active, and warms greatly, and heals melancholic infirmities <note>In the theory of the four humors, melancholy was considered a cold and dry state; the "warm and active" quintessence was thought to balance this.</note>, which are cold and dry, and it has the form of the most subtle water. This is named by the philosophers in many ways according to some property and similarity which it holds with diverse things—that is, water of life <note>original: "acqua di uita" or "aqua vitae."</note>, because it gives life to men. <term>Rhazes</term> <note>original: "Rasi." Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist highly respected in the Latin West.</note> in the *Light of Lights* <note>original: "lume de lumi," referring to the *Liber Lumen Luminum*, a famous alchemical text attributed to Rhazes.</note> says: "The water which descends from the greater stone is the water which by the philosophers is named water of life." It is named Heaven, because it is a simple quintessence, highly incorruptible, and separated from the coarse elemental matter. Mercurial water, because it is distilled from the mercury of metals; Solutive Mercury it is named, because it dissolves the bod-* <note>The catchword "cor-" suggests the next page begins with "corpi" (bodies).</note>

...enter into the composition of the <term>Elixir</term><term>Elixir: in alchemy, a transformative substance—often called the Philosopher's Stone—capable of changing base metals into gold or healing all illnesses.</term>, and therefore it is said that a single matter is common to them. It is true that the coagulating waters in this art are principally two sulfurs, namely *<term>Venus</term><term>Venus: the alchemical name and planetary correspondence for copper.</term>* and *<term>Mars</term><term>Mars: the alchemical name and planetary correspondence for iron.</term>*; and of these *<term>Albertus</term><term>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280): a German friar and philosopher whose "De Mineralibus" (On Minerals) was a foundational text for medieval and Renaissance alchemy.</term>* meant in the first book of *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine." abbreviated for "De Mineralibus"</note> in chapter 9 of the first treatise, saying: *Alchemists study above all to make waters which have the qualities of diverse elements in <term>virtue</term><term>virtue: here meaning "potential" or "inherent power" (Latin: in potentia), rather than being active or visible.</term>, and not in act, so that through them they may dry out and coagulate that which they wish to transmute.* <note>original: "Li alchimici sopra modo studiano di fare le acque, lequali hanno le qualità di diuersi elementi in uirtù, & non in atto, accioche per quelle disecchino, et coagulino quello che uogliono transmutare"</note> In the *Book of Vapors* it is written that one who wishes to cook meat quickly puts a piece of glass in the water with the meat: because the glass, being dry, draws so much moisture out of the meat through its boiling that the meat immediately remains cooked. But such meat is cooked even faster if one puts in glass made from the ashes of hay and fern, because that [glass] is less abundant in moisture; for the first kind of glass has some part of moisture which it took from lead, which was first in its composition.

**Dem.** I have not tried it, but I do not believe that by cooking meat with glass, it would be cooked any sooner.

**Geb.** This is the cause why many ignorant people want to claim that *<term>Pliny</term><term>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD): a Roman author whose "Natural History" was frequently quoted, and sometimes misinterpreted, by later alchemists.</term>* and *Albertus* were liars: because that which the wise have said by similitude, or by allegory, many take according to the letter, and therefore they find themselves deceived.

**Dem.** What, then, did he wish to signify by those things?

**Geb.** In this art, "glass" is sometimes used to signify <term>sulfur</term><term>sulfur: one of the "Three Primes" of alchemy; it represents the combustible, active, and masculine principle of matter.</term>, because just as common glass receives all colors, so too our sulfur, in the <insert>preparation</insert>—

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition following Geber’s dialogue, specifically elaborating on the metaphor of "cooking meat" mentioned at the end of the previous page. It explains the chemical process of fixing mercury using different types of sulfur and distinguishes between the goals of metallurgical transmutation (the Elixir) and medicinal healing (the Quintessence).</meta>

# THE EXPOSITION #

...it shows us all the colors, signified by the **eyes of Argus** <note>In Greek mythology, Argus was a giant with a hundred eyes; in alchemy, this refers to the *cauda pavonis* or "peacock's tail," a stage where the matter in the flask displays a shimmering array of colors.</note>, turned into a **peacock's tail**. By the "meat to be cooked," we mean our **mercury** <term>Mercury: Not just the liquid metal, but the "spirit" of matter that must be stabilized.</term> which is to be coagulated or fixed in the mercurial water by means of **sulfur**. And because there are two sorts of sulfurs, one of which is named **Mars** <term>Mars: The alchemical name for iron.</term> (hot and dry) and the other **Venus** <term>Venus: The alchemical name for copper.</term> (hot and dry, but less dry, as **Rhazes** <note>original: "Rasi"; refers to Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and one of the most important figures in the history of medicine and alchemy.</note> says), because it participates in the humidity of **quicksilver** <note>original: "argẽto uiuo"; another name for mercury.</note>. This humidity was taken from that salt called **lead**, from which the said Venus is distilled; therefore, that mercury and mercurial water coagulates and sweetens more quickly if one adds that sulfur of the "ashes of hay and fern"—that is, the aforementioned iron—because of the relationship with the name.

Note, however, that there is a difference between the **coagulation** of the **quintessence** and the **fixation** of the **Elixir**. This is because they are meant to cause different effects: the **Elixir** is intended to transmute imperfect metals into true gold and true silver, and therefore it must be fixed and converted into the substance of gold or silver that is "more than perfect." For this reason, the mercurial water does not remain within it, because it is an impediment to fixation, although the red quintessence remains, called **permanent water** <note>original: "acqua permanente"; a term for a stabilized, non-corrosive alchemical solvent.</note>. 

But because the **mercurial quintessence** is to be taken by mouth as a **medicine** that penetrates all the limbs and preserves them from corruption, it must not be so "fixed" that it converts into a golden or silver substance <note>The author warns that a medicine meant for the body should not be turned into literal, solid metal, which would be indigestible.</note>. Instead, it is enough that it be so well-cooked and digested that it coagulates, and that vaporous, foul, and sharp humidity is consumed, and it becomes sweet. And then, taken by mouth, it will easily—

<meta>The text cuts off at the catchword "mente," which completes the word "facilmente" (easily) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic "eyes of Argus" as the colorful peacock's tail stage of alchemy and distinguishes between the Elixir (for making gold) and the Quintessence (for medicinal use). It emphasizes that medicinal mercury must be "sweetened" and "coagulated" rather than fully hardened into metal.</summary>

<keywords>Argus, peacock's tail, mercury, sulfur, Mars, Venus, Rhazes, quintessence, Elixir, permanent water, medicinal alchemy, coagulation, fixation</keywords>

->**OF GEBER.**<-

...immediately penetrate throughout the entire body, as something most subtle and in harmony with the human body; and by its powerful and divine virtue, it will be able to make every place it penetrates so incorruptible that whoever takes three drops of it just once—either by itself or with something appropriate <note>Likely a reference to a "vehicle" or carrier liquid like wine or water.</note>—will live safely for some time, just as the ancient fathers did, who took of the **tree of life** <note>A biblical reference to the long-lived Patriarchs, suggesting the medicine acts as a restorative "Tree of Life."</note>. *Geber.* In this medicine there is the vegetable part, that is the **salt**, signified by the trees and herbs. There is also the animal part, that is the **soul and gold**, signified by animals, men, **Phoebus** <note>The sun god, representing alchemical gold.</note>, and by the **Golden Fleece**. The mineral part is that **fixed sulfur** called **Mars** <term>Mars: The alchemical name for iron, representing hardness and fixity.</term>, signified by stones, mountains, bones, and other hard things. The **mercurial water** and the vapors are signified by birds, winds, rivers, serpents, bulls, and dragons, which guarded the **Golden Fleece** dedicated to Mars; because in the 1st book, at chapter 15, I said that in distillation, none of the metals of the philosophers are submerged in the mercury—that is, the mercurial water—except for our **Sun** <note>Alchemical gold.</note>, and this is dedicated to Mars, because it must be joined with the sulfur called **Mars**. By **Orpheus** is signified our medicine, which, when it becomes sweet, then stops and coagulates all the aforementioned things.

*Democritus.* I certainly realize that I am not capable of understanding this science and these expositions of yours, unless first you do not...

*...explain to me what the "metals of the philosophers" are.*

**Geber.** The philosophers have hidden this science under the names of all the things in the world, and most especially under the names of metals.

**Democritus.** *How can they hide this art under the name of metals, if this work is actually made from metals?*

**Geber.** It is true that it is a very difficult thing to know how to discern when they are speaking of common metals, and when they are speaking of the "metals of the philosophers": because in appearance one would judge that they are always speaking of common metals. And sometimes they begin by speaking of common metals, and afterwards they continue by speaking of the metals of the philosophers. But he who has knowledge of this art, as soon as he hears a name, knows what it can signify in this art. For example, take the name **iron**, or **Mars** <term>Mars: The traditional alchemical and astrological name for iron.</term>: if the philosopher says that in its manifest [outward] form it is hot and dry, and that it is the "tincture of redness," the artist will easily recognize that he is speaking of **sulfur** <note>original: "solpho"</note>, because these qualities do not belong to common iron in its manifest state. **Razi** <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 864–925), a Persian physician and alchemist known in the West as Rhazes.</note>, in the *Book of Divinity*, says: "Know that things are so linked together by the subtle craftsmanship of nature that in every single thing, everything else exists potentially, even though it cannot be perceived by sight; this is best understood in liquefiable things <note>Meaning substances that can be melted or fused, such as metals.</note> more than in others: for the interiors of gold are silvery, and the interiors of silver are golden, because **Sol** <term>Sol: The Sun, representing alchemical Gold.</term> has been found within **Luna** <term>Luna: The Moon, representing alchemical Silver.</term>, and Luna within Sol. And in copper, there are gold and silver potentially and not visibly, and in those [are] iron, lead, and tin, and in the-"

### OF GEBER.

...silver and gold. This same thing is written by Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and saint who wrote extensively on minerals and alchemy.</note> in the third book of *On Minerals*, in chapter 8, saying that Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note>, Gilgil <note>Likely referring to an early alchemical authority cited in medieval texts, often associated with Arabic transmissions.</note>, Empedocles, and almost that entire multitude of alchemists, say that in every metal there are multiple species and natures of metals; one is occult <note>original: "occulta"; meaning hidden or internal</note> and the other is manifest <note>original: "manifesta"; meaning visible or external</note>, one is inside and the other is outside, and one is at the bottom and the other is on the surface (like those who have spoken of the "latency of forms" <note>A philosophical concept suggesting that the potential for a new substance exists hidden within the current one.</note>). And they said that everything is in everything, as it pleased Anaxagoras <term>Anaxagoras: An ancient Greek philosopher who proposed that all things contain a portion of everything else.</term>; they said that lead is gold on the inside and lead on the outside; and that gold is gold on the outside surface, but in its depths and on the inside, it is lead. And they say the same holds true between copper and silver, and nearly every metal to every other metal. 

And they say they do not call or name these "inside" and "outside," and the other designations, according to the physical placement of the parts within the whole, but according to the property and nature of the dominant or non-dominant element. For the dominant part holds closed and hidden within itself that which it dominates. In this way, these men directly state the opinion of Anaxagoras: that is, that all metals are in all others, and the naming is done according to the most dominant part.

**Democritus.** This seems impossible to me; and Albert, in the aforementioned place, rejects this opinion.

**Geber.** You are like those philosophers who used to say that there were no other substances in the world except those that could be seen. If you had examined, broken down, and transmuted metals, and had considered their intrinsic qualities, the diversity of their substances, and their apparent colors, you would not say that it seems impossible to you. ...it may seem to you that Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, mentioned on the previous page.</note> rejects that opinion; I say that he rejects that literal sense, but not the intention of the ancients, nor the allegorical sense.

**Dem.** Tell me then clearly the intention of the ancients and the true allegorical sense.

**Geb.** Through calcination <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances, reducing it to a powder or "calx."</term>, putrefaction <term>putrefaction: a stage of decay or breakdown in the alchemical process, often associated with the color black.</term>, and washing, we reduce our metal into a white salt, like common lime, and in several places I have called it lime <note>original: "calce"</note>. And because that substance has the properties of common lead, it is therefore called lead. Razi <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and highly influential figure in alchemy known in the West as Rhazes.</note> says in the aforementioned place that Saturn <note>The alchemical name for the metal Lead.</note> in its height <note>Meaning its external or manifest state.</note> is cold and dry—that is, lead and silver—black, soft, earthy, melancholic, bitter, foul-smelling, and feminine. But it is lead in act, and silver in potential <note>Using the Aristotelian concepts of "actuality" (what it is now) and "potentiality" (what it has the internal capacity to become).</note>. In its depth <note>Its internal or hidden nature.</note> it is hot and humid—that is, gold—airy, sanguine, saffron-colored, sweet, fragrant, and masculine. On one of its sides it is cold and humid—that is, tin and quicksilver—watery, phlegmatic, white, tasteless, mild, and feminine. On the other side it is hot and dry—that is, iron and copper—fiery, choleric, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. Behold then, that in a single common metal, and in a single philosopher's metal, there are all the metals of the philosophers; and we could say the same of the others.

**Dem.** These things seem to me very intricate and obscure.

**Geb.** In the preface of the second book, I said that the consideration of the things by which one reaches the completion of this work is also the consideration of the principles of bodies, according to their depth: the hidden and the manifest. ## OF GEBER

...[they] have three dimensions, namely, Height, depth, and width. Height is the manifest <note>The "manifest" refers to the external, observable qualities of a substance.</note>; Depth is the occult <note>The "occult" refers to the internal, hidden properties that are not immediately apparent.</note>; Width is the middle between the manifest and the occult—that is, part manifest and part occult. This substance, then, which is called lead, unprepared quicksilver <term>quicksilver: an archaic name for mercury.</term>, dry water, and pastille <note>original: "pastillo," likely referring to a small cake or medicinal-style tablet of processed material.</note>—about which I spoke in the chapter on descension, that is, distillation—ascends. Because in its manifest state it is white, it is therefore said to be cold, because whiteness is a sign of coldness; or it is said to be cold because it participates heavily in watery humidity, of which I have spoken in Book 2, chapters 2 and 7. 

And because it has much intrinsic earthiness, as I have said in several places when speaking of quicksilver and lead, it is therefore said to be of a dry nature. Thus, in Book 1, chapter 15, speaking of this mercury, I said: that it does not adhere—that is, intrinsically—to its sulfur called Mars <note>Mars is the alchemical name for Iron, but here "sulfur of Mars" refers to a specific reactive quality or reagent.</note>, even though it possesses a viscous humidity due to the dryness (that is, the earthiness) of that sulfur, which tempers that viscosity and does not permit it to adhere. 

Because of that earthiness, it is said to be of a melancholic <term>melancholic: one of the four traditional humors, associated with the element of earth, coldness, and dryness.</term>, livid, and black nature. But note that just as the aforementioned Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> is white, so too its intrinsic earthinesses before distillation are white, as I have said in the aforementioned place; and therefore in Book 2, chapter 7, I have compared them to talc and eggshells and similar things. But after distillation, they remain at the bottom of the flask as black; therefore, in Book 3, chapter 2, speaking of this mercury, I said that through slight artifice we see the black and foul earth emerge from it—

Nature, through sublimation <note>The process where a solid turns into a vapor and then back into a solid, used here to describe how minerals form in the earth's crust.</note>, leads that double smoke to mineral sites and joins it with the fixed sulfur; Art likewise joins that double smoke with the fixed sulfur. However, because that white smoke would be a great hindrance to fixation <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> for us, we therefore remove it, and in its place, we put that viscous, coagulated quicksilver which we have extracted from the burnt dregs. Therefore, in Book 2, chapter 6, I said that one should always separate that part which ascends as powder near the opening of the head of the aludel <note>A series of pear-shaped pots used as a condenser in the sublimation process.</note>—that is, the part dissolved in water—from that which remains prepared at the bottom of the flask. Nature washes that fixed sulfur; Art does the same.*

# THE EXPOSITION

*...you should no longer remove nor separate it; how then can the removal of sulfur <note>original: "solpho"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter.</note> be the cause of the variety of colors after the coagulation <note>The process where a liquid substance becomes solid or thickened.</note> of the Elixir?*

**Geb.** In sulfur (as Albert <note>referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and theologian</note> says in his book *On Minerals*), there are two combustible oilinesses <note>original: "ontuosita adustiue"; the "fatness" or "oil" of a substance believed to be responsible for its ability to burn.</note>: one is greater and almost extrinsic <note>external or superficial</note>, the other is lesser and intrinsic <note>internal or essential</note>. The first, which is red, is removed by washing, but the intrinsic oiliness is consumed after the coagulation of the Elixir itself. This Elixir first becomes black, and as that oily humidity—which is the cause of the blackness <term>nigredo: the "black stage" of the alchemical work, representing the death or putrefaction of the old matter</term>—is gradually consumed, it is said that the sulfur is removed. This is because it converts into perfect whiteness <term>albedo: the "white stage," representing purification and the creation of the silver-making stone</term>, then into a citron color, and ultimately into perfect redness <term>rubedo: the final "red stage," representing the completion of the Great Work and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</term>. Therefore, the sulfur twice becomes black, twice white, and twice red.

**Dem.** *How much time does it take to coagulate after the preparation of the sulfur and the quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo"; mercury.</note>?*

**Geb.** After they are placed together in their small flask <note>original: "boccietta"; a glass vessel</note> with a long neck and a round body, inside the ash-pot <note>original: "pignatta delle ceneri"; a vessel filled with ashes used as a "heat bath" to provide gentle, even warmth</note> over the fire of a lamp with five or six wicks <note>The number of wicks was a way to measure and control the intensity of the heat.</note>, it coagulates in twenty-one days or thereabouts. In the *Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original: "Specchio della alchimia"; a famous alchemical text often attributed to Roger Bacon</note> it is written: "Govern the matter with a slow fire, until the greater part is converted into black earth, which will occur in twenty-one days."

**Dem.** *What signs appear during that time?*

**Geb.** In the aforementioned place it is written: "As the matter feels the heat of the fire, it immediately dissolves into water, and then the fumes rise and descend through the long neck of the flask, and immediately above the water all the oil gathers like a black cloud." John of Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his work on "quintessence."</note> says, "I have..."

<summary>Geber explains how the internal "oiliness" of sulfur drives the color transitions of the Elixir from black to red and specifies a twenty-one-day heating period for coagulation.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, Albertus Magnus, coagulation, Elixir, nigredo, albedo, rubedo, quicksilver, alchemical flask, Mirror of Alchemy, John of Rupescissa</keywords>

it is said that once the calx <note>original: "calce"; the powdery residue or "lime" left after a substance has been heated or calcined.</note> is placed, a certain film will rise like oil. In the creation of the world as well, the spirit of the Lord was carried over the waters. After some days, the matter begins to coagulate <note>The process where a liquid substance becomes solid or thickened.</note> under the water, in the middle of the water, directly where the fire strikes, and little by little the coagulated matter grows, so much so that the earth begins to be seen dry, out of the water like a little island, and the water stays around it, and finally all the water together with the sulfurs shrink together black, coagulated, in the manner of an egg which holds a point at the bottom (as Master Leonello <note>Leonello da Vittori, an Italian physician and author on alchemy and medicine.</note> says).

**Dem.** How long does the blackness <term>nigredo: the "black stage" of the alchemical work, representing the death or putrefaction of the old matter</term> last, before it begins to turn white?

**Geb.** In the *Rosary* <note>referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical text</note> it is written: through the continuation of forty days, both one and the other water remain permanent, covered in blackness; if this blackness is properly governed, it will not last more than forty days.

**The** aforementioned things the ancients have hidden under the veil of poetic fables.

**Dem.** I marvel greatly that the philosophers have hidden this science in those chimeric fictions; I have read some doctors who have explained them according to a moral sense <note>the practice of interpreting myths as lessons in ethics or human behavior.</note>.

**Geb.** He who has no knowledge of this science cannot know the intention of the ancients, regarding what they wished to signify through the names of so many gods and goddesses, and through their generation, loves, and mutations. And do not think that in those fables they hid moral matters, because publicly with words and works in

# THE EXPLANATION

*they signaled the virtues and good morals.*

**Dem.** If you could help me understand these things, beyond my other obligations to you, I would never believe I could repay you in any amount of time.

**Geb.** Lest you think I am alone in this opinion, go read Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and theologian known for his works on natural science and alchemy.</note> in the first book *On Minerals* <note>original: "mine."; referring to *De mineralibus*.</note>, in chapter 4. He says that Empedocles <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who proposed the theory of the four elements.</note>, who lived much later than Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, confirmed that stones are generated by burning heat, taking this affirmation from the ancient fable called that of Pyrrha and Deucalion <note>In Greek mythology, the survivors of a great flood who repopulated the earth by throwing stones (the "bones" of Mother Earth) over their shoulders.</note>, in which the stones of the "great mother" are called bones. According to Empedocles, bones are composed of parts of Vulcan <note>original: "Volcano"; the Roman god of fire, here representing the element of fire itself.</note>.

**Dem.** It seems to me that the words of Albertus are not spoken to this purpose.

**Geb.** The stones which are generated by burning heat are the calces <note>The powdery residues left after a metal is heated; see previous page.</note> of our metal which are generated in the burning fire. And these, because of their hardness, are named "bones," and are composed of parts of Vulcan—that is, sulfur <note>original: "solpho"</note>, which is called fire—because in those things fire (that is, sulfur) predominates. And they are stones of the "great mother"—that is, the earth—because they are generated from metal in which there is a great quantity of earth, and the whole is a fixed earthy substance. Regarding Pyrrha and Deucalion, the Poets say that at the time of the flood <note>original: "diluuio"</note> they saved themselves upon a small boat. By the "flood," our "white waters" <term>white waters: the purified liquid state of the alchemical matter during the whitening stage</term> are signified. By Pyrrha and Deucalion together, that subtle sulfur is signified, which is called the Hermaphrodite <note>The "Hermaphrodite" represents the union of the active/male (sulfur) and passive/female (mercury) principles into a single, balanced substance.</note>. These are saved upon the small boat—that is, in that film which comes over the water like oil. In the Ark of Noah as well, human generation was saved from the flood, along with all the animals.

<summary>Geber explains how alchemical processes are hidden within ancient myths, identifying the 'bones' of Pyrrha and Deucalion as metallic calces and the Great Flood as the stage of white waters in the Great Work.</summary>

### OF GEBER.

...*were saved, both the male and the female. By the stones which Pyrrha threw, which were converted into women, is signified that **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: an older name for mercury, considered one of the two primary principles of all metals</term> which remains fixed and earthy in the flask after distillation. By the stones which Deucalion threw, which were converted into men, is signified that **coarse sulfur** <term>sulfur: along with mercury, the other essential principle in alchemy, representing heat and action</term> called **Mars** <note>original: "marte"; in alchemical shorthand, Mars represents iron or a particularly aggressive, "fiery" quality of sulfur.</note>. Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century scholar and patron saint of scientists.</note> also says in the first book *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine."; referring to the treatise *De mineralibus*.</note>, in chapter 8 of the second treatise, that the fable of the **Gorgon** <note>Gorgon: Medusa, the mythological figure whose gaze turned onlookers to stone.</note> says she turned all those who looked at her into stone. They have named the power of minerals 'Gorgon'; the looking upon her, they say, is the disposition of the humors <note>humors: the fluids or elemental balances within a substance.</note> of the bodies toward the stone-making power.*

<margin>Dem.</margin> *This explanation is more obscure than the text itself.*

<margin>Geb.</margin> The minerals are the sulfurs; their strong power is the metallic, hardening, and fixing power. The "looking" of the vapors of the metallic bodies toward that mineral power occurs when the fumes have risen above the long neck of the **flask** <term>flask: the glass vessel used in distillation, also called a cucurbit or "boccia"</term>, where they "look" upon one another and return back to that mineral power, which hardens and converts those vapors into the most noble **philosopher’s stone** <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life.</note>. *Also, Lot’s wife, because she looked back while fleeing the fire—against the command of God—was therefore turned into a pillar of salt. They also say that Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods.</note>, being in love with Ganymede, turned himself into an **eagle**, and having snatched him away, carried him to heaven.* What I have said above regarding that double smoke sufficiently explains this fable. ->### THE EXPLANATION<-

...**eagle**, because it flies above the other spirits. In the *Book of Alums and Salts* <note>original: "lib. delli alumi & sali"; likely referring to a common medieval alchemical compendium attributed to Rhazes or an anonymous author.</note> it is written: *The governance of the atrament <term>atrament: a term for vitriol or dark mineral substances containing sulfur</term> (that is, sulfur) is with the eagle, as Geber says, that is, with sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to make metals volatile so they can "fly" or sublimate</term>.* By **Ganymede**, who was so beautiful, is understood that sulfur called "gold." It is said that **Jupiter** is in love with him because they are naturally united together in one substance; therefore, in the **distillation** <term>distillation: the process of heating a substance to create vapor and then condensing it back into liquid</term>, he carries him with him into Heaven—that is, into the **head of the flask** <note>original: "capello della boccia"; the top part of the distilling apparatus, also called an alembic</note> which is shaped like the heavens. Virgil also writes in the sixth book <note>Referring to the *Aeneid*.</note> that **Daedalus**, together with his son, shut within the labyrinth, made wings of feathers and fixed them with wax to himself and his son, and with these they flew out of the labyrinth through the air; but **Icarus**, flying too high, fell into the sea and drowned there because the heat of the Sun melted the wax.

<margin>Dem.</margin> The explanation of this fable would be very pleasing to me.

<margin>Geb.</margin> **Daedalus** in Greek means "varied" in Latin <note>original: "uario in latino"; the name Daedalus is associated with the Greek word *daidallein*, meaning "to work cunningly" or "to variegate."</note>, and it signifies that sulfur called **Mars** <note>Mars: alchemical symbol for iron, or a "fiery" reactive sulfur.</note>, which varies from color to color and from nature to nature. This is the father of that other subtle and fusible sulfur signified by **Icarus**, since **arsenic** is the subtle and fusible part of Mars. By the **labyrinth** where they are enclosed, one may understand the flask; but it is better to say it signifies the "vile" **philosophers' stone** <note>original: "uile pietra de philosophi"; before it is perfected, the raw material of the Stone was often described as "vile" or "lowly."</note> in which the aforementioned sulfurs are enclosed. The wings with which they fly out and **sublimate** <term>sublimate: to heat a solid until it turns into a gas and then solidifies again, a process of "purifying" or "lifting" the substance</term> are those of which I have spoken in the second book, chapter 10, saying: *The bodies which require the administration of a lifting substance are **Venus** <note>Venus: alchemical symbol for copper.</note> and **Mars**, because of the slowness of their fusion—that is, solution. Venus requires **tutia** <term>tutia: zinc oxide, used as a flux to help metals melt or react</term>, and Mars requires arsenic.*

Along with this salt, it also draws out the subtle **sulfur** called **Venus**, which is enclosed in the depths of that salt. I have written of these things in the third book at the end of chapter 7, when speaking of the **elevation of bodies** <note>the process of turning solid metals into volatile "spirits"</note> with spirits; and in chapter 15, when speaking of the elevation of Venus with tutty.*

...that sulfur called gold; and because it does not distill all at once, but little by little, one drop after another, as the first is driven out by the violence of the fire, another similar golden one is born. For this reason, it is said that they fell into the sea—that is, into that water of salt—from which salt and sulfur Venus was generated, as I have said many times before.

Thus, it is said that he held her by her garments because he is joined with her and her salt only externally; nevertheless, during the separation, he leaves his seed—that is, that subtle sulfurous part—in the earth of that salt. From this distillation is born that sulfur named above as **Ganymede**, **Apollo**, and **Phoebus**. And she was "left in peace," meaning she was removed, because she is an impediment to **fixation** <term>fixation: the process by which a volatile, fluid substance is made stable and resistant to fire</term>.

->Therefore it is said that she was born before **Jove** <note>Jupiter, representing the metal tin</note>.<-

And this I intended to signify in Book 1, Chapter 11, when, having spoken of the preparation of Jove, I said that in **Saturn** <note>representing the metal lead</note> there was the same mode of preparation, with the mocking of those who are not our children <note>the uninitiated or "false" alchemists</note>, only by throwing away that which does not break the bodies—that is, throwing away the "earthiness," which does not break, meaning it does not dissolve the **sulfurous bodies**—and throwing away the **mercurial water**, which returns to the body more quickly than Jove, from its **calx** <term>calx: the powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been burned or calcined</term>, that is, from its white salt, like true lime. They say **Juno** presides over childbirth because through **distillation** she leads our **Phoebus** <note>the Sun, representing gold</note> to the light; for this reason she was called **Lucina** <note>the Roman goddess of light and childbirth</note>. She is over marriages because she is the means of joining the **sulfurous tinctures** <term>tinctures: the internal "souls" or coloring principles of metals</term>—namely, **Venus** <note>copper</note> and **Mars** <note>iron</note>—and she is signified by the finest net of **Vulcan** made of steel <note>referring to the myth where Vulcan traps Venus and Mars in a net; here it represents a chemical bond</note>. **Morienus** <note>a legendary 7th-century alchemist</note> used to say that the soul (that is, Venus) does not join with the body (that is, the **sulfur** called Mars) except by means of the spirit, which is this spiritual water. She is said to be the wife of Jove because both together generate our **Sun** <note>the philosophical gold</note>, or because they are joined together before distillation. She is the Queen of the Gods of the Gentiles <note>the Pagan gods</note>, who signify our **metals**; for she rules them, and through her they are born, dissolved, and refined, separated, joined, **mortified** <note>the process of "killing" or fixing a substance</note>, **vivified** <note>the process of "bringing to life" or restoring a substance</note>, and made to bear fruit; for this reason she is called the Goddess of Wealth.
