# OF GEBER

...[Let us re]turn to speaking of which metal the aforementioned things are made, which, having to be made of a single metal and not being able to be made of common gold.
I certainly believe they are made of **silver** <note>original: 'argento'</note>, because you have said that we must take that gold which is hidden in the depths of metals. And because the **Moon** <term>Luna: the alchemical name for silver</term> is gold in its depths—as you said in the *Testament*—we must therefore take that. You also said in the first book at chapter 18 that the Moon is the **tincture of whiteness** <note>original: 'albedine'; the alchemical stage of purification and whitening</note>. Many philosophers say the Moon is the **ferment of whiteness**, and physicians also use it in certain medicines. **Albertus Magnus** <note>a 13th-century scholar and saint known for his writings on alchemy and natural history</note> also says at the end of the third book of *Minerals*: "Let it not be hidden from us that in all things which share a circular generation <note>the philosophical concept that elements transform into one another in a cycle</note>, the transition is easier for those that share a greater affinity." For this reason, gold is more easily made from silver than from any other metal, because in silver one need not change anything except the color and the weight. These things are easily done because, once the watery humidity is consumed, the substance becomes more **fixed** <note>stable and resistant to the heat of the fire</note> and the weight increases; once the good **citrine sulfur** <note>original: 'solpho citrino'; a yellow sulfur used to 'color' or 'ripen' silver into gold</note> is added, the color will consequently be changed. And this method is also found in the others.

<margin>Geber</margin> I tell you that if we had to prepare the entire golden or silver substance without reducing them to their **prime nature** <term>the original, raw state of matter before it becomes a specific metal</term>, they would be better than the others; however, this method is of little use. It is true that according to that **short way** <note>a reference to the 'Short Path' or 'Dry Way' of alchemy, intended to reach the goal more quickly</note> mentioned above, gold is more easily made from silver than from any other metal. ### THE EXPLANATION

I have said in the second book at chapter 18 that the weight increases, and by adding even the smallest amount of our good prepared sulfur—which is outwardly white, but inwardly yellow and red—and by decocting <note>original: 'decotti'; the process of heating or boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its essence</note> them together for many days, the color will consequently change. This is because the tincture hidden within the sulfur will reveal itself through the power of the heat. This method also applies to the other metals. 

But if we wish to speak of making the **Elixir**, according to the "long way" of nine months—that is, by reducing the metal to its **first matter** <term>materia prima: the original, primitive substance from which all elements were thought to originate</term>—I say that it cannot be said that gold or silver are better than the others. This is because neither the first substance nor the first qualities remain in them, except for the internal ones, which are common to all metals. 

It is true that (as I have said) an imperfect metal, while in its mine, has acquired certain substances and properties which, once prepared, make it closer to our art than gold or silver, as you will come to understand. And responding to your arguments, I say that although the philosophers many times mean our **Jupiter** <term>Giove: the alchemical name for tin</term>, **Mercury**, and **Venus** <term>Venere: the alchemical name for copper</term> when they say "the Moon," nonetheless, by "the Moon" one can also understand that salt called **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for lead</term>. This salt, in its depths, is the "gold of the philosophers" (as I have said above). 

By "the Moon" one can also understand the sulfur prepared white like the moon. In its depths, this is silver and gold, and it is the tincture and ferment of **whiteness** and **redness** <note>original: 'albedine & rubedine'; referring to the *albedo* and *rubedo* stages, which are the second and final stages of the Great Work of alchemy</note> upon its mercury. This silver hardens and **fixes** <note>makes stable and resistant to fire</note> our Jupiter, converting it to its metallic nature, and it mixes with our **Sun** <term>Sole: the alchemical name for gold</term>, as I have said in the first book at chapter 18. By "the Moon" one can also understand the white **Elixir**, and this is the tinc-

...whiteness, and it is the ferment of whiteness in the multiplication <note>The process of increasing the potency or quantity of the alchemical agent</note>, and in its secret it is gold, and is easily converted into a golden **Elixir**. This is because by continuing the fire, the matter becomes more fixed, the weight increases, and the red sulfurous tincture—which was hidden in the depths—manifests itself. <margin>Geb.</margin> According to the reasoning of Roger Bacon <note>original: 'Roggiero baccone'; a 13th-century English philosopher and scientist often associated with early alchemy</note>, which I cited above when speaking of common gold, there is no doubt at all. This is because this science treats imperfect bodies insofar as it is suitable to make them perfect, as I stated in the preface of the *Book of Investigation*. Callisthenes <note>A figure from antiquity often cited in alchemical texts as an authority</note>, also a leader in this science, says that alchemy is the science which attributes the nobility of the superior metals to the inferior ones. Saint Thomas <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas; though his authorship of alchemical texts is debated by modern scholars, Renaissance readers believed he wrote on the subject</note>, at the beginning of the fourth book of the *Metaphysics*, says: "It belongs to alchemists only to transmute metals (that is, the imperfect ones) according to the truth, and not according to sophistication <note>original: 'sophisticatione'; referring to the creation of alloys that merely look like gold but are not truly transmuted</note>."

<margin>Dem.</margin> I believe that with the **Elixir**, the imperfect metals are made perfect through **projection**. <term>Projection: the final act of casting the alchemical medicine onto base metals to transform them</term>

<margin>Geb.</margin> I say that by preparing those metals without the addition of any foreign substance, they become a perfect **Elixir**. And I have stated this clearly in the preface of the *Book of Investigation*, and in the chapter concerning the preparation of the **sharpest vinegar**. <note>original: 'aceto acerrimo'; a powerful alchemical solvent, often used to dissolve or "open" metals</note>

# THE EXPLANATION

In the first book, again in chapter 8, I have said that in purifying metals (meaning the imperfect ones) through the ingenuity and craftsmanship of man, they are reduced to their pure and most noble substance in a short time—a feat which nature cannot accomplish. In the *Testament* <note>Referring to the *Testamentum*, a famous alchemical text often attributed to Ramon Llull</note> I have said that every metal (that is, an imperfect one) can be converted through calcination <term>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder</term> not only into salt, but into true **Sun** <note>original: 'sole'; gold</note> and true **Moon** <note>original: 'luna'; silver</note>. Here you cannot say that I meant the perfect metals, because those are already true gold and true silver; nor is this achieved by projection <note>The act of throwing the Philosopher's Stone onto a base metal to transmute it</note>, because this specific transformation is done through calcination.

In the *Book of Investigation*, in the chapter on vitriol, I have said that from imperfect bodies are extracted various vitriols necessary to us. I have said "various vitriols" because in this art, not only that alum <term>Alum: a specific type of chemical salt used as a mordant and in purification</term> but also the sulfurs are called vitriols. In the fourth book at chapter 9, speaking of the medicine <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone</note> which tints the **Moon** into gold, I said that this is done following the known and certain administration of that same imperfect body. 

And if you have studied my *Book of Roots*, I have said: Having seen the imperfect bodies prepared through our experience and ingenuity, and cleansed of every superfluous corruption, we have found them to be of greater clarity and splendor than the naturally perfect bodies; by this consideration, we have arrived at the end of this work and science. In the *Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original: 'Specchio della alchimia'; likely referring to the *Speculum Alchemiae*</note> it is written: If we can find the matter in which there is clean, clear quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals</term>, white or red, frozen in a mass but not yet brought to completion, such that with our natural ingenuity and our artificial fire we can reach the innermost...

<summary>The author cites various alchemical authorities and his own previous works to argue that imperfect metals (like lead or copper) can be refined through human artifice to exceed the purity of natural gold and silver.</summary>

You shall then taste that delightful thing, which is the ultimate goal of the philosophers. Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and influential scholar who wrote extensively on alchemy and minerals</note> also says in the 5th book of *On Minerals*, in chapter 1: "It is characteristic of all metals (that is, the imperfect ones) that they appear to be incomplete in their species, and are therefore convertible into all things. This is because the 'mean' <term>Mean: an intermediate substance or state that exists between two opposites</term>, properly speaking, has a formless nature, while the 'extremes' <term>Extremes: the final, perfected states of a substance, such as gold or the Elixir</term> retain a formed and distinct nature. In the mean, the extremes are found in a confused or blended state; therefore, by ingenuity and nature, the extremes are drawn out from the means when the power of one extreme is joined to the other." We must, therefore, pay the highest attention to the nature of these means for the transmutation of metals, because within them lies much of the knowledge regarding those substances we strive to convert into one another.

<margin>Demogorgon</margin> I cannot imagine what Albert intends by these "means," "extremes," and the "virtues" of the extremes.

<margin>Geber</margin> By "mineral means" in this context, Albert understands the **imperfect metals**, because they are on the path toward perfection. He does not mean that any perfect metal can be produced from them by nature alone, but rather that through the alchemical art, the most perfect extremes can be extracted from them—namely, the **White Elixir** and the **Red Elixir** <note>The White Elixir transmutes base metals into silver, while the more potent Red Elixir transmutes them into gold</note>. Because the power of these extremes resides in the sulfurs and the *quicksilver* <term>Quicksilver: mercury; in alchemy, it represents the fluid, feminine principle of matter</term>, when the sulfur is joined with the quicksilver of the imperfect metals, the extremes are created: that is, the White and Red Elixirs. For this reason, in the first book at chapter 16, I have said—

**Geb.** I reject that method if taken literally, because I was not speaking of common bodies, but of the metals of the philosophers. To be brief, then, I conclude for you that the Elixir, the Quintessence, and Potable Gold <note>A legendary medicinal form of gold believed to grant eternal youth or cure all diseases</note> are made from common imperfect metals, because through artifice they can be assisted toward perfection. They can easily be reduced to the First Matter <term>First Matter: the primordial, formless substance from which all elements were believed to originate</term> because they contain within themselves all the causes of corruption, which I have written about in the third book, chapter 6. And the "accidents" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or smell) that can change without changing the core essence of a thing</note> that have come upon the First Matter can easily be removed, and the undigested parts can be digested.

**Dem.** You said that the aforementioned things are made from one alone

<margin>Geber</margin> It is true that the aforementioned things are in all metals, both perfect and imperfect; however, we must understand the nature of all common metals, their properties, and which are easy or difficult to prepare. We must also know which are near to or distant from the Art <term>The Art: alchemy, specifically the "Great Work" of transmuting base metals into gold</term>, and which are pure or corrupted at their root, as I stated at the end of the first book.

Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure considered the founder of alchemy</note> also says that in Saturn <note>The alchemical name for the planet and metal Lead</note> the natures are joined with their completion—that is, earth, water, air, and fire—and with it, the doors of the sciences are opened. Rhazes <note>An influential Persian physician and alchemist, known in Latin as Rasis</note> says that in lead, the Sun <term>The Sun: the alchemical symbol for Gold</term> and the Moon <term>The Moon: the alchemical symbol for Silver</term> exist in potential, though not to the sight, and that its tincture <note>The substance or energy capable of changing the color and nature of a metal</note> cannot be separated, and that it is both the White and Red Elixir, and its "water" retains mercury. Pythagoras says that the entire secret lies in lead. Hermes, speaking of this again, says: "I know of nothing more possible, nor more precious than lead, in the white and red work." For this reason, we see many who seek to extract quicksilver from lead.

### THE EXPLANATION

<meta>catchword: za (completing the word "vigilanza" or vigilance)</meta>

**Geber.** Many times I have warned you that when the philosophers speak so openly, you should consider that they speak through equivocation <note>original: "equiuocatione"; meaning the use of ambiguous language to hide a secret meaning</note>, or through similitude and allegory. In Book 1, Chapter 19, I stated that some fools think and say that common lead, in its own nature, closely approaches gold. However, if the philosophers have praised lead, they intended the "lead of the philosophers" <note>The "lead of the philosophers" is a symbolic substance, distinct from the common metal, containing the "seed" of the Work</note>, in which all the aforementioned properties are found.

**Demogorgon.** I will say then that since it is not found in common lead, the Elixir <term>Elixir: the substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold or silver</term> must be extracted from common tin. For in Book 4, Chapter 18, you said: "We have considered, through the examination of this mastery, that Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term> approaches the greatest perfection in the work of the major order." And in Book 1, Chapter 22, you said that among all the bodies, Jupiter is transformed into a more splendid, more lucid, and more perfect solar and lunar body <note>solar and lunar bodies refer to gold and silver</note>. Furthermore, in Book 3, Chapter 9, you also said: "It is manifest that Jupiter very greatly approaches the perfect, because it participates more in perfection." And in Book 1, Chapter 20, you said that Jupiter is the most perfect among the imperfect bodies, because it is akin to the Sun and the Moon. Rhazes <note>original: "Rasi"; a famous Persian alchemist and physician</note> also says that tin is the white tincture of copper, and that from it is made a sharp water <note>original: "acqua acuta"; a corrosive acid or solvent used to dissolve metals</note> which retains mercury. And you, in the *Book of Investigation* <note>original: "libro della inuesti."</note>, have said that we must honor the "water of Jupiter," because it is that which we seek for the white work <note>original: "al bianco"; the stage of alchemy called the *albedo*, which results in silver</note>.

**Geber.** You have managed to find authorities that seem to support the art, but you should have studied my works more closely.

**Demogorgon.** I wish I had understood them as well as I have with much vigilance—

### ->THE EXPOSITION<-

*...humanity accompanies the male with the female, so that through the union of these, human generation might quickly multiply; so too with the Elixir, because through many projections <note>Projections: the final stage of the Great Work, where a small amount of the Elixir or "Stone" is cast upon a mass of molten base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</note> it would soon be exhausted, and it would be necessary to begin again from the start with great effort and a long time. Therefore, wishing to shorten the preparation and fixation <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it remains solid and does not evaporate when heated</term>, it is necessary to mix one part of the fixed Elixir with the female—that is, with our quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> called Jove <term>Jove: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>, which is not fixed and not made of earth, like sulfur. And because these have a natural affinity, they unite easily, and in a short time multiply in power and quantity. And thus, I said above that Jove is related to the Sun <term>the Sun: alchemical Gold</term> and to the Moon <term>the Moon: alchemical Silver</term>, and that it approaches the greatest perfection, and the perfect, and that it transforms into a more brilliant and more perfect lunar and solar body.*

*In the preface of the second book, I said that the consideration of the thing which ultimately causes the perfection of the Elixir in multiplication <note>Multiplication: the process of increasing the quantity and the transmuting power of the alchemical medicine</note> is the choice of the pure substance of quicksilver. And the Elixir is a medicine which has its origin from the matter of the quicksilver of the philosophers <note>Philosophical quicksilver is a purified, "living" essence of mercury, distinguished by alchemists from the common mercury found in shops</note>. And this substance, which is currently a medicine, is not quicksilver in the nature of quicksilver, because it has been transmuted and fixed; nor is it entirely generated from quicksilver, but also from sulfur, although it was a part of quicksilver which, being transmuted, is no longer of its [original] nature—that is, when that part of it has been made into the philosophers' stone.*

*In the Book of Vapors it is written: The practitioners of alchemy use borax <term>borax: a mineral salt used here as a "flux" to help metals melt and join together more easily</term> in the multiplication of their metal: Through borax...*

# OF GEBER.

...is understood to be that prepared salt, called mercury and Jove <term>Jove: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>. And if you remain in doubt that this was my intention regarding the first, second, and third orders, go and read the recapitulation of the *Summa* <note>original: "recapitulatione della summa," referring to Geber’s famous work, *The Sum of Perfection*</note>, and consider what I have said regarding the sublimation of the first degree, through which the stone <term>the stone: the Philosophers' Stone, the goal of the alchemical work</term> is cleansed of corrupting impurity, and becomes pure and volatile. After this, it is fixed, and this is called the second degree of preparation. The third degree of the administration of the stone is that you make the fixed stone volatile, and the volatile fixed, and this is the multiplication.

<margin>The three orders of the work: preparation, fixation, and multiplication.</margin>

Thus, see that the first order is the preparation, the second the fixation, and the third the multiplication. And just as the Elixir—because it is fixed and of gold or silver—fixes and transmutes our mercury in a few days, so too our washed sulfur, because it is partly fixed and possesses a metallic nature and splendor, fixes our quicksilver in a short time. However, the volatile without the fixed could never be fixed into metal. The aforementioned quicksilver and Jove are extracted from the burnt dross of the salt through washing. *Rasi* <note>Rhazes (865–925 AD), a Persian polymath and influential figure in the history of medicine and alchemy</note> in the *Light of Lights*, says: *When the dross becomes white, it is also named magnesia, and tin, and foam of Jove.* *Mireris* <note>likely a reference to an obscure or corrupted name of an Arabic or Greek alchemical authority</note> the philosopher says: *Take the ash of which the philosophers have spoken, saying: Do not despise the ash existing in the lower part of the vessel, for in it is the diadem of the heart.* The same person mentioned above says: *In the dross is that which you seek.* *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts are attributed</note>, speaking of the distillation of Saturn <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for Lead</term>, says: *Take that and raise it in the tabernacle <term>tabernacle: a specific part of a distilling apparatus, or the vessel itself</term>, until such time as all the vapor con—*

## OF GEBER

...[necessa]ry <note>original: "rio," completing the word "necessario" or "ingegnario" from the previous page</note> to endeavor that as soon as a part is reduced into the cap <note>the "capello" or head of the distillation apparatus</note>, it should be removed from the fire by the nose of the alembic and descend into the receiver in the cooling place. In the *Book of Investigation* <note>original: *De Investigatione Perfectionis*, a foundational work attributed to Geber</note>, in the chapter on the preparation of Jove <term>Jove: the alchemical name for Tin</term>, I said: "The pure and clean body will descend, while all the earthy substance remains with the glass and salts, or alums." This statement can have two meanings, just as our [materials] consist of two bodies: namely, sulfur and arsenic.

If we wish to understand the "body" as sulfur, I say that in its washing and preparation, it first ascends in those thin films; and then it descends pure and clean from 그 combustible oiliness <term>combustible oiliness: original *ontuosita adustiua*, referring to the impure, fatty substances in raw minerals that the alchemist must "burn away" or separate</term>. But if by "body" we mean arsenic—that is, that subtle sulfur which is hidden in that salt—I say that by distillation it descends into the receiver; and then with the glass (that is, with the flask) and with the salts or alums, all the earthy substance remains in the bottom of the flask, and that salt is [then] prepared Jove.

Returning then to speak of imperfect metals, I say that for the aforementioned reasons it is concluded that for Venus <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term> and Mars <term>Mars: the alchemical name for Iron</term> in common, although they have much accidental earthiness and sulfurity, which can easily be re[moved]... <note>The text ends mid-word, to be continued on the next page.</note>

...to move, nevertheless in their root they are clean, and their sulfur and quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury</term>, being pure and clean, can be sublimated; because they have fixed parts joined with natural ones at the most minute level; as I have said in the second book, the second chapter. *Dem.* This statement does not seem to me to be relevant to the current topic?

*Geb.* That can have two meanings. One is that if we wish to understand this as referring to common metals, it means that the whitening—that is, the quicksilver which is the "white medicine" of its sulfur—and the reddening—that is, the sulfur of Venus and Mars, which is the tincture of redness for that quicksilver called "moon"—are pure in their root. But if by Venus and Mars we wish to understand the aforementioned sulfurs extracted from one of them, I say that after the putrefaction <term>putrefaction: a stage in alchemy where the matter is broken down, often turning black, to allow for new growth or purification</term> they are impure due to the combustible oiliness <term>combustible oiliness: original *ontuosita adustiua*, referring to the impure, fatty substances in raw minerals that the alchemist must "burn away" or separate</term>. And although they are red, that redness does not occur with the medicine of the first order <note>the "first order" refers to the initial, basic preparation or stage of the alchemical medicine</note>—that is, through the first preparation—with the appearance of splendor; because they are impure and not fit to receive the radiance of the redness. And because through washing <note>alchemical "washing" or *ablutio* involves cleansing the matter with liquids to remove surface impurities</note> that first combustible redness is removed, and beneath that impure redness there is pure whiteness, therefore their whitening is pure. Having been changed from that first nature and fixed by long decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or to mature it</term>, they become the pure reddening of the moon—that is, of the White Elixir, or of its quicksilver.

### THE EXPLANATION

Take it, therefore, above all imperfect bodies—that is, above the sulfur called Mars <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term>—because this is called gold in the lesser and middle work; that is, in the preparation, because it is more easily prepared. And "middle" refers to fixation <note>The original *fissione* here likely refers to *fissazione* (fixation), the process of making a volatile substance stable under the heat of fire</note>, because being more useful and more pure, it is therefore more easily and more perfectly transmuted and better fixed. Regarding that other opinion, that nature converts copper into gold: you do not have good evidence if you believe that nature could do this on its own. For since the pure radical part is mixed in the smallest degree with the impure—even if the impurity is accidental—one could not wash away the superfluities without also washing away and separating the pure part. Therefore, I tell you that the mine of our copper is that salt mentioned above, Saturn <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for lead; here likely referring to lead acetate or "sugar of lead"</term>, from which, through distillation, the water emerges which carries with it our thinnest copper. **Geb.** I have said in Book 1, chapter 15: That quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury, considered by alchemists to be the essential "spirit" or fluid component of all metals</term> adheres easily to all metals, but to Mars in no way, except by artifice; from this certainly choose and take the greatest secret. From this—that is, from fixed sulfur, by which in this place I mean common iron, because the greater part of it is fixed and earthy sulfur, as I have said in Book 3, chapter 6. And the naming of a substance is based on its predominant element. In Book 4, chapter 14, having spoken of Mars, I say: Through this certainly the industry of the craftsman is expanded toward the true rectification <term>rectification: the process of purifying a substance through repeated distillation or refinement</term> of any body. fixed, that is, more fixed sulfur than all other metals.
**Dem.** *The sulfur of gold is more fixed.*
**Geb.** *Yes, but it costs dearly: and there is no profit in working upon it, nor can it be improved, because it has reached its ultimate perfection. But the sulfur of iron is on the path to ultimate perfection and tincture <term>tincture: in alchemy, a substance capable of infusing or changing the color and quality of a metal, often synonymous with the Elixir</term>, and with our mastery, it can be helped to reach permanent redness <note>original: "rubedine permanente," referring to the rubedo, the final red stage of the alchemical process signifying success</note>, and we can easily obtain a good quantity of it with little expense. **Geb.** *I do not say that the earthiness of iron is good in this work; but I say that the spirits born and nourished in the earthiness of iron are better and more virtuous than the spirits nourished in the little earth and other elements of the other metals. And this is proven by Raimundo <note>Ramon Llull, the influential Majorcan philosopher and alchemist previously mentioned</note> in the book *Of the Secrets of Nature*, speaking of iron under the name of the terrestrial element, and saying: The earth, by virtue of the rays of the celestial bodies which continually flow into it, contains three principal parts—that is, animal, vegetable, and mineral—and it is productive of things so wondrous <note>original: "terribili," which in this context implies things that inspire awe or are formidable in their power</note> that they cannot be described, a thing which does not happen to the other elements. And the more a thing descends into the terrestrial element, the more it grows in celestial virtue.*

<summary>In this dialogue, Geber argues that iron is more useful for alchemy than gold because gold is already "perfect," while iron contains a potent, "fixed" spirit that can be brought to perfection (the red stage) through art. He explains that the dense earthiness of iron serves as a powerful vessel for celestial influences.</summary>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of why iron is the most suitable base metal for the Great Work. Geber is addressing the objection that iron's excessive "earthiness" makes it impure, arguing instead that this very quality allows its "spirits" to be more potent and more easily purified than those of other metals.</meta>

->OF GEBER.<-

<sig>H iiii</sig>

Therefore, the waters and spirits, which are immersed in the terrestrial element, due to their similarity to natural matter, imitate and draw upon the properties and virtues of that matter and location. Because iron partakes more of the earth than other metals do, its waters and spirits are more virtuous <term>virtuous: in this context, possessing great natural power or medicinal potency</term> than those of other metals. But just as a grain of wheat falling into the earth does not bear fruit unless it dies, so too the spirits and living seed of gold, which have fallen into the "earth" of iron, will be without fruit unless they die through putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the alchemical stage where matter decays and blackens, necessary for rebirth</term> and decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating a substance in liquid to extract its essence</term>. 

Another reason can be given for why iron is better in this work, despite having much earthiness: it is because those earthy parts are better and more easily separated in iron than in other metals, as they are not well-mixed and have no affinity or agreement with the radical <term>radical: the fundamental, "root" moisture of a metal that cannot be destroyed by fire</term> part. 

And this is confirmed by Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century scholar and patron saint of scientists, who wrote extensively on alchemy and minerals</note> in the fourth book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "methau.", referring to Albert's commentaries on Aristotelian natural philosophy</note>, in the fourth treatise, chapter five, saying: Iron has much earth, and is marvelous in its earthiness; for although water suffers more easily from fire than earth does, nevertheless, in iron, the earth dissolves from the mixture and burns away sooner than the substance that is more watery. And the cause of this is because the earthy part in the composition of iron is muddy <note>original: "lutulento," implying a filthy or impure sludge</note>, not well purified, nor well blended. 

The sign of this is that it easily becomes rusty, and when it is burned in the fire, many scorched earthinesses <note>original: "terrestrità aduste," referring to the dross or slag that falls away when iron is forged</note> fall from it, because its earthy part is porous, and the fire can burn it from every side: as I have also said in the third book, in the chapter...

<summary>Geber argues that iron is the ideal alchemical subject because its impurities are not deeply integrated, allowing them to be easily purged by fire to release potent internal spirits. He uses the metaphor of a germinating seed to explain how the "seed of gold" must undergo decay within the iron to produce alchemical fruit.</summary>

# OF GEBER

...times, as it agrees better with Mars <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term> and its earthiness <term>earthiness: the solid, impure residue within a substance</term> than with other metals; therefore, it is better to extract it from iron than from the others. From this reasoning, it follows that the spirits sublimated from iron are cleaner of earthiness than if they had been sublimated from any other metal. And this is confirmed because, as I said in Book 2, Chapter 2: The dregs <term>dregs: or "feces," the solid waste material left after a process</term> of iron are fixed—meaning earthy—and cannot rise during the sublimation; they hold the impurities of the spirits with them at the bottom of the flask <term>flask: or "boccia," a glass vessel used in distillation</term> so that they cannot rise along with the spirits. <margin>Dem.</margin> <note>Likely "Democritus," a common name for the student/interlocutor in these dialogues.</note> It seems to me that in the aforementioned book, in the chapter on the sublimation of mercury, there is a contradiction; for you said: *When you see the quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury in its liquid form</term> as white as snow and almost "dead" clinging to the sides of the aludel <term>aludel: a series of pear-shaped pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>, then repeat its sublimation without dregs.* You also said in Book 2, Chapter 2, when speaking of the dregs from which we must sublimate the spirits, that one must choose the material with which the spirits to be sublimated have the most in common, and with which they unite most deeply and intimately <term>intimately: the original uses "per minima," meaning mixed at the level of the smallest possible particles</term>. These things seem very contradictory to me: because earlier you said that mercury should be sublimated from dregs with which it has no affinity or agreement. And afterward, you say it should be sublimated without dregs. And then you say that the spirits should be sublimated from material and dregs with which they most agree and unite most deeply.

*Some would say that the first and the second sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling</term> were not one and the same, and that the first time one ought to sublimate with the dregs <term>dregs: also called "feces," these are the solid residues or impurities left at the bottom of a vessel</term>, and afterwards without dregs; but it is actually one and the same distillation <term>distillation: here used broadly to describe the purification of a substance through heat</term>. This is because our quicksilver <term>quicksilver: another name for liquid mercury</term> in distillation easily flees a flat surface, as I said in Book 1, Chapter 15. Iron has another property over other metals: for its mercurial spirit—that is, the prepared salt—will better preserve the sulfurous spirit <term>sulfurous spirit: the volatile, combustible principle in alchemy, often associated with color and reactivity</term> prepared from combustion than will the mercury of other metals. In Book 2, Chapter 15, I said that it is a property of prepared salts not to burn before fusion, and to preserve the sulfurous spirit mixed in from the combustion. ...[Isi]dore <note>The text likely refers to Isidore of Seville, a common authority in medieval manuscripts.</note> intends this to refer to our internal spirits. Vincent <note>Vincent of Beauvais, author of the 13th-century encyclopedia *Speculum Maius*.</note> in his *Mirror of Nature* <note>original: "naturale," referring to the *Speculum Naturale*</note>, in Book 7, Chapter 90, says: "The salt which is mixed with the filings of the body in the fire defends it so that it does not burn." By "filings of the body," he means prepared sulfur, which is like the finest filings of silver.

Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (Albert the Great), a renowned philosopher and scientist of the Middle Ages.</note> also, in Book 4 of *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine." abbreviated for *De Mineralibus*</note>, in Chapter 1, under the name of sulfur, provides a reason which applies to common iron and to its sulfur called Mars <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron, associated with the red planet and the god of war</term>, in this manner: We must observe this: that when placed in the fire, its smoke shows that the terrestrial <gloss>earthy</gloss> substance within it is very ignitable and combustible. Its stench shows that this substance is very undigested <note>original: "indigesta." Alchemists viewed the "ripening" of metals in the earth as a process of "digestion" or cooking by natural heat.</note> and unfinished; rather, by the corrupting heat, it is an undigested body rather than being made complete through digestion.

And this constitution of its makes it so that it can be the universal matter of all metals. For if it were complete in a determined constitution, then without a doubt it would not be convertible into others unless that state were first removed; but because of its incompleteness, it is convertible into all, like seeds and other things from which natural things are generated. And for this reason, wise nature abounds with sulfur in every place where there is the generation of metals. And because iron abounds in sulfur, therefore that is the proper place of the metals of the philosophers <term>metals of the philosophers: the refined, "true" versions of metals used to create the Philosopher's Stone</term>. ### OF GEBER.

...that iron is closer to the art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, specifically the Great Work of transmutation.</note> than gold, because iron is more undigested and indeterminate. However, since common gold is already digested <note>original: "digesto," meaning fully matured or "cooked" by nature's heat</note> and determined, it would require great effort to remove that determined complexion and make it incomplete and indeterminate; otherwise, it could not be converted into other things. This is the reason why the transmutation of metals is not achieved unless they are reduced to the primary matter <term>primary matter: *materia prima*, the original, formless substance from which all things are created</term>.

In another way, the same Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century Dominican friar and philosopher famous for his scientific writings.</note>, in Book 5 of *On Minerals* <note>original: "de mine." abbreviated for *De Mineralibus*</note>, Chapter 1, proves that iron is the true middle mineral <term>middle mineral: a substance that shares properties of two different categories, acting as a bridge</term> from which, through artifice, the most perfect extreme can be extracted, saying: "Everything which in some part shares the quality <note>original: "passione," meaning a property or state of being</note> of stones, and in part shares the property of metals, we say is a middle mineral; for it is the property of stones not to melt, and it is the property of metals to melt in dry heat. Therefore, stones belong to the category of dry things and of the earth, and metals belong to the category of moist things."

And because these properties coincide in iron, it is considered the true middle mineral. For it contains much earthiness and stony substance, due to which it does not melt as easily as other metals. Indeed, it is named "stone" by the philosophers because of its dominance [of stony qualities]; nevertheless, because it melts in a strong fire—as Albert says in Book 4 of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "methau." abbreviated for *Metaphysica*</note>, Treatise 4, Chapter 5—and possesses the weight and luster of metal, it shares in the metallic nature.

**Dem.** <note>Likely Demogorgon or a similar interlocutor in a dialogue format.</note> Albert, in the following chapter, attributes this property to marcasite <term>marcasite: in old alchemy, this often referred to iron pyrites or other metallic ores that resemble gold or silver but are brittle like stone</term>.

**Geb.** <note>Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of alchemy.</note> The philosophers, because of the similarity in the matter and no...

*...iron, they have named it the Paschal Virgin. They depict this substance in the form of a woman with her hair spread across her shoulders; at the top of her hair was the symbol of Mercury; in the middle was a half-moon <note>The symbol for silver.</note>; and at the very ends was the symbol of the Sun <note>The symbol for gold.</note>. And they intended to signify that just as hair is indigestible even though it is very thin, so too the most subtle radical parts of iron are incombustible and incorruptible; and within those parts are the gold, the silver, and the mercury of the philosophers. However, the mercury is lighter than the silver, and the silver is lighter than the sun.* <note>In alchemy, "lightness" often refers to volatility or the ease with which a substance can be changed by fire.</note> *The philosopher Rudianus says: "The beginning of this work is when the sun shall be in Aries." <margin>Dem.</margin> *I believe they speak of the first degree of fire, which must be light—that is, warm and moist, like the air in springtime. For Aristotle, in the Book of Secrets to Alexander <note>Referring to the 'Secretum Secretorum,' a popular medieval treatise falsely attributed to Aristotle.</note>, says: "Springtime begins when the sun enters the sign of Aries."*

<margin>Geb.</margin> *This interpretation does not displease me, but the intention of the philosophers was to say that the material principle of this work is Mars <note>Iron.</note>, because Aries is the house of Mars. And when the sun has entered Aries, it is always ascending through many degrees; but when it has entered Leo, it can no longer ascend—rather, it descends. This signifies that the sun <note>Gold.</note>, which is imperfect within Mars, can be made perfect through the art; but gold itself cannot rise further—rather, through calcination <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or reduce it to powder</term> or solution <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</term>, it descends and becomes imperfect. Rhazes also says in the Book of Divinity: "I say that this thing..."*

# OF GEBER.

Therefore, men who are born under the planet Mars are warlike and choleric. Iron is called "man" because it possesses a healthy soul, body, and spirit (as I said before), because it is pure in its root; it is young and strong, because it is hard and powerful. It is called the <term>microcosm</term> <note>original: "minor mondo." And the proof of this (as Raymond Lully and John of Rupescissa say) is the needle placed upon the mariner’s <term>lodestone</term> <note>A naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite, used as a primitive compass.</note>, which always turns toward the North Star as if toward its own kind. Have I not told you that Vulcan's net was made of steel? And that the Golden Fleece <note>original: "velo dell'Oro" (veil of gold), likely referring to the *Vellus Aureum* or Golden Fleece of mythology.</note>, the Bulls, and the Dragons were all dedicated to Mars?*

-># THE EXPOSITION<-
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: vi=</meta>

*...so diversely have they named this stone, so that the ignorant may not know it; for if they knew it, they would not sell it at such a cheap price as they do, according to the Assembly of Philosophers <note>original: "Turba de Philosophi." Referring to the 'Turba Philosophorum,' one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, which takes the form of a debate between ancient sages.</note>. "This stone which you seek is sold publicly and at a cheap price." Arnaldus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note> says: "The vegetable stone, nourished in the mountains; many who wander do not know it, but it is sold publicly and at a cheap price."*

**Dem.** You have said that our medicine is not made of vegetable things.

**Geb.** Iron is called "vegetable" not only because it is composed of green <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "uitreolo uerde." This refers to ferrous sulfate. Geber notes it "makes ink black" because vitriol reacted with tannins to create the standard writing ink of the era.</note>, but because by means of fire it is transmuted, and has the immediate power to be converted into the Elixir.

**Dem.** Previously you said that for this work, one does not have to spend anything on the weight of materials, because it is made of the most vile thing. All our philosophers affirm the same; Alphidius <note>An early Arabic alchemical authority frequently cited in medieval European manuscripts.</note> says: "This secret is not bought with a price and is found thrown in the street so that every poor man may have it." In the *Lily* <note>original: "Lilio." Likely referring to the alchemical text 'Lilium Medicinae' or the 'Rosarium' often associated with the flower metaphor.</note> it is written: "Know that the foundation of this art, for which many have perished, is one thing—more colored, stronger, and more sublime than all things to the philosophers, but to the ignorant it is the vilest of all things." In the *Turba Philosophorum*, it is just as the wise have said: that is, to the rich and to the poor, to the generous and to the miserly, to him who walks and to him who sits; for it is thrown in the streets and trampled in <term>dunghills</term> <note>original: "sterquilini." Manure or refuse heaps. This is a classic alchemical trope: the "Great Work" begins with waste or common matter that others despise.</note>.

**Geb.** I grant you that iron is reduced into the form of a stone vi[le...] <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ui=" which likely completes the word "vile" on the next page.</note>

<summary>Geber explains that the "philosopher's stone" is hidden in plain sight, often called "vegetable" or "vile" to mislead the unworthy. He identifies iron (linked to green vitriol) as the common, inexpensive starting material that possesses the potential to become the Elixir.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's stone, vegetable stone, green vitriol, iron, Turba Philosophorum, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Alphidius, Elixir, alchemy of the common</keywords>

->### OF GEBER ###<-
<sig>I ii</sig>

...most vile, and from it the Elixir is made. Of that stone Aristotle says to Alexander <note>original: "Aristot. ad Alessandro." Referring to the 'Secretum Secretorum,' a pseudo-Aristotelian treatise written as a letter to Alexander the Great, which was highly influential in medieval alchemy.</note>: "Take the vegetable, animal, and mineral stone, which is not a stone nor has the nature of a stone. And when you shall have the water from the air, and the air from the fire, and the fire from the earth, then you shall fully possess the art."

**Dem.** I well believe that these things are impossible.

**Geb.** The Philosophers, speaking metaphorically or by similitude, have not told a lie. Therefore Aristotle, in those words, teaches you the distillation and separation of the elements. When, therefore, in the distillation with a little fire, you have separated the water—that is, that <term>aquosity</term> <note>The watery, moist quality of a substance.</note>—from the air—that is, from that <term>mercurial water</term>—and afterward by bath <note>original: "per bagno." Likely referring to a *balneum Mariae* (bain-marie or water bath), a gentle heating method.</note>, you have separated the air from the fire—that is, from that subtle sulfur mentioned above called <term>arsenic</term> <note>In this context, "arsenic" refers to a volatile alchemical principle rather than just the modern chemical element.</note>—and the fire from the earth of the mercury, with the expression of fire, then you shall have the art of distillation and separation of the elements. I have spoken of this separation in the second book at chapter 12. Arnaldus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311).</note>, speaking of that stone, says: "It is a stone in likeness and touch, not in nature." Hali <note>Likely Khalid ibn Yazid, an early Umayyad prince and alchemist known to the West as Hali or Calid.</note> the philosopher says: "They are stones and are not stones, but we call them stones because of the likeness which they have to stones."

**Dem.** I grant that the medicine, when it is finished, has the form of a stone that appears vile to the <term>foolish</term> <note>original: "insipienti." Those lacking wisdom or spiritual insight.</note>, and yet it is not a stone; and therefore we should not say that the matter from which the Elixir is made is a stone in any way, unless we wished to say that every thing which is not consumed in the fire, or can be divided into the four elements, is no...

<summary>Geber explains that the "stone" of the philosophers is a metaphor for a substance that can be separated into the four elements (water, air, fire, earth) through distillation. He cites authorities like Aristotle, Arnaldus, and Hali to argue that while the Elixir may look like a common stone, its true nature is something entirely different.</summary>

->### CONCERNING GEBER ###<-

I iii

The <term>slag</term> <note>original: "loppa"; refers to the dross or scoria produced during the smelting and forging of iron.</note>, that is, the <term>lime of iron</term> <note>original: "calce del ferro"; a powder or scale produced by heating iron to a high temperature (calcination).</note>, which is generated in the fire by blacksmiths and thrown out into the streets and onto dung heaps: this is the most vile stone of the philosophers. <margin>Demogorgon</margin> How can this be the most virtuous stone of the philosophers, being burnt earth in which nothing virtuous can remain? The philosopher <term>Philodelphus</term> <note>A common pseudonym in alchemical literature; the name implies "Brotherly Love" or "Lover of Truth."</note> says: The radical moisture of metals, due to the homogeneity and strong union of its elements, does not separate nor is it consumed in the fire, because it is fixed and permanent within the fire. And although the aforementioned lime appears arid, dry, and deprived of humors, nevertheless, if you put it to the test, you will find all the things mentioned above within it. Of this lime I spoke in the second book, in chapter 7, saying: Therefore, the <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling.</note> of quicksilver is better when done from the lime than from anything else in the world, because it shares a common nature with it and has no <term>sulfureity</term> <note>original: "sulphureità"; an excess of "common" or impure sulfur that would contaminate the quicksilver.</note>. In some books the text says <term>talc</term> <note>original: "talco"; often used by alchemists to refer to a shining, flaky mineral believed to be a key ingredient.</note> and not lime; both names are suitable, but they carry different meanings. ...to see <note>The text begins with "dere," a fragment of the word "vedere" (to see).</note> from the first <term>lime</term> <note>original: "calce"; the powdery residue left after a substance is burned or oxidized through intense heat.</note>. And because I stated in the *Testament* <note>Refers to the *Testamentum*, a foundational alchemical text traditionally attributed to Geber or Raymond Lull.</note> that <term>calcination</term> is the shorter path to perfection, I have said that we must extract the quicksilver from the lime (that is, from iron), because its earthy parts do not agree with it. Furthermore, it has no combustible <term>sulfurity</term> <note>The sulfurous principle within a substance; "combustible" refers to the part that burns away, while "fixed" refers to the part that survives the fire.</note>, because that is consumed during calcination; however, the fixed sulfurity is not consumed in the fire, as I said in Book 3, Chapter 7. 

If by "lime" you wish to understand **salt**, I say that from this lime only "weak bodies" are sublimated—as I said in the "second cause of descent"—namely water and oil. Afterward, the quicksilver called **Jove** <note>original: "Ioue" (Jupiter); the alchemical and planetary name for the metal **tin**.</note> is extracted by washing from the burnt dregs, which do not agree with the quicksilver called **Jove**. This lime has no combustible or oily sulfurity, because we have separated it. From the first lime, we sublimate not only the weak bodies but also the fixed sulfur called **Mars** <note>original: "Marte"; the alchemical and planetary name for the metal **iron**.</note>. 

If the text mentions <term>talc</term> <note>original: "talco"; in this context, it refers to a white mineral residue or purified "earth."</note>, I meant the dregs—that is, the white earths of the aforementioned lime, namely salt and the quicksilver called **Saturn** <note>original: "Satur." (Saturnus); the alchemical and planetary name for the metal **lead**.</note>. Because the "earths" of the quicksilver of iron have no affinity or agreement with it (as I said above), it is better to sublimate and separate the quicksilver from iron than from all other things in the world. These also have no "unclean" sulfurity, unlike the quicksilver found in common lead and tin. # BY GEBER #

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> ...as I have said. He who has seen this knows by experience that it is necessary, and has recognized that when he has <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: "sublimato"; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again to purify it.</note> from something foreign to the nature of <term>sulfur</term> and <term>arsenic</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur and arsenic were considered the "spirits" or active principles that allowed metals to be transformed.</note>, he has sublimated in vain. It is, therefore, the intention regarding the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "feccie"; the impurities or sedimentary waste left over after a chemical process.</note> that the limes of metals be administered to them, because the work of sublimation is easy in those; there is, therefore, nothing else that can take their place. We do not say, however, that sublimation is impossible without the limes of the bodies, but that it is extremely difficult, and of very long labor and prolongation, even unto desperation. Johannes de Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan friar and alchemist also known as Jean de Roquetaillade, famous for his work on the "Quintessence."</note> meant this lime when he said he wished to teach how to "enchant" <note>original: "incantar"; here meaning to chemically disguise the metal's appearance.</note> gold and silver and reduce them into earth and lime, so that they could be carried openly in the hand in times of war without fear of thieves.

...not breakable, and this too is iron, and it is not good. **Geber.** According to the opinion of <term>Albertus Magnus</term> <note>original: "Alberto"; a 13th-century scholar and saint whose work "De Mineralibus" (On Minerals) was a foundational text for medieval and Renaissance alchemy.</note> in the third book of *On Minerals*, chapter 2, and chapter 5 of the second treatise: In metals there are two unctuosities, or oily moistures, of which one is extrinsic, thin, and flammable. The other is intrinsic, held in the roots of the metal so that it cannot be snatched away nor consumed by the fire, and this is not burnable nor combustible. But it is certain that this glass is the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>original: "humido radicale"; in medieval science, this was the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid within a substance that was necessary for its existence and resistant to destruction.</note> of the iron; therefore, it is incombustible, and is not totally deprived of moisture, although it is deprived of that almost extrinsic moisture and in-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformation of iron into a glass-like substance. In the preceding text, Demogorgon questioned how iron, once deprived of its "moisture" or mercury, could still be fusible. This page provides authoritative support from Albertus Magnus and Vincent of Beauvais to explain that this "glass" is actually the purest essential moisture of the metal, which survives the fire.</meta>

# OF GEBER #

...flammable. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on whether the purified substance remains combustible.</note> Albert <note>referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher who wrote extensively on alchemy and natural science.</note> in the aforementioned book at chapter 4, speaking of this glass, says: We deny that the matter which enters into the substance of glass is ash, but rather it is that purest **radical moisture** <note>original: "humido radicale"; in medieval science, this was the essential, life-sustaining fluid inherent in all things that allows them to remain flexible or fusible.</note>, which was intrinsic to that incinerated thing; which moisture, by the power of an incinerating fire, could not be extracted, but flows by means of the strongest fire, and this is the moisture which has suffered most greatly from the dryness. 

This is therefore the matter of liquefiable things and the **prime and remote matter** <note>original: "materia prima & remota"; the fundamental, underlying substance of all physical reality before it takes a specific form.</note>, one and common—that is, the moisture of this kind. The aforementioned Albert again, in the 4th book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "methau."; referring to Aristotle's Metaphysics, on which Albertus Magnus wrote famous commentaries.</note>, in treatise 2, chapter 9, says: Vehement heat gathers homogeneous parts and separates heterogeneous ones, because it dissolves the moisture and separates it from the earthy matter; and the moisture, being trapped by the fine earthy matter, cannot be totally separated, and therefore begins to distill with the fine earthy matter, and through the strongest fire it melts into glass; and then the coarse earthy matter, which held it strongly, burns away into **scale** <note>original: "scaglia"; the flaky dross or slag produced when metal is heated or forged.</note>. 

I also, in the 2nd book at chapter 13, have said that fire with the greatest force reduces matter into glass. It is therefore manifest that this glass is the radical part of iron. And because the coarse earthinesses which hindered the fusion in the iron are burned into scale, therefore that glass is fusible, because it has returned to its liquefiable nature. And because the pores are closed, the fire cannot penetrate and destroy it. Vincent <note>referring to Vincent of Beauvais (c. 1184–1264), author of the "Speculum Maius," a massive medieval encyclopedia.</note> in the *Natural History* <note>original: "nel naturale"; likely the *Speculum Naturale*.</note>, in the 7th book, speaking of the **philosopher's stone** <note>original: "pietra de philosophi"; the ultimate goal of alchemy, a substance capable of perfecting both metals and the human soul.</note>, says: "It is called stone not stone; stone because it is crushed, not stone..."

<summary>Geber cites Albertus Magnus and Vincent of Beauvais to argue that the glass-like state of purified iron is not mere ash, but the "radical moisture" of the metal. This essence survives the fire and represents the metal's most pure, fusible, and indestructible form.</summary>

# THE EXPOSITION

...through, <note>The catchword "tra" from the previous page suggests a continuation of a sentence such as "through which it melts."</note> because it melts, and flows in the fire without evaporation, like gold, and there is no other thing to which that property belongs.

And this is confirmed by **Roger Bacon** the Englishman* <note>original: "Georgio Baccone Anglico"; though the text says "Georgio," it almost certainly refers to the famous Franciscan philosopher Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), known for his works on alchemy.</note>*, saying: Of the qualities of all, I say that they must be **fixed** <term>fixed: "fissi"; in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to intense heat.</term> upon the fire—that is, that they do not flee from the fire, nor are they consumed, nor corrupted by the fire, and finally that they do not change color in the fire. Such things, therefore, are those which give the beginning to this work. **Alphidius*** <note>An Arabic alchemist often cited in Latin medieval texts, likely Al-Fidiyani.</note> *says: Fire enters into all bodies, substances, spirits, and stones: but it does not enter that stone, nor does it dominate it, nor do the **sulphurs*** <note>original: "solphi"; in the "mercury-sulphur theory" of the time, sulphur represented the principle of combustibility and heat.</note> *corrupt it. **Arnaldus*** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note> *in the letter to the King of Naples says: The more the said stone stays in the fire, the more it increases in virtue and goodness, which does not happen to other things, because all other things burn in the fire, but the said stone, all alone in the fire, always improves, and its goodness grows, and fire is the nourishment of that stone; and this is one of the evident signs by which to recognize this stone. **Avicenna*** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were foundational to Western science.</note> *in the book on the soul says: There are some who do not grant the mastery of alchemy, saying that from a thing whose nature is to not endure fire, one cannot extract a thing which endures fire. To whom we respond with the reasoning of **niter*** <note>original: "nitro"; likely referring to saltpeter or sodium carbonate, used here as an example of a mineral that transforms rather than disappears in heat.</note>*, because the earth certainly quickly...*

<summary>This page features a dialogue where Geber argues that the "Stone" must be inherently fire-resistant from its very beginning, citing authorities like Roger Bacon, Arnaldus de Villa Nova, and Avicenna to prove that the substance is perfected rather than destroyed by heat.</summary>

removes from the fire, but while it is touched by the fire it becomes stronger, because fire has no power over nitre. <note>In this context, "nitre" likely refers to a specific alchemical salt or borax rather than modern saltpeter, as Geber argues it is fire-resistant.</note>

Dem. *Saltpeter is very combustible.* <note>Democritus points out the common knowledge that saltpeter (salnitro) fuels fire.</note>

Geb. *By nitre, **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were foundational to Western alchemy.</note> means the aforementioned nitre, through the figure of speech called antithesis, changing the first letter, as one might say **olli** for **illi**.* <note>original: "olli per illi"; these are archaic Latin forms of the word "those." Geber is arguing that Avicenna is using a linguistic substitution or "antithesis" to hide the true name of the substance.</note> Avicenna also says in his epistle to **Arsen the philosopher**: <note>Likely referring to Aris or Aristeus, a recurring figure in alchemical dialogues.</note> The place in the East where precious stones are found is close to the sun; so also the nitre, the more it is touched by the heat of the sun, the more it is worth. Therefore, the mineral stone which best sustains the fires and other labors is worth the most. **Albertus Magnus**, in the 4th book of the *Meteorologica*, treatise 3, chapter 16, says: Although some things are corruptible by fire, they are not however **combustible**, because their moisture is not the fuel of the fire—such as stones and iron—but some things which have such humors, due to the narrowness of their pores, do not give entrance to the fire, and therefore they do not burn a second time. And he means the aforementioned **glass**. <term>glass: "uetro"; in alchemy, the "glassy" state represents a substance that has been purified and "fixed" so that it is no longer volatile or combustible.</term> I also, at the end of the proemium of the *Book of Investigation*, <note>Geber refers to his own "De Investigatione Perfectionis," a core medieval alchemical text.</note> and in the proemium of the 2nd book, have said that glass is one of the things which help the preparation. This is because by reducing iron to the nature of glass, it is prepared, because many earthy impurities fall away. And in the 3rd book, chapter 7, I said: If we then say that bodies are cleansed by **calcination**, <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to oxidize it or remove volatile impurities.</term> understand this certainly to mean the removal of the earthy substance which is not united at the root. I meant this glass when, in the 1st book, speaking of **sulfur**, <note>original: "solpho"; sulfur was considered one of the two primary principles of metals (the other being mercury).</note> I said: Some, because they have looked more deeply into it, have cleansed it, and...

<summary>Geber argues that the "nitre" of the philosophers is fire-resistant, unlike common saltpeter, and explains how substances like iron can be purified through vitrification (turning into a glass-like state) and calcination.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Avicenna, Albertus Magnus, nitre, saltpeter, glass, calcination, sulfur, alchemy, fire-resistance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Geber and Democritus. The previous page established that certain fire-resistant substances (referred to as "nitre") gain strength through heat. Here, Geber moves the discussion to the nature of "glass"—not as a common household material, but as a specialized alchemical "metallic glass" or "calx" used to fuse and transform metals.</meta>

## THE EXPOSITION

...they have removed the flight <note>original: *fuga*; in alchemical terms, this refers to volatility, or the tendency of a substance to evaporate when heated.</note> and the burning <note>original: *adustione*; the process of being scorched or parched by fire.</note>, and it is made fixed and earthy, and through fire it does not yield a good fusion, except for the vitrifying kind.

**Dem.** Perhaps when the philosophers spoke of glass, they meant common glass.

**Geb.** Common glass does not have such humors, nor sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: *arg. uiuo* (argentum vivum), the alchemical name for mercury.</note> suitable for metals. Therefore **Rhazes** <note>original: *Rasi*; Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist whose works were foundational to the field.</note> says: The ancients have made metallic glass. And I, in the first book, chapter 11, have said: The philosophers make glass from metallic bodies. **Rhazes** also says in the *Book of Divinity*: Others have said that the tincture <note>A potent alchemical substance capable of transmuting or "dyeing" base metals into gold or silver.</note> is from glass and not from anything else, and it is the greater stone, base, and found famous. He also says in the *Book of Alums*: *Glass liquefies iron and all bodies and makes them run in fusion.* And I, in the second book, chapter 8, have said: It is a property of glass to melt everything, and to convert them to itself. In the *Testament* in the chapter on Mars <note>In alchemical symbolism, Mars represents the metal iron.</note>, I have said that the calx <note>original: *calce*; the powdery substance produced by heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures (calcination).</note> makes stones melt. For this reason, some make certain gems with the help of this glass.

**Dem.** When you said above that glass makes meat cook quickly, you said that by "glass" the philosophers mean sulfur; how then do they mean that calx?

**Geb.** It is true that the philosophers often use equivocation <note>A deliberate use of ambiguous terms to hide secret processes from the uninitiated.</note>, but if you wish to know and distinguish which they speak of, you must consider the properties of the things, and you will easily know when they mean sulfur or the aforementioned calx. **Alphidius** <note>An early alchemical author, likely of Arabic origin, frequently cited in medieval texts.</note>, speaking of this glass, says: Break the glass and draw out the stone, and put it into the glass bottle, and draw out the oil, and you will find what the philosopher says. In this glass is the quicksilver which...

<summary>Geber explains that "philosopher's glass" is not common glass but a metallic derivative (calx) used to melt and transform other substances. He notes that alchemists often use the word "glass" as a code for different substances like sulfur, depending on the context.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Democritus, Rhazes, metallic glass, calx, sulfur, quicksilver, Alphidius, transmutation, alchemical terminology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Geber’s exposition on the nature of "metallic glass." Here, Geber cites more authorities to explain why glass is the ideal container and state for capturing volatile alchemical "spirits."</meta>

->### OF GEBER.<-

...overcomes the fire, and is not overcome by it; as I said in the 3rd book, at the end of chapter 6. **Isidore** <note>original: *Isidoro*; likely referring to Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an encyclopedist often cited in medieval scientific texts.</note>, speaking on this matter, used to say: **Quicksilver** <note>original: *arg. ui.* (argento vivo); mercury, the "living silver."</note> is best kept in glass vessels, because it pierces through other materials. In the 2nd book, chapter 6, I also said that only glass is similar to glass, because it has no pores; it is capable of retaining the spirits so they do not escape <note>original: *fuggino*; in alchemy, this refers to volatility, where a substance evaporates or "flees" when heated.</note> and are not destroyed by the fire. I meant that the spirits of iron always flee from intense fire until they are converted into the nature of glass the color of sapphire, or dark sky-blue. In the 4th book, chapter 18, I said that in prepared bodies we must create the sky-blue color, which is made perfect through the goodness of the **quicksilver**.

**Albertus Magnus** <note>original: *Alber. magno*; a 13th-century philosopher and theologian known for his work in the natural sciences.</note> also, shortly before the end of his *Metaphysics* <note>original: *methau.* (Metaphysica).</note>, secretly confirms the aforementioned things, saying: "It is not an easy thing to know when minerals have their specific forms and when they do not, because it is not an easy thing to know when it is true stone, or metal, unless through corruption it becomes very weak, such that only the shape remains, and nothing of the **virtue** <note>original: *uirtu*; the inherent power or characteristic quality of a substance.</note> which follows the **essence** <note>The fundamental nature or "soul" of the material.</note>."

And the likeness of this is in the bodies of the dead; for when they have been dead for a short time, they seem to have little difference from the living, but when they have been in the tombs for a long time, then only the shape is in them, and if they are touched, they immediately fall into ashes. And then there is a great difference between the bodies of the living and the dead; so it is also in vegetables and minerals. For minerals die in their own way, just as animals do, but one does not know when they have lost their being and nature, except by

<summary>Geber explains why glass is the only material capable of containing volatile alchemical "spirits" and cites Albertus Magnus to compare the "death" of minerals to the decay of human bodies.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Isidore of Seville, Quicksilver, Glass vessels, Albertus Magnus, Mineral essence, Volatility, Alchemical spirits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical dialogue between Demogorgon and Geber. The previous page discussed the nature of minerals and how "metallic glass" (specifically iron-based) can act as a vessel for spirits. This page concludes a complex theoretical statement about the "living" nature of minerals and introduces the mythological figure of Demogorgon as an allegory for iron.</meta>

# THE EXPLANATION

...a very great change; for the sapphire, while it still possesses the color, transparency, and shape of a sapphire, is as if it were "living," possessing the properties <note>original: *operationi*; refers to the active powers or medicinal/alchemical effects of the stone.</note> of a sapphire, and for this reason it is called a **living sapphire**. But after a very long change, it grows dark and loses its continuity, and then it is known not to be a true sapphire, but merely a **likeness of a sapphire**. And we must say the same of **living and dead gold**, and of **silver**, and of the other minerals.

**Dem.** I beg you, please do not let it trouble you to say a few words in explanation of the aforementioned long sentence.

**Geb.** In short, they mean to say that when iron has been in the fire for so long that it has become very weak and brittle, and no part of the nature or substance of iron remains in it, but it is instead changed into the nature of glass—of a color almost like a dark sapphire—then it will have been converted into the **Philosopher's Stone**. The ancient poets concealed these things by saying that **Demogorgon** <note>A mysterious primordial deity, often cited in Renaissance alchemy as the "father of all things" and the personification of the chaotic, raw materials of the earth.</note> was the great-grandfather of all the pagan gods. They said he is surrounded on all sides by darkness, mists, and gloom; that he walks in the midst of the bowels of the earth and stays hidden there, dressed in a certain pale green and a **despised moisture** <note>original: *humidita diſprezzata*; in alchemy, this refers to the common, base substances (like iron or mercury) that people normally ignore but which contain the "seed" of the Stone.</note>. He exhales an earthy, horrible, and foul odor; not generated by anyone, but eternal and the father of all things.

**Dem.** Although this seems like a hideous **chimera** <note>A mythological monster made of parts from different animals; here used to mean a confusing or nonsensical idea.</note> to me, nonetheless I would still like to hear the explanation of it.

**Geb.** **Demogorgon** in Greek means "God of the Earth," and "God of the People," or "Terrible God" <note>original: *Dio della terra, et Dio del popolo ouero Dio terribile*; the text provides multiple etymologies for the name: *Demos* (people), *Ge* (earth), and *Gorgos* (terrible).</note>; and it signifies **iron**, which is said to be the great-grandfather of all the pagan gods because they all derived from it (as I have said above) from...

<vocab>sapphire, living gold, silver, minerals, iron, philosopher's stone, Demogorgon, pagan gods</vocab>

<summary>Geber explains that iron, when processed by fire into a dark, glass-like substance, becomes the Philosopher's Stone. He uses the mythological figure of Demogorgon as a coded allegory for iron and the raw, earthy origins of all metals.</summary>
