# OF GEBER.

on every side he is surrounded by mists and gloom—that is, by earthly substance. He walks in the midst of the bowels of the earth, because beneath the earth he is generated and nourished. They say he is eternal and not generated by anyone, because from him every thing, according to some likeness, is generated; and therefore he is called the father of all things. Or he is called eternal because before the earth and other elements there was no **elemented thing** <note>A physical object composed of the four elements, as opposed to the primordial "Chaos" or "First Matter."</note>, or because he holds enclosed within himself the true, incorruptible **quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence," a purified substance beyond the four physical elements.</note>. He is clothed in a certain despised moisture, that is, rust, and a certain green paleness, that is, terrestrial and sulfurous **vitriol** <note>Green vitriol, or ferrous sulfate, often associated with the "Green Lion" in alchemical symbolism.</note>. He gives off a horrible and foul earthly odor—that is, sulfurous—and when placed in the fire, a foul water is made from it. To him (as Boccaccio says in the book *The Genealogy of the Gods of the Heathens* <note>original: *genealogia delli dei de gentili*; a famous 14th-century work by Giovanni Boccaccio.</note>), the ancients gave Eternity and Chaos as companions. 

Eternity is the quintessence, that is, the **radical moisture** <note>The innate, life-sustaining fluid thought to exist in all matter.</note>, or **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury</term>, which according to what Pliny says in book 33, is an eternal liquid. And this prepared quicksilver Claudian <note>A 4th-century Roman poet often cited by alchemists for his mythological descriptions.</note> names a serpent, which in its coagulation eats and devours its own tail—that is, the mercurial water distilled from the quicksilver itself in the form of a lion's tail and a serpent <note>This refers to the *Ouroboros*, the snake eating its own tail, symbolizing the cyclical nature of the alchemical process.</note>. 

**Chaos** is that earthy salt aforementioned as **Saturn** <note>In alchemy, Saturn typically represents lead, but here it refers to the raw, "confused" state of matter before it is refined.</note>. For it is confused matter, and in it everything is without form—namely the Sun, the Moon, the stars (that is, the aforementioned planets) which do not give their light—the four elements, and vegetable, animal, and mineral things. And as Claudian says, figures of diverse metals dwell with Demogorgon. And I have said above that in iron are all...

<summary>This page uses the mythological figure of Demogorgon and the concept of Chaos to describe the "First Matter" of alchemy, identifying it with minerals like iron and vitriol. It links classical literature (Boccaccio, Pliny, Claudian) to the chemical properties of mercury and sulfur.</summary>
<keywords>Demogorgon, Chaos, Quintessence, Quicksilver, Mercury, Saturn, Vitriol, Boccaccio, Pliny, Alchemical Symbolism, Radical Moisture, Ouroboros</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical explanation of the first matter of the Great Work, moving from the description of Demogorgon to the birth of his "son," the sulfurous principle identified as Mars or iron. ->THE EXPOSITION<-

of all the metals of the philosophers. The first son of **Demogorgon** <note>A primordial deity often representing the "First Matter" or the hidden soul of the earth in alchemical mythology.</note> was **Litigation** <note>original: *letigio*; meaning strife, discord, or contention. In alchemy, this refers to the violent or reactive state of substances during the initial stages of the work.</note>, that is, that **sulfur** <term>sulfur: not common brimstone, but the active, masculine principle that gives metals their color and hardness</term> called **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term> which, in the process of **putrefaction** <note>The stage of decomposition where the matter turns black and "dies" before being reborn.</note>, is the first to be born; it causes a tumult in the belly of **Chaos** <note>The confused, raw mass of mineral matter at the start of the experiment.</note>, that is, of that **salt** <term>salt: the third alchemical principle representing the body or physical stability of a substance</term>. For that salt is the first matter of metals, which they name *hyle* <note>original: *hylen*, from Greek *hylē*, meaning "wood" or "matter."</note>, meaning a "forest" <note>original: *sylua* (Latin: silva). Alchemists used the metaphor of a dense forest to describe the chaotic, unrefined state of the First Matter.</note>. It is so called because it is the vegetable part, and from it, through **distillation**, all the elements are born. And because the aforementioned sulfur would seek to separate itself—being different in nature because it has no salty parts—therefore, the belly of this Chaos having been opened (that is, dissolved in vinegar), Demogorgon extended his hand and pulled out Litigation: that is, that sulfur called iron. By the "hand of Demogorgon" is meant that other sulfur called **arsenic** <note>In this context, likely a reference to the volatile, "gripping" power of arsenical compounds used to refine or "capture" the metallic essence.</note>, because (as I said above) Mars rises by means of arsenic. 

And because, as I have said before, **Vulcan** <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing.</note> and Mars signify one and the same thing, just as they say Vulcan was cast into the Island of Lemnos as soon as he was born because he was ugly, they also say that this Litigation, as soon as it was born, was cast into the breezes—that is, into the **distilled waters**—because of its "sulfurousness" and its **burning oiliness** <note>original: *ontuosita adustiua*; a technical term for the combustible element in minerals that must be cleansed away.</note>. There it is washed and dissolved and "flies on high"—that is, into those waters. It could not go any lower because that arsenic which had pulled it out (that is, separated it from the belly of that salt) is lower than all things; for it remains in the center of the "earth" of that salt called Chaos.

**Dem.** <note>Likely the character "Demogorgon" or a "Democrat" figure in a philosophical dialogue.</note> This has been a long exposition, and because I cannot yet pass judgment on it, I remain content with this explanation until such time as I shall see the **practice** <note>The actual laboratory work or physical experiment, as opposed to the theoretical or allegorical explanation.</note>.

<summary>The text explains the alchemical birth of "Mars" (iron) from the primordial Chaos, using the myth of Vulcan to describe how sulfurous impurities are washed away in distilled waters. It identifies "the hand of Demogorgon" as arsenic, the agent used to extract the metallic soul from the raw mineral salt.</summary>

**Dem.** Why then did they not all live for a long time?

**Dem.** Therefore, all those first ones should have lived a long time, and yet Scripture mentions only a few.

**Dem.** Then everyone should have lived a long time, and yet this is not true.

# WOOD
<meta>Traces of ink from the reverse side are visible on this page.</meta>

->*the aid of some preservative medicine.*<-

**Ray.** The first people had the wood of life <note>original: "legno della uita"; a reference to the Tree of Life in the Garden of Eden</note>, which for a long time defended them from death.

**Dem.** As soon as they were cast out of the earthly paradise, they were deprived of that wood.

**Ray.** *I will tell you the cause of their long life. Adam was created full of knowledge, and had understanding of all natural things, and knew those things which could prolong human life; he used these himself, and afterwards taught them to some of his descendants, and those to some others; and therefore, the first people did not all live a long time, because this secret was not revealed to all.* <term>knowledge: the Italian "scientia" here refers to a divinely infused or philosophical understanding of the natural world.</term>

**Dem.** Therefore you, in whom all the sciences have been infused, teach me what this medicine is that Adam taught to some descendants, by which they lived nine hundred years.

**Ray.** *Preservative medicines must be very durable and removed from corruption (as I have written in the Book of the Secrets of Nature <note>original: "secreti della natura"; likely referring to the pseudo-Lullian "De secretis naturae," a famous alchemical text</note>). For since they must preserve the human body from corruption, it is necessary that they themselves be very durable; otherwise, they would cause even greater corruption. Therefore, wishing to preserve youth in the young and to restore radical moisture and natural heat in the old: we must choose the most incorruptible substance that exists beneath the lunar sphere, and prepare that as a medicine and a most sweet food, so that when taken by mouth it almost immediately penetrates throughout the whole human body and makes it almost incorruptible.* <term>radical moisture: in medieval medicine, this was the "oil" in the lamp of life; when it was consumed by the body's natural heat, the person died.</term> <term>lunar sphere: in the ancient world-view, everything below the moon was subject to decay, while everything above it was eternal. Ray is looking for the most stable element in our changing world.</term>

<summary>Raymond explains that the extreme longevity of Biblical figures was due to a secret medicinal knowledge granted to Adam, involving highly stable substances that preserve the body's vital heat and moisture.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Tree of Life, radical moisture, natural heat, preservative medicine, longevity, corruption, Ramon Llull, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Demogorgon and Raymond (Ramon Llull). Having discussed the "Wood of Life" and Adam's secret knowledge on the previous page, the speakers now debate the physical and philosophical possibility of a medicine that can prevent decay.</meta>

->**ON LIFE.**<-

->K iiii<-

**Dem.** Speaking with all due reverence: what you have just said seems impossible to me. For if all bodily things, created by God for human use, are either elements or composed of elements <note>original: "elementate"</note> and are therefore corruptible, where then shall this so-called "incorruptible" medicine be found? We also see that every generated thing is produced by its like, as the Philosopher <term>The Philosopher: A standard title for Aristotle, the ultimate authority on natural science in this period.</term> says in the seventh book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "Methaphi."</note>. Therefore, whatever part of the corruptible human flesh is lost or consumed must be regenerated and regained through another corruptible thing. We see this in the case of corruptible foods, which by virtue of our natural heat <term>natural heat: In medieval medicine, the internal fire or energy of the body that drives digestion and life.</term> are broken down and transformed within the stomach, the liver, and the limbs; by virtue of the nutritive power, they are converted into human flesh. If, then, that medicine is truly incorruptible, it cannot be transformed into human flesh—just as the matter of the heavens cannot be transformed into elemental matter.

**Ray.** You have spoken learnedly, but do you truly think that in things composed of elements there is nothing present but those same corruptible elements?

**Dem.** Of what other thing, then, are these elemental objects composed?

**Ray.** Of a subtle substance, or radical moisture <term>radical moisture: A vital, innate fluid thought to be the "fuel" of life; its depletion was believed to cause aging and death.</term>, which is intrinsic and called the fifth essence <note>original: "quinta essentia"; the quintessence, a pure substance beyond the four earthly elements.</note>. It is diffused through all the elemental parts, remaining simple and highly incorruptible, and it preserves things in their being for a long time. Wise Nature has ordered it so because she desires to preserve herself for a long time within individuals, and perpetually within the species. And although in the process of generation...

<summary>Demogorgon challenges Raymond’s claim of an incorruptible medicine, arguing from Aristotelian physics that human flesh requires corruptible food to sustain itself. Raymond responds by introducing the alchemical concept of the 'fifth essence' or 'radical moisture' as the hidden, enduring substance within all things.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Metaphysics, natural heat, radical moisture, fifth essence, quintessence, alchemy, corruption and generation, nutritive power</keywords>

# THE WOOD

A
<meta>catchword: gli</meta>

univocal generation <note>original: "uniuoca"; a philosophical term where the cause and effect are of the same nature, such as a human giving birth to a human.</note> of animals. Every animal generates its like in species; nonetheless, in generation that is subject to ceasing—which occurs through the decay <term>decay: originally "putrefattione" (putrefaction), which in alchemy and early medicine was seen as a necessary stage for transformation and new life.</term> and corruption of the generator—the thing generated is different from the generator in species, or genus. We see this in the case of foods, through which that which has been lost from human flesh is regenerated by the power of internal and external heat. And I do not say that this medicine is totally incorruptible like the Heavens, but because it is generated from matter more incorruptible than all others, and is made simple through the separation of the corruptible elements, it would last ten thousand years without corruption if it were properly preserved. Moreover, taken by mouth, it would for a long time preserve the human body in an incorruptible state. For this reason, physicians always urge the use of the least corruptible foods.

<margin>Dem.</margin> To me, it seems that this opinion of yours is similar to that of certain Philosophers who said that the Salamander lived on fire alone; and the Herring <note>original: "Alec"; while it means herring, in this context of elemental myths, it refers to a fish believed to live solely on the element of water.</note> on water alone; and the mole on earth alone; and the chameleon on air alone. This opinion is false, because no "elemented" thing <note>original: "elementata"; something composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note> lives on a single element, but lives on those things of which it is composed (as the Philosopher <term>The Philosopher: Aristotle.</term> says). Therefore, a man could not live on that radical moisture <term>radical moisture: The innate fluid or "oil" that fuels the lamp of life; medieval thinkers believed aging was the gradual drying out of this moisture.</term> alone, being simple and so incorruptible.

<margin>Ray.</margin> I do not say that man could live on that medicine alone, but by using it, along with moderation in other foods, he could reach nearly the age of the ancient fathers <note>The "ancient fathers" refers to the Biblical patriarchs, such as Methuselah, who were said to live for hundreds of years.</note>, who in the terrestrial paradise would have used not only the Wood of Life, but also the

<summary>Raymond argues that a purified medicine could preserve the human body for centuries, while Demogorgon counters using Aristotle's principle that complex beings cannot survive on a single, simple element.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Demogorgon and Raymond (Ramon Llull). Having established the possibility of an "incorruptible" medicine on the previous page, they now discuss its historical and mythological presence, identifying it as the ultimate "preservative" sought by patriarchs and hidden by ancient philosophers.</meta>

-># ON LIFE.<-

...other fruits.

**Demogorgon.** Did the first patriarchs <note>original: "primi padri"; referring to the long-lived biblical figures before the Flood</note> use this medicine?

**Raymond.** They must have used it to live for such a long time.

**Demogorgon.** Could they not have taken something better that would defend them from death?

**Raymond.** Speaking according to natural laws, this was the best medicine that existed in the whole world. For according to the opinion of the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> in the tenth book of the *Metaphysics*: "In every genus there is one which holds the primary rank in that category." And because this medicine is produced from the most incorruptible and most virtuous matter that exists under the Heavens, it therefore holds the first rank in the order of preservative medicines <term>preservative medicines: substances intended to maintain the body's "radical moisture" and vital heat to prevent aging and decay</term>.

**Demogorgon.** Then it can be nothing other than the Wood of Life.

**Raymond.** I have already told you that, according to the writings of Moses, they were deprived of that Wood <note>Referring to the expulsion from Eden</note>.

**Demogorgon.** Perhaps Moses—who in his youth was instructed in all the sciences of the Egyptians and had knowledge of this divine science (as Vincent says in the seventh book of the *Natural Mirror* <note>Vincent of Beauvais (c. 1184–1264), an encyclopedist who wrote the *Speculum Naturale*</note>)—spoke with some obscurity, just as the other Philosophers have done.

**Raymond.** I do not wish to believe more or less than what is appropriate.

**Demogorgon.** Perhaps it must be that herb with which Medea restored Aeson to youth, and with which Aesculapius <note>the Greek god of medicine and healing</note> raised those who were nearly dead.

**Raymond.** It is that medicine, but do not call it an herb.

**Demogorgon.** The ancients have hidden this science under poetic fables and have spoken through metaphors <note>original: "similitudine"</note>.

<summary>Demogorgon and Raymond discuss a supreme alchemical medicine, identifying it as the "Wood of Life" and suggesting that biblical figures and mythological healers like Medea used it, though ancient authors hid its true nature behind metaphors.</summary>

Having just discussed the "Wood of Life" mentioned by Moses and the long lives of the patriarchs, the characters now debate whether the alchemical medicine is literally a plant or if the term "vegetable" is a metaphor for its properties during the Great Work.</meta>

-># THE WOOD<-

**Demogorgon.** What kind of similarity is this?

**Raymond.** In the preparation of this medicine, at one point it becomes green like grass, and for that reason they have named it "vegetable" and "herb." For this cause, Metuendo <note>Possibly a corruption of a name from the Hermetic corpus or a specific disciple in the legendary lineage of Hermes Trismegistus</note>, a disciple of Hermes, said when speaking of this matter: "O, how much is it like the vegetables, because of its green color?"

**Demogorgon.** Could it not actually be a plant, since herbs are so virtuous and medicinal?

**Raymond.** This medicine could not be made from botanical things, nor from animals, nor from anything descending from them.

**Demogorgon.** What is the reason?

**Raymond.** Since it must be incorruptible <term>incorruptible: in alchemy, a substance that does not decay or break down into its constituent elements under the influence of heat or time</term> above every created thing, it is necessary that it be extracted from matter that is, above all else, alien to corruption.

**Demogorgon.** But you yourself said in the *Book of the Secrets of Nature* <note>original: "lib. de secreti della natura"; a title often attributed to the pseudo-Lullian alchemical corpus</note> that we must extract it from red wine. And John of Rupescissa <note>A 14th-century Franciscan who pioneered the use of "quintessence" (distilled alcohol) for medicinal alchemy</note> confirms the same. And others say it comes from Celandine <note>original: "Celidonia"; a yellow-flowered herb whose name and sap made it a favorite for alchemists seeking the "yellow" or "gold" stage</note>. And others from human blood.

<margin>C</margin>
**Raymond.** Do not let yourself be deceived, and do not believe the simple letter of the Philosophers in this science; because where they have spoken most openly, there they have spoken most obscurely—that is, through riddles, or else through metaphors.

**Demogorgon.** What metaphor have they used in this place?

**Raymond.** Senior the Philosopher <note>Refers to Zadith ben Hamuel, an 10th-century Arabic alchemist known in the Latin West as "Senior Zadith"</note> says: "This thing converts from color to color, from flavor to flavor, and from nature to nature; for this reason its names are multiplied." Mireris <note>likely a corruption of an alchemical authority name</note> the Philosopher says: "Why is it made red before it becomes white?" He answers: "Twice it becomes black, twice it becomes citrine <term>citrine: a pale, lemon-yellow color indicating a transition between the 'black' and 'red' stages of the work</term>, and twice it becomes red." Why, then, is it made red twice, as...

<summary>Raymond explains that the alchemical medicine is called "vegetable" only because it turns green during its preparation, warning that alchemical texts use metaphors to hide the true, incorruptible nature of the matter. He clarifies that despite mentions of wine, blood, or herbs in his own and others' writings, the literal meaning should not be followed.</summary>

This page transitions into a discussion on why minerals are more suitable than animals for the Great Work.</meta>

red wine and human blood—that is, after putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the alchemical stage where matter is broken down, often turning black, before it can be purified</term> and during distillation—and for that reason the ancients, speaking through metaphors, named it red wine, human blood, dragon's blood <note>original: "ſangue di drago"; a common alchemical term for a deep red resin or a specific red stage of the philosopher's stone</note>, and other similar things.

**Dem.** I would believe that since animals are more noble than any other inanimate thing, they would also possess more medicinal virtue.

**Ray.** The excellence of animals does not consist in the proportion and mixture of the elements, but consists in the soul. And because the aforementioned medicine is made of corporeal and material substance, for that reason certain minerals are better, whose composition is very hard and stronger than that of animals, whose physical composition is weak and easy to corrupt, and of little virtue. We see that animals, which are occupied with high and sublime things <note>referring to the intellectual and sensory functions of the soul</note>, are deprived of those which are vile and low. Therefore, some minerals which have no intellectual or sensory operation have more corporeal virtue than all the animals.

**Dem.** If this medicine cannot be made of vegetables, nor of animals, could it perhaps be made from middle minerals <note>original: "mezi minerali"; a class of substances in medieval alchemy that were neither stones nor metals, such as salts or vitriols</note>? Such as salts, alums, vitriols <term>vitriols: (originally 'attramenti') sulfate minerals used in making inks and acids, often associated with the color green or blue</term>, marcasites, antimonies, sulfurs, and common quicksilvers?

**Ray.** Not from these either.

**Dem.** Are they very durable?

**Ray.** They are not more virtuous than every other thing made of elements, nor are they incorruptible.

**Dem.** What corrupts these?

**Ray.** A great fire.

**Dem.** Does fire corrupt and ruin everything?

# THE WOOD

**Ray.** It does not, however, corrupt the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the essential, life-sustaining fluid believed by alchemists to exist within all matter, providing its structural integrity</term> of metals, nor their complexional <note>original: "compleßionale"; referring to the "complexion" or the specific balance of primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that defines a substance</note> and radical heat. As Geber <note>Geber: the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note> asserts at the end of the second book, and Albert <note>Albert: referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher who wrote extensively on minerals</note> in the third book on minerals, chapter 2.

**Dem.** For what reason?

**Ray.** Because of their homogeneity, and their very hard and very strong composition and binding, and because of the vaporous mixing, and the very long and very temperate decoction <term>decoction: a process of slow heating or "ripening" that occurs naturally within the earth over centuries</term> in the mine.

**Dem.** Therefore, you wish to conclude that this medicine must be made from the radical moisture and similarly radical heat of metals?

**Ray.** Let us look at the metals which for a thousand years have been underground among the antiquities of Rome, being yet uncorrupted and having suffered no harm; think then how long their radical moisture would last once purified, distilled, and separated from any gross and elemental corruptible part. For this reason Geber in the second book, chapter 12, said: "We see that a distilled thing becomes purer and is better protected from putrefaction."

**Dem.** From whence do you claim that metals derive such great virtue over all elemental things?

**Ray.** From Heaven.

**Dem.** Hard things do not receive celestial virtue <note>the spiritual or astrological influence emitted by the stars and planets</note>, because they are not obedient to the Heavens. We see that a seal does not imprint its image in hard stone, but in soft wax.

**Ray.** The rays of the celestial bodies unite <note>the text breaks here; "uni" is the beginning of the word "uniscono" (unite)</note>

<summary>Raymond argues that metals are the ideal source for the alchemical medicine because their "radical moisture" is incredibly durable, citing ancient Roman artifacts as proof. He further asserts that metals possess superior virtues because they are directly influenced by the Heavens.</summary>
<keywords>radical moisture, Geber, Albertus Magnus, decoction, Roman antiquities, distillation, celestial virtue, alchemy of metals</keywords>

# ON LIFE.

...[grow] <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "nascono" (are born) or "crescono" (grow)</note> with as much power and strength as in the earth, because the earth is the proper and firm vessel for celestial virtues and the center of their spheres; therefore, it is productive of wonderful things: namely, vegetables, animals, and minerals. Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus: the legendary figure to whom the foundation of alchemy and the "Hermetic" tradition is attributed</note> said that the mother of metal is the earth, which carries that metal in her womb. And that the earth was the mother of metals, and the Heaven the father. And that the earth was impregnated by the heaven in the wild mountains, plains, waters, and in all other places. It is true that while the earth remains in its hardness it cannot produce anything, because it is not obedient to the "movers" <note>the "movers" refers to the celestial bodies or the spiritual forces of the planetary spheres that guide change on Earth</note>; but because of the continuous celestial motion, and the power of the sun and other planets, this earth is continually "cooked" <term>decoction: the alchemical process of maturation through heat, mimicking the natural ripening of minerals within the earth over centuries</term>, and thus it becomes refined and altered, and the subtlest parts rise as smoke by the power of the Sun. Therefore, the celestial virtues—which continually come from Heaven and penetrate the earth—finding the matter simple, very subtle, spiritual, incorruptible, shining, and clear, and prepared by the ingenuity of nature to be fit to receive the celestial virtues, are nobly infused into that double radical metallic smoke <note>original: "doppio fumo metallico radicale"; this refers to the traditional alchemical theory that metals are formed from the combination of two vapors—often identified as sulfur and mercury—rising within the earth's crust</note> because of the similarity they share. And since this spiritual matter has remained in that state of spirituality and celestial obedience for a very long time—because the substance of metallic principles is uniform and homogeneous, hardening over a very long time and over a thousand years, as Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan: a foundational authority in medieval alchemy</note> says in the first book, chapter seven—the Heavens have infused into it more and more wonderful virtues than into any other thing, because they have worked upon it longer. This page explores how to release that trapped celestial power to create a restorative medicine, citing biblical figures like Noah and medieval authorities like Vincent of Beauvais and Albertus Magnus.</meta>

<header>THE WOOD</header>

*...of the greatest power of metals above any other created thing <note>The Italian "elementata" refers to anything composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>. It is true that after a long time, this double smoke and spiritual vapor having reached the hardness of metal—even though it retains celestial virtues—nonetheless, being hindered and suffocated by earthliness and hardness, it cannot demonstrate those virtues outwardly. But if we wish to bring those celestial virtues to the light, we must remove the earthliness and density of the metal and reduce it to its first simplicity. Once it is sufficiently refined <term>decoction: the alchemical process of maturing or "cooking" a substance to its essence through steady heat</term>, it shows such power that it almost immediately transforms human bodies and restores them to true health. Consider what power this medicine has! For Noah, who had knowledge of this science (as Vincent says in his "Natural Mirror" <note>original: "Vincen. nel naturale"; referring to the *Speculum Naturale* by Vincent of Beauvais, a massive 13th-century encyclopedia of natural history</note> in the 7th book), being five hundred years old, fathered Shem, Ham, and Japheth.*

<margin>Dem.</margin> *What you have just said pleases me greatly. But would it not be better to make the aforementioned medicine from precious stones rather than from metals?*

<margin>Ray.</margin> *For what reason?*

<margin>Dem.</margin> *Because they are very incorruptible and highly medicinal, and they possess more celestial power than metals. Therefore (as Albertus cites in his book on minerals <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a dominant medieval scholar who wrote *De Mineralibus*, a study on the properties and origins of stones and metals</note>), Hermes and his followers said that all the virtues of lower things are first found in the stars and celestial images. And they said these virtues descend into natural things, both nobly and ignobly. Nobly, when the materials receiving these celestial virtues are more similar to the celestial bodies in their light and clarity. Ignobly, when the materials are confused and full of dregs <note>The Italian "feculenti" implies something thick, murky, or sediment-heavy, the opposite of the "clear" heavens.</note>, in which...*

It also introduces a debate on whether precious stones, due to their clarity and resemblance to the stars, are superior to metals for this purpose.</summary>

### ON LIFE

...the celestial virtue is almost oppressed. Since gems, in their substance, light, clarity, and colors, are more like the celestial bodies than metals—for which reason some philosophers call them "elemental stars" <note>original: "stelle elementali"</note>—they receive celestial virtues more nobly than metals and ought to be superior for creating this powerful medicine.

<margin>Ray.</margin> *I admit that if we were to convert and prepare their entire substance into medicine, some gems would be better than metals. The virtue of metals is suffocated by earthiness and other qualities that affect the "radical matter" <term>radical matter: the fundamental, original substance of a thing before it takes its final form</term> where the celestial virtues reside. However, as I have said before, neither metals nor stones receive these celestial virtues once they have already become solid metal or stone. They receive them only while they are in the form of vapors, before they harden. Because the vapors that create metals remain in that spiritual state—obedient to celestial influence—longer than the vapors that create gems, metals actually contain more celestial virtue than gems.*

<margin>Dem.</margin> How can we be certain of this?

<margin>Ray.</margin> *In their substance, gems are very close to the basic elements; their prime matter and elemental qualities have been altered or transformed very little from their original nature. With very little effort, nature has coagulated and hardened them, preserving the transparency and clarity they possessed when they were still a smoky "prime matter" <term>prime matter: the formless base of all physical reality in alchemical theory</term>. Therefore, even though they are hard, and their virtues, because of this hardness, are...*

<summary>Raymund explains that while gems look like "elemental stars," metals are actually superior for medicine because their vapors remain in a receptive, spiritual state longer during their formation, allowing them to absorb more celestial influence.</summary>
<keywords>celestial virtue, gems, metals, elemental stars, alchemy, radical matter, prime matter, vapors, transparency</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Raymund and Demogorgon regarding the nature of stones and metals. Raymund is explaining why metals, despite their density, are more spiritually potent than stones for alchemical medicine due to the long process of their formation.</meta>

# THE WOOD

...somewhat bound, they nevertheless still demonstrate some celestial virtue. This does not happen to metals, because before the metallic form is introduced into them, they require a very long transmutation, a balancing of elements, and the purification and transformation of sulfur <note>original: "solpho"</note>, quicksilver <note>original: "argento uiuo"</note>, salts, and alums, and a very long decoction <term>decoction: a process of heating or "cooking" a substance to extract its essence or ripen it</term> of these things. 

And if stones are very durable, it is not due to a high quantity of viscous radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid thought to exist within all matter, acting as the "glue" of existence</term> which tightly binds their parts together—for they possess very little of it and are easily crushed—but rather, it is because they are coagulated by cold. In this, nature has expended little effort. Because this cold coagulation closes the pores and locks the natural heat inside, the heat of the external air cannot open or corrupt them. However, the heat of a vehement fire—which, though it may corrupt some metals—cannot corrupt their radical moisture, because it is most strongly mixed with the subtle, terrestrial dry element and is fully "digested" <note>in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the state of being matured or refined through gentle, consistent heat</note>. These principles are named by the ancients as sulfur and quicksilver. With these two, therefore, the aforementioned medicine is made, being incorruptible and virtuous above all others.

<margin>Dem.</margin> Marsilio Ficino <note>a famous 15th-century Florentine philosopher and priest who sought to reconcile Platonic philosophy with Christianity</note> says that it is totally false to believe that the matter of gold enters into the composition of the human body, or that it resembles it, or that it can be converted into a drinkable substance <note>referring to "potable gold" or *aurum potabile*, a legendary alchemical elixir intended to grant longevity</note>. This is because it would first need to be transformed into blood and then into flesh, which is impossible due to its extreme hardness. Therefore, if this medicine cannot be made of gold, even less can it be made...

<summary>Raymund explains that metals are superior to stones for medicine because their "radical moisture" is more thoroughly refined and protected by a long natural process. Demogorgon counters by citing Marsilio Ficino's skepticism regarding the possibility of creating "potable gold" for human consumption.</summary>

# CONCERNING LIFE.
L

...make from other metals.

<margin>Raymund</margin> It is true that metals existing in the substance of metals—even if they were refined with the water of life <note>original: "acqua uite," a term for concentrated alcohol or a potent solvent used to extract essences</note>—would have no affinity or close connection with human flesh. However, once their first hard form is destroyed <note>original: "corrotta," meaning the physical structure is broken down to release the inner essence</note> and they are reduced to their primary matter, sufficiently purified and digested <note>in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the maturation of a substance through consistent, gentle heat</note>, they would then have that affinity and connection with the human body that other foods or preservative medicines have, though this medicine would be much more virtuous than the others.

<margin>Demogorgon</margin> From what you have said, I understand that you wish to dissolve and distill this radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid thought to exist within all matter</term> and primary matter.

<margin>Demogorgon</margin> How can I make medicine for human bodies from metallic waters, since they are most foul-smelling, horrible, corrosive, and so sharp that the Philosophers <term>Phi.: an abbreviation for Philosophers, specifically the "Natural Philosophers" or alchemists</term> say they are the worst poison? Morienus Romanus <note>a legendary 12th-century alchemist said to have lived as a hermit in Jerusalem</note>, speaking of that odor, said: *This is an odor which resembles the smell of the sepulchers in which the dead are buried.* We see that the waters of the baths have a certain harshness because, passing through underground and mineral places, they draw with them some mineral part and metallic odor; therefore, that metallic substance converted into water will be even more horrible.

<margin>Raymund</margin> Just as fruits at the beginning of summer are sour and astringent because they have not had sufficient digestion and decoction <term>decoction: the process of "cooking" or heating a substance to ripen it or extract its essence</term>. And while they draw more and more new moisture from the earth, they cannot be sufficiently ripened and sweetened; but through the long heat of the entire summer...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Raymund and Demogorgon regarding the transformation of harsh metallic substances into a healing medicine. summer, they are decocted <note>the process of boiling or heating a substance to extract its essence</note> and digested; they become sweet and fragrant. So it happens to our medicine extracted from the earth of metals: for before sufficient digestion and decoction, it is foul and horrible, and while its vapors rise and fall during the decoction, it cannot be sufficiently sweetened; but once those vapors are coagulated <note>turned from a gas or liquid into a solid or semi-solid state</note> and sufficiently digested, it acquires a wonderful mildness and sweetness. Therefore Arnald in the *Rosary* <note>Arnald of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist; the *Rosary of the Philosophers* is a foundational alchemical text</note> says: *Sweeten the bitter, and you shall have the whole mastery.* <note>original: "Dolcifica lo amaro, & hauerai tutto il magisterio"</note> <term>mastery: also called the "magistery," this refers to the completed alchemical elixir or the successful mastery of the chemical process</term>

**Dem.** Is there any sign by which I may know when this medicine is finished?

**Ray.** Johannes de Rupescissa <note>a 14th-century Franciscan chemist known for his works on "quintessence"</note> and I also have said that by two signs one may know when this medicine is finished and perfect. The first: if the vessel in which this medicine is placed—being set in the corner of the house—shall, by a miracle and invisible bond, draw to itself all who enter and make them stand still around it, then it shall be finished. The second sign: if the said vessel, placed atop a tower, shall draw all the birds that smell the odor of the medicine and make them stop around it, then the medicine shall be finished.

**Dem.** What did Johannes de Rupescissa mean to signify by this?

**Ray.** By the tower is signified the furnace placed in the corner of the house, in which furnace the glass is placed where our medicine is cooked. By those who enter the house and by the flying birds are signified those spirits, or rather vapors, which by virtue of the heat fly and go up and down through the long neck of the flask; when these stop and rise no more, then the watery humidity <note>the excess "water" or volatile component of the substance that must be stabilized</note>

-># ON LIFE.<-
->L ii<-

...is consumed, and the matter is coagulated and almost sweetened, and the medicine is finished. And do not wonder at this understanding: for, according to the judgment of Rhazes <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and chemist whose works were foundational to both medicine and alchemy</note>, the ancients—in order to hide this divine science <note>referring here to alchemy, the "sacred art" of transformation</note>—used so many names, metaphors, fables, and riddles that a man could hardly, and only with great difficulty, find new ways to hide it as they did. Giovanni Pico della Mirandola <note>a renowned Renaissance philosopher (1463–1494) who sought to reconcile all schools of thought</note> also says at the end of his book *On the Dignity of Man*: that it was the custom of the ancient philosophers to describe all things divine, high, and sublime under the veil of many riddles and poetic fables.

<margin>Dem.</margin> This medicine that you have described, could it heal all curable infirmities?

<margin>Ray.</margin> I have said that this medicine holds the first rank in the order of medicines. And because there are various degrees found in medicines, and some extend more or less to heal different infirmities and to preserve the human body from corruption <note>the natural process of decay or disease</note>, this one is powerful and virtuous above all others to heal many infirmities and defend the human body from corruption. We see also that the subtle and formal substance, which is submerged in quantity and matter, cannot exercise its powers; but the more it is spiritual and formal, and separated from matter and quantity, the more it extends its powers to perform many effects. And because our medicine is composed of subtle spirits and simple matter—almost separated from all elemental matter—it can therefore extend itself to all curable infirmities without any hindrance. We know also that every...

<summary>Raymund explains that alchemical secrets were intentionally hidden behind metaphors by ancient philosophers like Rhazes and Pico della Mirandola, and asserts that the perfected "medicine" is superior to all others because of its spiritual, non-elemental nature.</summary>
<keywords>Rhazes, Pico della Mirandola, alchemy, divine science, universal medicine, corruption, subtle substance, elemental matter</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Raymund’s explanation of the alchemical medicine’s potency. He is expanding on the idea that the medicine is "spiritual and formal," explaining how its similarity to celestial bodies gives it universal healing power across both internal medicine and surgery.</meta>

# THE WOOD

*...an agent has the power to act according to the proximity and similarity it shares with the primary active force. The primary and universal active corporeal and simple force is the celestial body, which, as a universal cause, extends to all inferior things <note>In the cosmology of the time, "inferior things" refers to everything on Earth, beneath the moon’s sphere, which is subject to the influence of the "superior" heavens</note>. And because our medicine—through its subtlety, purity, and incorruptibility—possesses a great proximity and similarity to the celestial bodies above all other corporeal substances, the Philosophers call it "Heaven" and the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>also known as quintessence; the pure, fifth element that makes up the heavens, distinct from earth, air, fire, and water</note>. Therefore, above all inferior corporeal things, as a universal medicine, it will be able to heal all curable illnesses, pertaining not only to the <term>Physician</term> <note>original: "Phisico"; a doctor of internal medicine</note> but also to the <term>Surgeon</term> <note>original: "Cirugico"; historically, one who treated external wounds and performed manual operations</note>. Doctors also say that <term>Hiera Picra</term> <note>original: "Ierapigra"; a traditional bitter purgative made of aloes and other herbs, whose name literally means "sacred bitter"</note> has the singular power to draw the humors from the head, neck, and chest, and not from other lower members, because it is generated by the power of the stars which have influence over the head, neck, and chest—namely the stars of Aries, Taurus, and Gemini. And similarly, we could speak of other medicines that draw humors from the legs, knees, and feet, because they have received special influence and power from the stars of Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces. And because all metals receive their being, name, number, colors, powers, and properties from all the planets, and all the planets have influence and power over the entire human body, then our medicine, generated from metals, will have power over the entire human body. And if the waters of the baths <note>referring to natural thermal or mineral springs</note> have the power to heal many illnesses because they draw into themselves some metallic and mineral virtue, a much greater power will be held by...*

<summary>Raymund explains that the alchemical medicine acts as a "universal medicine" because it shares the pure nature of the heavens (the Fifth Essence). He relates medical efficacy to astrology, noting that just as certain herbs and metals are governed by specific zodiac signs and planets, this metal-derived medicine governs the whole human body.</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Essence, universal medicine, medical astrology, Hiera Picra, humors, planetary influence, metals, thermal baths</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Democritus and Raymund Lull. Raymund is concluding his argument that a single metallic substance, when refined into a potable form, acts as a universal medicine because it contains the celestial virtues of all planets and metals. This transitions into a debate on how one medicine can treat multiple, often opposite, diseases.</meta>

# ON LIFE

83

...this metallic substance converted into a potable substance. And because (according to the opinion of the ancient Philosophers) all metals—by way of their similarity, virtue, names, colors, and properties—are present within every other metal, as I have written in my exposition on **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath considered the father of Islamic alchemy</note>, it follows that our medicine, even if it were extracted from a single metal, would nevertheless possess within itself the virtues of all metals and planets. It would have the power over the entire human body to heal all curable infirmities. And this is what **John of Rupescissa** <note>Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366) was a Franciscan friar and alchemist famous for his theories on the "quintessence" or "water of life" extracted from wine</note> meant when, speaking of our **radical moisture** <note>the vital fluid thought to sustain life in medieval medicine</note> and <term>Fifth Essence</term> under the name of *water of life*, he said that good "burning water" <note>original: "acqua ardente"; usually refers to distilled alcohol, used here as a solvent for alchemical properties</note> has within it the virtues of all metals. And as the aforementioned says, our water is not "water of the vines," but is **water of life**; because it gives life to men <note>A play on words in the original Italian: "acqua de uiti" (water of the vines/wine) versus "acqua di uita" (water of life)</note>.

**Dem.** Although the reasons mentioned above are stated wisely, it nonetheless seems impossible to me that a single and simple medicine could heal all infirmities. And the reason is this: contrary infirmities (according to what physicians say) are cured with contrary medicines. And because contrary qualities cannot exist in a single subject, it does not seem possible to me that one medicine could heal so many infirmities. All philosophers also say that from a single thing, only one effect proceeds; therefore, a single medicine cannot heal more than one infirmity.

**Ray.** A single and simple thing produces many contrary effects, according to the diverse nature and disposition of the recipients. We see that the heat of the Sun performs contrary operations

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical theory that a "potable" metallic medicine contains the virtues of all planets and metals, making it a universal cure. It introduces a classic medical debate: how a single substance can heal different diseases that have opposing qualities.</summary>
<keywords>potable gold, Geber, Johannes de Rupescissa, Fifth Essence, quintessence, radical moisture, universal medicine, contrary qualities, alchemy, solar heat</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Democritus and Raymund Lull. The text continues from the previous page's discussion on how a single medicine can have multiple effects, just as a single celestial body can influence different terrestrial materials in various ways. The catchword "ni," from the previous page completes the word "nientedimanco" (nevertheless).</meta>

# THE WOOD

...less; <note>The text continues the word "nevertheless" from the previous page</note> for as the sun dries the mud and liquefies the wax, it is nonetheless a single operation and in itself possesses no contradiction at all. And although our medicine is but one, and perhaps possesses a single and proper operation, nonetheless if we wish to consider it—insofar as it holds a great similarity to all celestial causes, which are many—it is receptive to many celestial virtues. And because it is extracted from all the elements, we therefore say that our medicine possesses all elemental qualities in its virtue; it is manifold and able to cause many and varied contrary effects.

**Democritus.** Now tell me distinctly the practical method of this medicine.
**Raymund.** Read the exposition of **Geber** <note>original: "Geber"; referring to the works of the 8th-century alchemist Jabir ibn Hayyan</note>, for in that you will find the matter and the practice distinctly written.
**Democritus.** I thank you for such generosity. I commend myself to you.

# THE WOOD

...nor [is it any different than the sun], for it dries the mud and liquefies the wax; nevertheless, it is a single operation and in itself contains no contradiction. And although our <term>medicine: In alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher’s Stone, which was believed to "cure" base metals into gold and restore human health.</term> is one alone, and perhaps has but one single and proper operation, nevertheless if we wish to consider it—insofar as it has a great similarity with all the celestial causes, which are many—it is receptive to many celestial virtues. And because it is extracted from all the elements, we therefore say that our medicine possesses all elemental qualities in its power, and is manifold, and able to cause many and various contrary effects.

**Dem.** Now tell me clearly the <term>practice: The practical, laboratory application or experimental procedure, as opposed to the "theory."</term> of this medicine.
**Ray.** Read the exposition of Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a legendary figure in Islamic alchemy whose writings were foundational to European chemistry.</note>, for in that you will find the matter and the practice clearly written.

<meta>This page serves as the colophon and concluding dialogue for the treatise. ->THE WOOD<-

I hope that she <note>Likely referring to "Lady Wisdom" or the personification of the Alchemical Art</note> has taught it to you, because there is nothing that is not a single operation, existing in itself with one single thing. Regarding how much this thing of ours is one alone and performs a single and proper operation, we have considered this elsewhere. This is due to the great virtue <note>original: "virtù," meaning power or inherent strength</note> which she possesses over all causes and effects—which are many and flee from many things and effects. And because she is extracted from all the elements, we therefore say that our medicine <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Alchemical Elixir or Philosopher’s Stone, believed to cure both metals and the human body</note> must possess all elemental qualities and manifold effects, and have the power to cause many and various different results.

Dem. Now tell me clearly the practice of this medicine.
Ray. <note>Ray. refers to Raymond Llull, a major philosopher and purported alchemist</note> Read the exposition of Geber <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century chemist whose works were foundational to European alchemy</note>, for in that work you will find the matter and the practice written out distinctly.
Dem. I thank you for such great generosity, and I commend myself to you.

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page serves as the colophon and printer's register for a work concerning alchemical medicine and the "Wood of Life." It concludes a dialogue or exposition based on the teachings of Geber.</meta>

->**T H E   W O O D**<-

It seemed to me that he <unclear>spoke</unclear> to Italy, and I say, that this medicine
performs but a single operation within itself, by
an arcane art <note>Secret or hidden knowledge, typical of alchemical traditions</note>, as much as it is possible for medicine to perform 
only one, and that it be a single and proper operation,
and to favor and consider that operation as one. Because of 
the great subtlety which it possesses with all the 
celestial causes—which are many—it is superior to many 
earthly causes. And because it is extracted from all the elements, 
we therefore say our medicine is well-composed 
of elemental qualities and manifold effects, and is able 
to cause many and various contrary effects.

Questioner: Now tell me distinctly the practice of this remedy.
Response: Read the exposition of Geber <term>Geber: The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a famous Persian alchemist whose writings were foundational to European alchemy</term>, for in it you
will find the material, and the practice from the middle to the end, and the final result.
Questioner: I thank you for such great generosity. I commend myself to you.

### THE END OF THE EXPOSITION
### of Geber, and of the Wood of Life <note>Referring to the "Lignum Vitae," a term used in alchemy to describe a substance capable of prolonging life or the "Philosopher's Stone"</note>.
